STOCHASTIC CAUCHY INITIAL-VALUE FORMULATION OF THE HEAT EQUATION FOR RANDOM FIELD INITIAL DATA: SMOOTHING, HARNACK-TYPE BOUNDS AND p-MOMENTS
Steven D. Miller

Abstract
. The following stochastic Cauchy initial-value problem is studied for the parabolic heat equation on a domain 𝐐 ⊂ 𝐑 n \mathbf{Q}\subset{\mathbf{R}}^{n} with random field initial data. □ ​ u ⁡ ( x , t ) ^ \displaystyle{\square}\widehat{u(x,t)} ≡ ( ∂ ∂ t − 𝚫 x ) ​ u ⁡ ( x , t ) ^ = 0 , x ∈ 𝐐 , t > 0 \displaystyle\equiv\left(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}-\bm{\Delta}_{x}\right)\widehat{u(x,t)}=0,~x\in\mathbf{Q},t>0 u ⁡ ( x , 0 ) ^ = ϕ ⁡ ( x ) + 𝓙 ⁡ ( x ) , x ∈ 𝐐 , t = 0 \displaystyle\widehat{u(x,0)}=\phi(x)+\bm{\mathscr{J}}(x),~x\in\mathbf{Q},t=0 where ϕ ​ ( x ) ∈ C ∞ ​ ( 𝐐 ) \phi(x)\in C^{\infty}({\mathbf{Q}}) and 𝒥 ⁡ ( x ) \mathscr{J}(x) is a classical Gaussian random scalar field with expectation 𝔼 ​ ⟦ 𝓙 ⁡ ( x ) ⟧ = 0 \mathbb{E}\llbracket\boldsymbol{\mathscr{J}}(x)\rrbracket=0 and with a regulated covariance 𝔼 ⁡ ⟦ 𝒥 ⁡ ( x ) ⊗ 𝒥 ⁡ ( y ) ⟧ = ζ ​ J ​ ( x , y , ℓ ) \mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)\rrbracket=\zeta J(x,y;\ell) . The correlation length is ℓ \ell and 𝔼 ⁡ ⟦ 𝒥 ⁡ ( x ) ⊗ 𝒥 ⁡ ( x ) ⟧ = ζ < ∞ \mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x)\otimes\mathscr{J}(x)\rrbracket=\zeta<\infty . The randomly perturbed solution u ⁡ ( x , t ) ^ \widehat{u(x,t)} can be represented as stochastic convolution integral. This leads to stochastic extensions and versions of some classical results for the heat equation; in particular, a Li-Yau differential Harnack inequality 𝔼 ⁡ ⟦ | ∇ u ⁡ ( x , t ) ^ | 2 | | u ⁡ ( x , t ) ^ | 2 ⟧ − 𝔼 ⁡ ⟦ ∂ ∂ t ​ u ⁡ ( x , t ) ^ u ⁡ ( x , t ) ^ ⟧ ≤ 1 2 ​ n ​ 1 t \displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\frac{|\nabla\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}|}{|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}}\right\rrbracket-\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\frac{\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}\widehat{u(x,t)}}{\widehat{u(x,t)}}\right\rrbracket\leq\frac{1}{2}n\frac{1}{t} and a parabolic Harnack inequality 𝔼 ⁡ ⟦ | u ⁡ ( y , t 2 ) ^ | 2 ⟧ ≥ 𝔼 ⁡ ⟦ | u ⁡ ( x , t 1 ) ^ | 2 ⟧ ​ | t 1 t 2 | n ​ exp ⁡ ( − ‖ x − y ‖ 2 4 ​ | t 2 − t 1 | ) \mathbb{E}\left\llbracket|\widehat{u(y,t_{2})}|^{2}\right\rrbracket\geq\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t_{1})}|^{2}\right\rrbracket\left|\frac{t_{1}}{t_{2}}\right|^{n}\exp({-\tfrac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{4|t_{2}-t_{1}|}}) . Decay estimates and bounds for the volatility 𝔼 ​ ⟦ | u ⁡ ( x , t ) ^ | 2 ⟧ \mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}\rrbracket and p-moments 𝔼 ​ ⟦ | u ⁡ ( x , t ) ^ | p ⟧ \mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}\rrbracket are derived. Since lim t ↑ ∞ 𝔼 ⁡ ⟦ | u ⁡ ( x , t ) ^ | p ⟧ = 0 \lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}\rrbracket=0 , the Cauchy evolution of the randomly perturbed solution is stable since the heat equation smooths out or dissipates volatility induced by initial data randomness as t → ∞ t\rightarrow\infty .



1.
Introduction

There have been long-standing and efficacious interactions between probability theory, stochastic analysis, geometry and PDE, perhaps best exemplified by random motion or Brownian motion and stochastic analysis on manifolds
\mathbf{[1-7]}
. The well-known Feynman-Kac formula
\mathbf{[1-3,7]}
connects the heat equation with Brownian motion, and the heat equation and heat kernel in turn play crucial roles in geometric analysis and the Ricci flow
\mathbf{[8-11]}
. Other examples are the probabilistic proofs of the Gauss-Bonnet-Chern Theorem and the Atiyah-Singer Index Theorem
\mathbf{[1,12]}
and Driver-type integration by parts formulas
\mathbf{[13]}
. Within the theory of stochastic PDE, the parabolic heat equation has been studied quite extensively from the perspective of a stochastic or noisy heat source term
\mathbf{[14-18]}
. However, this paper is concerned with random Gaussian perturbations of the initial Cauchy data for the classical parabolic heat equation defined on an Euclidean domain. The initial data is then essentially a classical Gaussian random (scalar) field and there is a stochastic convolution integral solution. This leads to stochastic extensions and versions of some well known and classical results for the heat equation, including Harnack-type estimates and bounds. Moments and volatility can also be estimated and the heat equation is expected to smooth out the initial data randomness at
t=0
, as t increases.

Classical random fields correspond naturally to structures, and properties of systems, that are varying randomly in time and/or space. They have found many useful applications in mathematics and applied science: in the statistical theory or turbulence, in geoscience, medical science, engineering, imaging, computer graphics, statistical mechanics and statistics, biology and cosmology
\mathbf{[19-36]}
. Coupling random fields or noise to ODEs or PDEs is also a useful methodology in studying turbulence, chaos, random systems, pattern formation etc.
\mathbf{[19-35,37,38,39]}
The study of stochastic partial differential equations (SPDEs),arising from the coupling of random fields/noises to PDEs is also a rapidly growing area
\mathbf{[15,37,38,39]}
. Such SPDES can model the propagation of heat, diffusions or waves in random medias or randomly fluctuating medias. Many dynamical systems are affected or influenced by colored noise which is regulated.
\mathbf{[39-43]}
.

An important class of random fields are the Gaussian random scalar fields (GRSF) which are characterized only by the first and second moments. The GRSF can also be isotropic, homogenous and stationary. The details are made more precise in Appendix A, but the advantages of GRSF are:

(1)

GRSF have convenient mathematical properties which generally simplify calculations;indeed, many results can only be evaluated using Gaussian fields.

(2)

A GRSF can be classified purely by its first and second moments, and all high-order moments and cumulants can be ignored. If
\mathscr{J}(x)
is a time-independent GRSF existing for all

x\in{\mathbf{R}}^{n}
or
x\in\mathbf{Q}\subset{\mathbf{R}}^{n}
then

\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x)\rrbracket=0
and

(1.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)\rrbracket=\zeta J(x,y;\ell)

with a correlation length
\ell
. It is regulated if
\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x)\otimes\mathscr{J}(x)\rrbracket=\zeta<\infty

For a white-in-space Gaussian noise or random field
\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)\rrbracket=\zeta\delta^{n}(x-y)
and is unregulated. This paper utilises only regulated GRSFs.

(3)

Gaussian fields accurately describe many natural stochastic processes including Brownian motion.

(4)

A large superposition of non-Gaussian fields can approach a Gaussian field.

The following generic stochastic scenarios can arise for linear, quasi-linear and nonlinear PDEs subject to randomness or noise.

Proposition 1.1
.

Let
\mathbf{Q}\subset{\mathbf{R}}^{n}
be a domain or (compact) set with boundary
\partial\mathbf{Q}
and let
\boldsymbol{\mathscr{L}}
be a linear differential operator (usually elliptic)acting on a function or field
u(x,t)
defined on
\mathbf{Q}\times[0,T]
or
\mathbf{Q}\times\mathbf{R}^{+}
. The generic (deterministic)Cauchy initial value problem (CIVP) and boundary value problem (BVP) is then

(1.2)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathscr{L}}u(x,t)=f(x,t),~(x,t)\in\mathbf{Q}\times[0,T]

(1.3)

\displaystyle u(x,0)=\phi(x),~x\in\mathbf{Q},t=0

(1.4)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathscr{B}}u(x,t)=\Xi(x),~x\in\partial\mathbf{Q}

where
\boldsymbol{\mathscr{B}}
is a ’boundary operator’,
f(x,t)
is a source term,
\Xi(x)
is some function and
\phi(x)
is the initial Cauchy data with
\phi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbf{Q})
and at least
C^{2}(\mathbf{Q})
. Similarly, one can have a quasi-linear operator
\boldsymbol{\mathscr{Q}}
and a nonlinear operator
\boldsymbol{\mathscr{N}}
. Introducing randomness, fluctuations or noise leads to the following possibilities for generic random problems:

(1)

The random or stochastic CIVP with initial data
\phi(x)
having additive randomness, random perturbations or fluctuations such that

\displaystyle\widehat{\phi(x)}=\phi(x)+\mathscr{J}(x)

where
\mathscr{J}(x)
is a (Gaussian) random scalar field (GRSF).(Appendix A.) For multiplicative random perturbations

\displaystyle\widehat{\phi(x)}=\phi(x)\otimes\mathscr{J}(x)

(2)

The random boundary value problem with
\Xi(x)
having randomness, random perturbations or fluctuations such that
\widehat{\Xi(x)}=\Xi(x)+\mathscr{J}(x)
.

(3)

The random source problem such that the source term
f(x,t)
is subject to randomness, random perturbations or noise such that
\widehat{f(x,t)}=f(x,t)+\mathscr{J}(x,t)
, where
\mathscr{J}(x,t)
is a random field or noise in space and time; for example, a white noise or Wiener process. This is the usual approach to studying stochastic PDEs.

(4)

The random operator problem:
\boldsymbol{\mathscr{L}}
or
\boldsymbol{\mathscr{B}}
is random.

(5)

The random or stochastic geometry problem:the domain
\mathbf{Q}
is random.

(6)

Some combination of these conditions.

The stochastic heat equation is usually studied as a stochastic problem of the type (3), with either additive or multiplicative Gaussian noise
\mathbf{[15]}
, usually with white noise as a random source term. This is the prototype for a basic SPDE
\mathbf{[14-18]}
.

(1.5)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-{\Delta}\right)\widehat{u(x,t)}=\mathscr{W}(x,t),~~(x\in{\mathbf{R}}^{n},t>0)

(1.6)

\displaystyle\widehat{u(x,0)}=\phi(x),~~(x\in{\mathbf{R}}^{n},t=0)

where
\phi\in C^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{n}
and
\mathscr{W}(x)
is a space-time white noise on
{\mathbf{R}}^{n}\times[0,\infty)
. This is characterised as a (distribution-valued) centred Gaussian process with
\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{W}(x,t)\rrbracket
and covariance

(1.7)

\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{W}(x,t)\otimes\mathscr{W}(y,s)\rrbracket=\delta^{n}(x-y)\delta(t-s)

The SPDE has the ’mild solution’, which is a stochastic convolution integral of the form

\displaystyle\widehat{u(x,t)}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}{(4\pi|t-s|)^{-n/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{4|t-s|}\right)\otimes\mathscr{W}(y,s)dsd\mu_{n}(y)

\displaystyle+\int_{\Omega}{(4\pi t)^{-n/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{4|t-s|}\right)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

(1.8)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}h(x-y,t-s)\otimes\mathscr{W}(y,s)dsd\mu_{n}(y)+\int_{\Omega}h(x-y,t)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

where
h(x-y,t)={(4\pi t)^{-n/2}}\exp\left(-\tfrac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right)
is the heat kernel. If
\phi(x)=0
then

\displaystyle\widehat{u(x,t)}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}{(4\pi|t-s|)^{-n/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4|t-s|}\right)\otimes\mathscr{W}(y,s)dsd\mu_{n}(y)

(1.9)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}h(x-y,t-s)\otimes\mathscr{W}(y,s)dsd\mu_{n}(y)

This is also a centred Gaussian process, but its covariance function is somewhat more complicated. The space-time regularity properties of (1.8) are discussed in
\mathbf{[15]}
. Solutions to ordinary stochastic differential equations are in general
\gamma
-H¨older continuous (in time) for every
\gamma<1/2
but not for
\gamma
=1/2, in dimension n = 1, u as given by (1.8) is only ‘almost’ 1/4-Holder continuous in time and ‘almost’ 1/2-Holder continuous in space. The covariance can be estimated as

(1.10)

\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket\widehat{u(x,t)}\otimes\widehat{u(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})}\big\rrbracket=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{n}}h(x-y,t-s)h(x^{\prime}-y,t^{\prime}-s)dsd\mu_{n}(y)

so that
\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}\big\rrbracket=\lim_{x\rightarrow x^{\prime},t\rightarrow t^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket\widehat{u}(x,t)\otimes\widehat{u}(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})\big\rrbracket
. The variance or volatility is then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}\big\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}dsd\mu_{n}(y)\equiv\int_{0}^{t}\|(h(x-\bullet,t)\|_{L_{2}({\mathbf{R}}^{n}})^{2}ds

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}{8\pi|t-s|^{-n/2}}\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{n}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{2|t-s|}\right){2\pi|t-s|^{-n/2}}d\mu_{n}(y)ds

(1.11)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}8\pi|t-s|^{-n/2}ds

since
\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{n}}h(x-y,t)d\mu_{n}(y)=1
. The integral (1.11) converges only if
n=1
and diverges logarithmically for
n=2
. For
n=1
we have the estimate

(1.12)

\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket|\widehat{u(s,0)}-\widehat{u(t,0)}|^{2}\big\rrbracket\sim\int_{0}^{t}|t-s^{-1/2}\sim|t-s|^{1/2}

which can be compared to Standard Brownian motion for which
\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket|\mathscr{B}(s)-\mathscr{B}(t)|^{2}\big\rrbracket=|t-s|
. So the process
\widehat{u(x,t)}
, for any fixed
x
, is almost certainly
\gamma
-Holder continuous for
\gamma<1/4
but not for
\gamma=1/4
.

Remark 1.2
.

If
u(x,t)=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}h(x-y,t)d\mu_{n}(y)
is a solution of the homogenous deterministic heat equation
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\psi={\Delta}\psi
then

\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}u(x,t)=0
and
\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}|u(x,t)|^{2}=0
. However, the solution for the stochastic heat equation grows without bound for all
n\geq 1
since

(1.13)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}\big\rrbracket=\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\int_{0}^{t}8\pi|t-s|^{-1/2}ds=\infty

for any fixed
x
. This is because the stochastic forcing term pumps energy into the system faster than the deterministic evolution can dissipate it
\mathbf{[15]}
.

Instead of a random source or forcing term, one can consider a deterministic linear PDE like the heat equation and consider initial Cauchy data which is random or randomly perturbed; that is, the stochastic problem (1) of Proposition 1.1. Kamp de Feriet
\mathbf{[35]}
first studied the random heat equation for random Cauchy data. He considered the heat equation on an infinite rod
(-\infty,\infty)
with a random field or function at
x
at
t=0
. The initial-value problem is

(1.14)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\Delta\right)\widehat{u(x,t)},~~(x\in(-\infty,\infty),t>0)

(1.15)

\displaystyle\widehat{u(x,0)}=\mathscr{F}(x),~~(x\in(-\infty,\infty),t=0)

where
\mathscr{F}(x)
is a generic ’random function’. The solution is the stochastic convolution integral

(1.16)

\widehat{u(x,t)}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{(4\pi t)}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right)\otimes\mathscr{F}(y)dy

In this paper, the following similar stochastic Cauchy initial-value problem (SCIVP) is considered in much greater detail.

Proposition 1.3
.

Let
\mathbf{Q}\subset{\mathbf{R}}^{n}
be a bounded domain with boundary
\partial\Omega
. Let
\mathscr{J}(x)
be a Gaussian random scalar field (GRSF) defined with respect to a probability space
(\Omega,\bm{\mathfrak{F}},\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}})
and which exists for all
x\in\mathbf{R}^{n}
.(See Appendix A). The expectation is

\mathbb{E}\llbracket\bullet\rrbracket=\int_{\Omega}\bullet d\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}
such that
\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x)\rrbracket=0
. The covariance is regulated in that
\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)\rrbracket=\zeta J(x,y;\ell)
, where
\ell
is a correlation length and

\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)\rrbracket=\mu<\infty
. The SCIVP is an initial-value problem for the linear parabolic heat equation with random initial Cauchy data-that is, the initial data itself is a Gaussian random scalar field such that

(1.17)

\displaystyle\square u(x,t)=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-{\Delta}\right)u(x,t),~~(x\in\mathbf{Q},t>0)

(1.18)

\displaystyle\widehat{u(x,0)}=\phi(x)+\mathscr{J}(x),~~(x\in\mathbf{Q},t=0)

and possibly
\widehat{u(x,t)}=0
on
\partial{\mathbf{Q}}
. Here
\phi\in C^{2}(\mathbf{Q})
. Then the following can be studied:

(1)

The general solution
\widehat{u(x,t)}
, which can be expressed as a stochastic convolution integral over the heat kernel. And its average or expectation
\mathbb{E}\llbracket\widehat{u(x,t)}\rrbracket
.

(2)

Stochastic extensions of basic and well-known estimates and results for the heat equation.

(3)

Estimates for the decay or growth of the p-moments
\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}\rrbracket

and volatility
\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}\rrbracket
for
t\rightarrow\infty
.

(4)

The solution is stable and decays if the initial volatility is smoothed out so that

(1.19)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}\rrbracket=0,~~~\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}\rrbracket=0

or

(1.20)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}\leq\lambda,~~~\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}\leq\lambda

for some
\lambda>0
. This is equivalent to

(1.21)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}{\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}}[|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}=0]=1,~~~\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}{\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}}[|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}>0]=0

and

(1.22)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}{\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}}[|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}\leq\lambda]=1,~~~\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}{\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}}[|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}>\lambda]=0

(5)

The solution is unstable and grows without bound if

(1.23)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}=\infty,~~~\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}=\infty

or

(1.24)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}{\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}}[|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}=\infty]=1,~~~\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}{\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}}[|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}<\infty]=0

(6)

The solution blows up if for some finite
t_{B}\in[0,\infty)

(1.25)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\uparrow t_{B}}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}=\infty,~~\lim_{t\uparrow t_{B}}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}=\infty

or

(1.26)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\uparrow t_{B}}{\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}}[|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}=\infty]=1,~~~\lim_{t\uparrow t_{B}}{\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}}[|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}<\infty]=0

(7)

The inhomogenous problem with a source function can also be studied such that

(1.27)

\displaystyle\square\widehat{u(x,t)}=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\Delta\right)\widehat{u(x,t)}=f(x,t),~~(x\in\mathbf{Q},t>0)

(1.28)

\displaystyle\widehat{u(x,0)}=\phi(x)+\mathscr{J}(x),~~(x\in\mathbf{Q},t=0)

where
f\in C^{2}(\mathbf{Q}\times[0,\infty))
, and only the initial Cauchy data is randomly perturbed or is a GRSF.

(8)

Finally, the problem for multiplicative random perturbations of initial Cauchy data can be studied such that

(1.29)

\displaystyle\square\widehat{u(x,t)}=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-{\Delta}\right)\widehat{u(x,t)},~~(x\in\mathbf{Q},t>0)

(1.30)

\displaystyle\widehat{u(x,0)}=\phi(x)+\mathscr{J}(x),~~(x\in\mathbf{Q},t=0)

These various estimates are also evaluated explicitly when
\mathbf{Q}={\mathbf{L}}\subset\mathbf{Q}^{+}
, a segment on the real line;
\mathbf{Q}=\mathbf{B}_{R}(0)\subset\mathbf{R}^{3}
, and Euclidean ball of radius R and centre at the origin; and
\mathbf{Q}={\mathbf{S}}^{1}
a circle or ring.

Example 1.4
.

(
Initial temperature profile induced by a ’noisy’ ultrafast laser pulse
)
For a more practical potential application one could consider the heat equation in a finite one-dimensional medium
\mathbf{Q}=[0,L]
or semi-infinite slab geometry
\mathbf{Q}=[0,\infty)
along
z
, when the initial data is due to an ultrashort laser ’impulse from
t=0
to
t=t_{*}
with
|t_{*}|\ll 0
. For an ultrashort flat or step function pulse
t_{*}>0
but
t_{*}\sim 0
. If the medium has an absorption coefficient
\alpha
then a beam with radiant flux or intensity I decays exponentially within the medium as Beer’s Law so that
I(z)=I(0,t)\exp(-\alpha z)\equiv I\exp(-\alpha z)
. Absorbed laser energy is then (instantaneously) converted to heat from
t=0
to
t=t_{*}
during this impulse, but the heat equation then has no source term for
t>t_{*}
. The initial temperature profile through the slab then has the same Beer Law exponential decay so that
u(z,0)\sim I(z)
or
u(z,0)=\beta\exp(-\alpha z)
. The Cauchy initial value problem (CIVP) is then the homogeneous problem

(1.31)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z^{2}}\right)u(z,t)=0,z\in\mathbf{Q},t>t_{*}

(1.32)

\displaystyle u(z,0)=\phi(0)=\beta\exp(-\alpha z),~~z\in\mathbf{Q},t\in[0,t_{*}]

This is an ultrashort but flat or ’step function’ profile so that
I=I(0,t)=const.

for
t\leq t_{*}
and
I=I(0,t)=0
for
t>t_{*}
.

If the beam flux or intensity is subject to some intrinsic classical noise or (short-scale)
spatial random fluctuations along the beam axis then in vacuum

\widehat{I(z)}=I_{o}+b\mathscr{J}(z)

where
\mathscr{J}(z)
is a GRSF and
b
is a constant. The average intensity is then

\mathbb{E}\llbracket\widehat{I(x)}\rrbracket=I_{o}
and the correlations in intensity between any pair of points
(z,\overline{z})
on the beam axis is

(1.33)

\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(z)\otimes\mathscr{J}(\overline{z})\rrbracket=|I_{o}|^{2}+b^{2}\zeta J(z,\overline{z};\ell)

and
\ell
is a very short correlation length. Within the matter the intensity fluctuations are described by

\widehat{I(z)}=(I_{o}+b\mathscr{J}(z))\exp(-\alpha z)

Fluctautions in beam intensity will induced fluctuations in the initial temperature profile.
This scenario could then be described by a stochastic CIVP for a perturbed temperature
\widehat{u(x,t)}

(1.34)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z^{2}}\right)\widehat{u(z,t)}=0,z\in\mathbf{Q},t>t_{*}

(1.35)

\displaystyle~or~\widehat{u(z,0})=\widehat{\phi(0)}=(\beta+b\mathscr{J}(x))\exp(-\alpha z),~~z\in\mathbf{Q},t\in[0,t_{*}]

where
\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(z)\rrbracket=0
and

\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(z)\otimes\mathscr{J}(z^{\prime})\rrbracket=\zeta J(z,z^{\prime};\ell)
. The random field
\mathscr{J}(z)
could be a colored noise, which
occurs in laser physics and dynamical systems
\mathbf{[39-43]}
.

2.
Heat kernal and the Cauchy initial value problem–classical results and estimates

Some basic definitions and theorems are first given for the heat kernel
\mathbf{[9,44]}
..

Definition 2.1
.

The heat kernel can exist on any manifold
{\mathbf{M}}
and plays a central role in geometric analysis
\mathbf{[9]}
. On Euclidean space
{\mathbf{M}}={\mathbf{R}}^{n}
and the metric is

(2.1)

\displaystyle ds^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(dx^{j})^{2}=|d\bm{x}|^{2}

The fundamental solution or heat kernel is then
\mathbf{[9,44]}
.

(2.2)

h(x,t)={(4\pi t)^{-n/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{4t}\right),~(x\in{\mathbf{R}}^{n},t>0)

with
h(x,t)=0
for
t<0
. Then
\square u(x,t)=\left(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}-{\Delta}\right)h(x,t)=0
. At the origin
h(0,0)
is singular and normalized as
\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}h(x,t)d^{3}x=1
. As
t\rightarrow 0
,
h(x,t)
approximates the delta function on
{\mathbf{R}}^{n}
. Physically, the heat kernel describes temperature distribution in space and time arising from a point source at the origin.
h(x,t)
is also infinitely differentiable or smooth and has uniformly bounded derivatives of all orders. Specifically, the heat kernel has the following well-known properties:

(1)

h(x,t)
is singular at
(0,0)
.

(2)

For all
\lambda>0
,
\alpha^{n}h(x,t)(\alpha x,\alpha^{2}t)=h(x,t)
.

(3)

h(\bullet,t)\in L^{p}({\mathbf{R}}^{n})
for all
p\in[1,\infty)
, and for all
t>0
and
x\in{\mathbf{R}}^{n}
.

(2.3)

\displaystyle\|h(x-\bullet,t)\|_{L_{1}}=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}h(x-y,t)d\mu_{n}(y)=1

(2.4)

\displaystyle\|h(x-\bullet,t)\|_{L_{p}}=\left(\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}|h(x-y,t)|^{p}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/p}=\frac{1}{p^{n/2p}(4\pi t)^{{\frac{n}{2}(1-\frac{1}{p})}}}

(4)

The norm decays as
\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\|h(\bullet,t)\|_{L_{p}}=0
.

(5)

\square u(x,t)=\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}h(x,t)-{\Delta}h(x,t)=0
.

(6)

Smoothness.
h(x,t)\in C^{\infty}(\bm{\mathbf{R}}^{n+1}\char 92\relax(0,0))
and all derivatives to all orders are uniformly bounded.

(7)

Satisfies the Cauchy IVP

(2.5)

\displaystyle\square h(x,t)=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-{\Delta}\right)h(x,t)=0

with
h(x,0)=\delta^{n}(x)
.

(8)

Obeys Varadhan’s large deviation formula such that as
t\rightarrow 0

(2.6)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\uparrow 0}-4t\log h(x-y,t)=|d(x,y)|^{2}

on a Riemannian manifold with distance function
d(x,y)
which is

d(x,y)=\|x-y\|
on
{\mathbf{R}}^{n}
.

In Euclidean space
{\mathbf{R}}^{n}
, the heat kernel satisfies a fundamental double-sided Gaussian bound
\mathbf{[45]}
.

Theorem 2.2
.

If
h(x-y;t)
is the heat kernel for all
(x,y)\in{\mathbf{R}}^{n},t>0
then there exists constants
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}>0
such that
h(x-y;t)
satisfies the double-sided Gaussian bound.

(2.7)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{1}t^{-n/2}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Lambda_{1}t}\right)~\leq~h(x-y;t)~\leq~\Lambda_{2}t^{-n/2}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Lambda_{2}t}\right)

It follows that for
p=2
and
p\geq 2

(2.8)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{1}^{2}t^{-n}\exp\left(-\frac{2|x-y|^{2}}{\Lambda_{1}t}\right)~\leq~|h(x-y;t)|^{2}~\leq~\Lambda_{2}^{2}t^{-n}\exp\left(-2\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Lambda_{2}t}\right)

(2.9)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{1}^{p}t^{-pn/2}\exp\left(-\frac{2p|x-y|^{2}}{\Lambda_{1}t}\right)~\leq~|h(x-y;t)|^{p}~\leq~\Lambda_{2}^{p}t^{-pn/2}\exp\left(-2p\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Lambda_{2}t}\right)

The double-sided Gaussian bound will also hold for all
(x,y)
within any bounded domain
\mathbf{Q}\subset{\mathbf{R}}^{n}
.

Corollary 2.3
.

The gradient of the heat kernel satisfies the 2-sided (Faber-Krahn) bound
\mathbf{[46,47]}
.

\displaystyle\left|\Lambda_{1}t^{-n/2}\left(-\frac{2|x-y|}{\Lambda_{1}t}\right)\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Lambda_{1}t}\right)\right|\leq|{\nabla}h(x-y,t)|

(2.10)

\displaystyle\leq\left|\Lambda_{1}t^{-n/2}\left(-\frac{2|x-y|}{\Lambda_{2}t}\right)\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Lambda_{2}t}\right)\right|

On general manifolds, the heat kernel is relevant to many scenarios within differential geometry and geometric analysis and has been studied extensively
\mathbf{[8,9}
and references therein
\mathbf{]}
. The key question on general complete non-compact manifolds
{\mathbf{M}}
is: what geometric properties are sufficient to describe the long-time behaviour or decay of the heat kernel? Much is known about upper bounds. The works of Nash, Aronson, Varopoulos , Carlen, Kusuoka, Stroock and Davies, have elucidated an understanding that uniform upper bounds of the heat kernel are closely related to isoperimetric-type inequalities, including the Sobolev, Nash and the logarithmic Sobolev inequalities
\mathbf{[45-51]}
.

The heat kernel will satisfy the following estimate

(2.11)

\displaystyle h(x-t,t)\leq\psi(t)

where
\psi:{\mathbf{R}}^{+}\rightarrow{\mathbf{R}}^{+}
is a smooth monotonically decreasing function. If
\psi(t)=Ct^{-n/2}
then
h(x-y;t)\leq Ct^{-n/2}
. Carlen and Strook prove
\mathbf{[50]}
prove that the bound (2.11) is equivalent to the Nash inequality

(2.12)

\left(\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}|\psi|^{2}\right)^{1+\frac{2}{n}}\leq C\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}|{\nabla}\psi|^{2}\left(\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}|\psi|\right)^{4/n}

Varapoulos
\mathbf{[49]}
established that the inequality is also equivalent to a Sobolov inequality but only for
n>2
so that

(2.13)

\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}|{\nabla}\psi|^{2}\geq\left(\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}|\psi|^{2n/(n-2)}\right)^{(n-2)/2}

This inequality is somewhat close to the classical isoperimetric inequality for the area and volume of a bounded domain
\mathbf{Q}\subset{\mathbf{R}}^{n}
such that

(2.14)

area(\mathbf{Q})\geq C|v(\mathbf{Q})|^{(n-1)/n}

Lemma 2.4
.

The heat kernel has the following derivatives, which will prove useful

(2.15)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}h(x-y,t)=-\frac{2^{-n-2}(2nt-|y-x|^{2})\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right)}{\pi^{n/2}t^{\frac{n+4}{2}}}

(2.16)

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}h(x-y,t)\equiv\nabla_{x}h(x-y,t)=\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}\left(-\frac{|x-y|}{2t}\right)e^{-|x-y|^{2}/4t}

(2.17)

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}\equiv\Delta_{x}h(x-y,t)=-\frac{2^{-n-2}(2nt-|y-x|^{2})\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right)}{\pi^{n/2}t^{\frac{n+4}{2}}}

Then it can be seen that
{\square}h(x-y,t)=\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}h(x-y,t)-\nabla_{x}h(x-y,t)=0
as required.

Proposition 2.5
.

Given the double-sided Gaussian bounds on the heat kernal and its derivatives, the
double-sided integral bounds over all space follow as

\displaystyle\Lambda_{1}^{2}t^{-n}\int_{{\mathbf{R^{n}}}}\exp\left(-\frac{2|x-y|^{2}}{\Lambda_{1}t}\right)d\mu_{n}(y)~\leq~\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)

(2.18)

\displaystyle~\leq~\Lambda_{2}^{2}t^{-n}\int_{{\mathbf{R}^{n}}}\exp\left(-2\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Lambda_{2}t}\right)d\mu_{n}(y)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{1}^{2}t^{-n}\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\left(-\frac{2|x-y|}{\Lambda_{1}t}\right)^{2}e^{-\frac{2|x-y|^{2}}{\Lambda_{1}t}}d\mu_{n}(y)~\leq~\int_{\mathbf{Q}^{n}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)~

(2.19)

\displaystyle\leq~\Lambda_{2}^{2}t^{-n}\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\left(-\frac{2|x-y|}{\Lambda_{2}t}\right)^{2}\exp\left(-2\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Lambda_{2}t}\right)d\mu_{n}(y)

Proof.

These can be shown to hold for
n=1
at
x=0
and integrating over
\bm{\mathbf{R}}^{+}=[0,\infty)
. The double-sided bound (2.18) becomes

(2.20)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{1}^{2}t^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\frac{2|y|^{2}}{\Lambda_{1}t}\right)dy\leq\frac{1}{4\pi t}\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\frac{|y|^{2}}{2t}\right)dy\leq\Lambda_{2}^{2}t^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\frac{2|y|^{2}}{\Lambda_{2}t}\right)dy

Performing the integrals

(2.21)

\displaystyle\frac{\pi^{1/2}}{2^{3/2}}\Lambda_{1}^{5/2}t^{-1/2}\leq\frac{1}{2^{5/2}\pi^{1/2}}t^{-1/2}\leq\frac{\pi^{1/2}}{2^{3/2}}\Lambda_{2}^{5/2}t^{-1/2}

Redefining the constants

(2.22)

\displaystyle\overline{\Lambda}_{1}t^{-1/2}\leq\frac{1}{2^{5/2}\pi^{1/2}}t^{-1/2}\leq\overline{\Lambda}_{2}t^{-1/2}

so the double-sided integral bound is satisfied. Similarly, for (2.18) we find that

(2.23)

\displaystyle\overline{\Lambda}_{1}t^{-1/2}\leq\frac{3}{2^{9/2}\pi^{1/2}}t^{-1/2}\leq\overline{\Lambda}_{2}t^{-1/2}

∎

Definition 2.6
.

(\underline{Global~Initial~Value~Cauchy~Problem(GIVCP)})

The GIVCP of the homogeneous heat equation
\mathbf{[44]}
consists in finding a solution
u\in\mathrm{C}^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{n}\times(0,\infty))\bigcap\mathrm{C}({\mathbf{R}}^{3}\times(0,\infty))
such that

(2.24)

\displaystyle{\square}u(x,t)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-{\Delta}\right)u(x,t)=0,~(x\in{\mathbf{R}}^{n},t\in(0,\infty))

(2.25)

\displaystyle u(x,0)=\phi(x),~(x\in{\mathbf{Q}}^{n},t=0)

where
\phi\in\mathrm{C}^{2}({\mathbf{R}}^{n})
is the initial data at
t=0
.

Definition 2.7
.

(\underline{Initial~Value~Dirichlet-Cauchy~Problem(IVDCP)})

Let
{\mathbf{Q}}\subset{\mathbf{R}}^{n}
be an open bounded domain. The IVDCP of the homogeneous heat equation consists in finding a solution
\psi\in\mathrm{C}^{2}({\mathbf{Q}}\times(0,\infty))\bigcap\mathrm{C}({\mathbf{Q}}\times(0,\infty))
such that

(2.26)

\displaystyle{\square}u(x,t)

\displaystyle\equiv\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-{\Delta}\right)u(x,t)=0,~(x\in{\mathbf{Q}},t\in(0,\infty))

(2.27)

\displaystyle u(x,t)=b(x,t),~(x\in\partial{\mathbf{Q}},t>0)

(2.28)

\displaystyle u(x,0)=\phi(x),~(x\in{\mathbf{Q}},t=0)

where
\phi\in\mathrm{C}({\mathbf{R}}^{n})
is the initial data at
t=0
and
u=h\in\mathrm{C}(\partial\mathbf{Q}\times(0,\infty))
is the boundary condition.
The GCIVP has a unique solution.

Theorem 2.8
.

Given the initial value Cauchy problem for the homogenous heat equation

(2.29)

\displaystyle{\square}u(x,t)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-{\Delta}\right)u(x,t)=0,~(x\in\mathbf{R}^{n},t\in(0,\infty))

(2.30)

\displaystyle u(x,0)=\phi(x),~(x\in{\mathbf{R}}^{n},t=0)

and the fundamental solution
h(x,t)
which solves the heat equation away from the singularity
(0,0)
, then
h(x-y,t)
also solves the heat equation. Here,
\phi\in C^{2}({\mathbf{R}}^{n})
is a suitable smooth continuous function. The general (and unique) solution is the representation formula or convolution integral

\displaystyle u(x,t)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}h(x-y,t)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

(2.31)

\displaystyle={(4\pi t)^{-n/2}}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{4t}\right)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y),~(x\in\mathbf{R}^{n},t>0)

where
\int d\mu_{n}(x)\equiv\int d\mu_{n}(x)
The solution has the following properties [39]:

(1)

\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\|u(\bullet,t)-u(\bullet,0)\|_{L_{1}}=0
or

(2.32)

\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}(u(x,t)-u(x,0))d\mu_{n}(x)=0

(2)

’Mass conservation’

(2.33)

\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{n}}|u(x,t)|d\mu_{n}(x)=\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{n}}\phi(x)|d\mu_{n}(x)

(3)

Dissipation property

(2.34)

\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\|u(\bullet,t)\|_{L_{p}}=0

for all
p\in(1,\infty)
.

Proof.

Since
h(x-y,t)
solves the heat equation then

(2.35)

{\square}h(x-y,t)=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-{\Delta}_{x}\right)h(x-y,t)=0

Equation (2.31) then solves (2.29) if

{\square}u(x,t)=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u(x,t)-\Delta_{x}u(x,t)=0
so that

(2.36)

{\square}u(x,t)=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-{\Delta}\right)u(x,t)=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-{\Delta}_{x}\right)h(x-y,t)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)=0

The derivative can be taken under the integral since
h(x-y,t)
is smooth. The proof of (1) is standard and can be found in Evans
\mathbf{[44]}
. for example. The proof of (2) follows immediately from the convolution property and the Fubini-Tonelli Theorem so that

(2.37)

\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}u(x,t)d\mu_{n}(x)=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}u(x,t)\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}h(x-y,t)d\mu_{n}(y)=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}u(x,t)d\mu_{n}(x)

Finally, (3) follows from Young’s convolution inequality. For functions
(f,g)\in L_{r}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
with convolution
(f*g)
and
\tfrac{1}{q}+\tfrac{1}{r}=1+\tfrac{1}{p}
Young’s inequality is
\|f*g\|_{L_{p}}\leq\|f\|_{L_{r}}\|\phi\|_{L_{q}}
. Hence

\displaystyle\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\left\|\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{n}}h(x-\bullet,t)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right\|_{L_{p}}\leq\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\|\phi(\bullet)\|_{L_{r}}\|\|h(x-\bullet,t)\|_{L_{q}}

(2.38)

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\|\phi(\bullet)\|_{L_{r}}\frac{1}{q^{n/2q}(4\pi t)^{{\frac{n}{2}(1-\frac{1}{q})}}}=0

∎

Lemma 2.9
.

(
Holder decomposition of
u(x,t)
)
)
Given the convolution integral solution
u(x,t)
for the CIVP for the heat
equation on
\mathbf{Q}\subset{\mathbf{R}}^{n}
then for
\tfrac{1}{p}+\tfrac{1}{q}=1

there is a Holder decomposition

\displaystyle u(x,t)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\leq\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|h(x-y,t)|^{q}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/q}\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\phi(y)|^{p}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\equiv\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)|^{\frac{p}{p-1}}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{\frac{p-1}{p}}\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\phi(y)|^{p}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|h(x-y,t)|^{p}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{p}\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\phi(y)|^{p}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/p}

(2.39)

\displaystyle\equiv\left(\|h(x-\bullet,t)\|_{L_{p}(\mathbf{Q})}\right)^{p^{2}}\|\phi(\bullet)\|_{L_{p}(\mathbf{Q})}

since
q=p/(p-1)\leq p
and
h(x-y,t)\geq 0
and

\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}h(x-y,t)\geq 0
for all
t>0
.

Proof.

Define the
L_{p}(\mathbf{Q})
norms

\displaystyle\|h(x-\bullet,t)\|_{L_{p}(\mathbf{Q})}=\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|h(x-y,t)|^{p}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/p}

(2.40)

\displaystyle\|\phi(\bullet)\|_{L_{p}(\mathbf{Q})}=\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|{\phi}(y)|^{p}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/p}

Let
\bm{u}(x-y,t)
and
v(y)
be two generic functions defined for all

(x,y)\in\mathbf{Q}
and
t>0
. From Young’s inequality

(2.41)

\displaystyle|\mathbf{u}(x-y,t)v(y)|\leq\frac{|u(x,y,t)|^{q}}{q}+\frac{|\phi(y)|^{p}}{p}

Define

(2.42)

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}(x-y,t)=\frac{h(x-y,t)}{\|h(x-\bullet,t)\|_{L_{p}({\mathbf{Q}})}},~~\bm{v}(y)=\frac{\phi(y)}{\|\phi(\bullet)\|_{L_{p}({\mathbf{Q}})}}

then

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\bm{u}(x-y,t)\bm{v}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)=\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\frac{h(x-y,t)}{\|h(x-\bullet,t)\|_{L_{p}({\mathbf{Q}})}}\frac{\phi(y)}{\|\phi(\bullet)\|_{L_{p}({\mathbf{Q}})}}d\mu_{n}(y)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\frac{|h(x-y,t)|^{q}}{q\|h(x-\bullet,t)\|^{q}_{L_{p}({\mathbf{Q}})}}d\mu_{n}(y)+\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\frac{\phi(y)|^{p}}{p\|\phi(\bullet)\|^{p}_{L_{p}({\mathbf{Q}})}}d\mu_{n}(y)

(2.43)

\displaystyle=\frac{\|h(x-\bullet,t)\|^{q}_{L_{p}({\mathbf{Q}})}}{q\|h(x-\bullet,t)\|^{q}_{L_{p}({\mathbf{Q}})}}+\frac{\|\phi(\bullet)\|^{p}_{L_{p}({\mathbf{Q}})}}{p\|\phi(\bullet)\|^{p}_{L_{p}({\mathbf{Q}})}}=\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{p}=1

and so (2.39) follows.
∎

Corollary 2.10
.

The moments can be Holder decomposed as

\displaystyle u(x,t)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|h(x-y,t)\phi(y)|^{p}d\mu_{n}(y)\leq\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|h(x-y,t)|^{q}d\mu_{n}(y)y\right)^{p/q}\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\phi(y)|^{p}d\mu_{n}(y)y\right)

\displaystyle\equiv\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|h(x-y,t)|^{\frac{p}{p-1}}d^{n}y\right)^{(p-1)}\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\phi(y)|^{p}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|h(x-y,t)|^{p}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{p}\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\phi(y)|^{p}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)

(2.44)

\displaystyle\equiv\left(\|h(x-\bullet,t)\|_{L_{p}({\mathbf{Q})}}\right)^{p^{2}}\|\phi(\bullet)\|_{L_{p}(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{Q})}}

Lemma 2.11
.

Given the Cauchy IVP (2.29) and the solution (2.31) with
u\in C^{\infty}(\bm{\mathbf{R}}^{n}\times[0,\infty)
, then
\exists
a dimensional constant
c=c(n)
such that for all
x\in\bm{\mathbf{R}}^{n}
and
t>0
.

(2.45)

|{\nabla}u(x,t)|\leq\frac{c(n)}{\sqrt{t}}\|\phi\|_{\infty}

Proof.

\displaystyle|{\nabla}u(x,t)|

\displaystyle={(4\pi t)^{-3/2}}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\frac{\|x-y\|}{-2t}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{4t}\right)\phi(y)d^{3}y

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|f\|_{\infty}}{(4\pi t)^{3/2}}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\frac{\|x-y\|}{-2t}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{4t}\right)d\mu_{n}(y)

(2.46)

\displaystyle=\frac{\|f\|_{\infty}}{(4\pi)^{n/2}\sqrt{t}}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\|y\|\exp\left(-\|y\|^{2}\right)d\mu_{n}(y)

∎

Theorem 2.12
.

Given the initial value Cauchy problem for the inhomogenous heat equation

(2.47)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\Delta\right)u(x,t)=f(x,t),~(x\in{\mathbf{R}}^{n},t\in(0,\infty))

(2.48)

\displaystyle u(x,0)=\phi(x),~(x\in\mathbf{R}^{n},t=0)

Here,
f\in C^{2}({\mathbf{R}}^{n})
is a suitable smooth continuous function. The general(and unique) solution
u\in\mathrm{C}^{2}({\mathbf{R}}^{n}\times(0,\infty))\bigcap\mathrm{C}({\mathbf{R}}^{n}\times(0,\infty))
is
\mathbf{[39]}
.

\displaystyle u(x,t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{n/2}}\int_{0}^{R}\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{n}}\frac{\exp\left(\frac{-\|x-y\|^{2}}{|t-s|^{2}}\right)}{|t-s|^{2}}f(y,s)d^{3}yds

(2.49)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{3/2}}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{4t}\right)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y),~(x\in{\mathbf{R}}^{n},t>0)

Proof.

The proof is standard and readily follows from Duhamel’s Principle
\mathbf{[44]}
.
∎

Theorem 2.13
.

(\underline{Maximum~principle~for~the~CIVP})

If
u\in\mathrm{C}^{2}({\mathbf{R}}^{n}\times(0,\infty)\bigcap\mathrm{C}({\mathbf{R}}^{n}\times(0,\infty)
solves the homogenous CIVP with

f=0
and satisfies the growth condition

(2.50)

u(x,t)\leq Ae^{a\|x\|^{2}},~(x\in{\mathbf{R}}^{n},t\in(0,\infty))

then

(2.51)

\sup|u|_{{\mathbf{R}}^{n}\times(0,\infty)}=\sup|\phi|_{{\mathbf{R}}^{n}}

The solution (2.49) is unique.

Lemma 2.14
.

Let
u\in\mathrm{C}^{2}(\bm{\mathbf{Q}}\times(0,T])\bigcap\mathrm{C}^{2}(\bm{\mathbf{Q}}\times(0,T])
solve the inhomogeneous Cauchy problem. Then there is at most one unique solution
u(x,t)
satisfying the growth condition
|u(x,t)|\leq Ae^{\|x\|^{2}}
for
t>0
.

Proof.

If u and v are both solutions then set
w=\pm(u-v)
and apply the maximum principle so that one must have
u=v
.
∎

Definition 2.15
.

Given a general solution for the heat equation CIVP, the following Dirichlet energy integrals can be defined
\mathbf{[44]}
:

(2.52)

\displaystyle\bm{\mathrm{I\!E}}_{I}(t)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}|u(x,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(x)\equiv\frac{1}{2}\|u(\bullet,t)\|_{L_{2}}^{2}

(2.53)

\displaystyle\bm{\mathrm{I\!E}}_{II}(t)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}|\nabla u(x,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(x)\equiv\frac{1}{2}\|u(\bullet,t)\|_{L_{2}}^{2}

On a bounded domain
\bm{\mathbf{Q}}\subset{\mathbf{R}^{n}}

(2.54)

\displaystyle\bm{\mathrm{I\!E}}_{I,\bm{\mathbf{Q}}}(t)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\bm{\mathbf{Q}}}|u(x,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(x)\equiv\frac{1}{2}\|u(\bullet,t)\|_{L_{2},\bm{\mathbf{Q}}}^{2}

(2.55)

\displaystyle\bm{\mathrm{I\!E}}_{II,\bm{\mathbf{Q}}}(t)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\bm{\mathbf{Q}}}|\nabla u(x,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(x)\equiv\frac{1}{2}\|u(\bullet,t)\|_{L_{2},\bm{\mathbf{Q}}}^{2}

The time derivative is then non decreasing in that

(2.56)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\bm{\mathrm{I\!E}}_{I}(t)=\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}^{n}}|u(x,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(x)=\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|u(\bullet,t)\|_{L_{2}}^{2}<0

The energy integral for the CIVP with a heat source term, satisfies the following estimate.

Theorem 2.16
.

Let
{\mathbf{Q}}\subset{\mathbf{R}}^{n}
be a bounded domain with the CIVP

(2.57)

\displaystyle\square u(x,t)=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\Delta\right)u(x,t)=f(x,t),~~x\in{\mathbf{Q}},t\in[0,T]

(2.58)

\displaystyle u(x,0)=\phi(x),~~x\in{\mathbf{Q}},t=0

(2.59)

\displaystyle u(x,t)=0,~~x\in\partial{\mathbf{Q}}

where
f(x,t)
is a heat source term and the temperature is zero on the boundary. Then the following estimate holds

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\sup_{[0,T]}\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|u(x,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(x)+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\nabla u(x,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(x)dt

(2.60)

\displaystyle\leq T\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|f(x,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(x)+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\phi(x)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(x)

or

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sup_{[0,T]}\bm{\mathrm{I\!E}}_{I}(t)+\int_{0}^{T}\bm{\mathrm{I\!E}}_{I}(t)dt

(2.61)

\displaystyle\leq T\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|f(x,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(x)+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\phi(x)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(x)\equiv\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\|f(\bullet,t)\|_{L_{2}}^{2}dt+\|\phi(\bullet)\|_{L_{2}}^{2}

For the inhomogeneous problem with
f(x,t)=0

(2.62)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sup_{[0,T]}\bm{\mathrm{I\!E}}_{I}(t)+\int_{0}^{T}{\bm{\mathrm{I\!E}}}_{I}(t)dt\leq+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\phi(x)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(x)\equiv+\|\phi(\bullet)\|_{L_{2}}^{2}

2.1.
Eigenfunction expansion representation of the heat kernel

The heat kernel has a formal eigenfunction-eigenvalue representation via the spectral properties of the Laplacian
\mathbf{[9]}
.

Theorem 2.17
.

Let
{\mathbf{Q}}\subset{\mathbf{R}}^{n}
be a compact set or domain with boundary
\partial{\mathbf{Q}}
and let
\chi_{k}(x)
be a set of functions existing for all
x\in{\mathbf{Q}}
such that
\chi_{k}:{\mathbf{Q}}\rightarrow{\mathbf{R}}
. Elliptic spectral theory for the Laplacian
\Delta_{x}
establishes that
{\Delta}_{x}\chi_{k}(x)=-\vartheta_{k}\chi_{k}(x)
, where
\{\vartheta_{k}\}
are a set of eigenvalues. The eigenfunctions form an orthonormal set so that

\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{n}}\chi_{i}(x)\chi_{k}(x)d\mu_{n}(x)=\delta_{ij}
. The set
\{\chi_{k}(x)\}
form an orthonormal basis with respect to the
L_{2}
norms. The heat kernel then has the eigenvalue-eigenvector representation
\mathbf{[9]}

(2.63)

\displaystyle h(x-y,t)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\exp(-\vartheta_{k}t){\chi}_{k}(x)\chi_{k}(y)

which is unique and well defined on
{\mathbf{Q}}\times\bm{\mathbf{Q}}\times(0,\infty)
. Given any function
\phi_{0}(x)=u(x,0)\in L_{2}({\mathbf{Q}})
the function

(2.64)

\displaystyle u(x,t)=\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\exp(-\vartheta_{k}t)\chi_{k}(x)\chi_{k}(y)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)=\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}h(x-y,t)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

solves the heat equation
{\square}u(x,t)=(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-{\Delta}_{x})u((x,t)=0
for
x\in{\mathbf{Q}},t>0
and
u(x,0)=u_{0}(x)
for
x\in{\mathbf{Q}},t=0
and the BC
u(x,t)=0
on
\partial{\mathbf{Q}}\times(0,\infty)
. Also
\lim_{t\uparrow 0}u(x,t)=u_{0}(x)
. Moreover
h(x-y,t)
is positive on

\bm{\mathbf{Q}}{\char 92\relax}\partial{\mathbf{Q}}\times{\mathbf{Q}}{\char 92\relax}\partial{\mathbf{Q}}\times(0,\infty)
and
\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}h(x-y,t)\leq 1

Proof.

A detailed proof and discussion is given in Li
\mathbf{[9]}
. Basically, any function
\psi_{0}(x)=\phi(x)
can be expanded as a weighted sum

(2.65)

\displaystyle u(x,0)=u_{0}(x)=\phi(x)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{A}_{k}\chi_{k}(x)

with
\mathcal{A}_{k}=\int_{\mathbf{Q}}u_{0}(x)\chi_{k}(x)d\mu_{n}(x)
. Any
u(x,t)
with the expansion

(2.66)

\displaystyle u(x,t)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\exp(-\vartheta_{k}t)\mathcal{A}_{k}\chi_{k}(x)

then satisfies the heat equation
{\square}u(x,t)=(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}-{\Delta}_{x})u(x,t)=0
with prescribed boundary and initial conditions. To show that the sum (2.66) is a solution of the heat equation, take the derivatives so that

(2.67)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u(x,t)=-\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\vartheta_{k}\exp(-\vartheta_{k}t)A_{k}\chi_{k}(x)

(2.68)

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}u(x,t)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\exp(-\vartheta_{k}t)A_{k}\nabla_{x}\chi_{k}(x)

(2.69)

\displaystyle\Delta_{x}u(x,t)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\exp(-\vartheta_{k}t)A_{k}\Delta_{x}\chi_{k}(x)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\exp(-\vartheta_{i}t)A_{k}(-\vartheta_{k}\chi_{k}(x))

since the Laplacian has the spectral property
\Delta\chi_{k}(x)=-\vartheta_{k}\chi_{k}(x)
. Then clearly
{\square}u(x,t)=\left(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}-\nabla\right)u(x,t)=0
as required.
∎

Example 2.18
.

The solution of the heat equation on the ring or circle
{\mathbf{S}}^{1}

has a Fourier series representation. The CIVP for the heat equation on the circle is

(2.70)

\displaystyle{\square}_{\theta}u(\theta,t)=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\theta^{2}}\right)u(\theta,t)=0,~x\in{\mathbf{S}},t>0

(2.71)

\displaystyle u(\theta,0)=f(\theta),x\in{\mathbf{S}^{1}},t=0

Since
u(\theta,t)=u(\theta+2\pi,t)
, the solution is periodic on the ring so there is a Fourier series solution

(2.72)

\displaystyle u(\theta,t)=\mathcal{A}_{o}+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\exp(-m^{2}t)\left(\mathcal{A}_{m}\cos(m\theta)+\mathcal{B}_{m}\sin(m\theta)\right)

with Fourier coefficients
\mathcal{A}_{i}=\tfrac{1}{\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}f(\theta)\cos(m\theta)d\theta
and
\mathcal{B}_{i}=\tfrac{1}{\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}f(\theta)\sin(m\theta)d\theta

Remark 2.19
.

Any heat kernel
h_{(\mathbf{M})}(x,y,t)
on a Riemannian manifold
({\mathbf{M}},\bm{g})
approaches the heat kernel in Euclidean space as
t\rightarrow 0
and
d(x,y)\rightarrow 0
since all Riemannian manifolds are locally Euclidean
\mathbf{[9]}
. Then for all
(x,y)\in{\mathbf{M}}\char 92\relax\partial{\mathbf{M}}

(2.73)

\displaystyle h_{(\mathbf{M})}(x-y,t)\sim|4\pi t|^{-n/2}\exp\left(-\frac{d^{2}(x,y)}{4t}\right)

as
t\rightarrow 0
and
d(x,y)\rightarrow 0
. Also, for all fixed

(x,y)\in{\mathbf{M}}\char 92\relax\partial{\mathbf{M}}
one has
h_{\mathbf{M}}(x-y,t)\rightarrow 0
as
t\rightarrow\infty
. Also, for
t\gg 0
, the asymptotic behavior is dominated by the first eigenvalue and eigenvectors so that

(2.74)

\displaystyle h_{(\mathbf{M})}(x-y,t\sim\exp(-\vartheta_{1}t)\chi_{1}(x)\chi_{1}(y)

As an example, the heat kernel is known for hyperbolic 3-space
{\mathbf{H}}_{3}
which has negative curvature
\mathbf{[52]}
so that

(2.75)

\displaystyle h_{(\mathbf{H}_{3})}(x-y,t)=|4\pi t|^{-n/2}\frac{d(x,y)}{\sinh d(x,y)}\exp\left(-\left((t+\frac{d(x,y)}{4t}\right)\right)

Since
\sinh d(x,y)=d(x,y)+\tfrac{1}{6}|d(x,y)|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{120}|d(x,y)|^{5}+...
then

\sinh d(x,y)\sim d(x,y)
. For small
t
and
d(x,y)\sim|x-y|
the kernel

h_{(\mathbf{H}_{3})}
then approaches the Euclidean heat kernel so that

(2.76)

\displaystyle h_{(\mathbf{H}_{3})}=|4\pi t|^{-n/2}\frac{d(x,y)}{d(x,y)}\exp\left(-\left(t+\frac{d(x,y)}{4t}\right)\right)\sim|4\pi t|^{-n/2}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right)

The eigenvalue-eigenvector representation can also be used to prove that heat
kernel obeys the semi-group property
\mathbf{[9]}
.

Lemma 2.20
.

The heat kernel obeys the semi-group property
\mathbf{[9]}

(2.77)

\displaystyle h(x-y,t+s)=\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{n}}h(x-z,t)h(y-z,s)d\mu_{n}(z)

and in particular

(2.78)

\displaystyle h(x-x,2t)=\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{n}}h(x-y,t)h(x-y,t)d\mu_{n}(y)

Proof.

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{n}}h(x-z,t)h(y-z,t)=\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{n}}\sum_{i,j}^{n}\exp(-\vartheta_{i}t)e^{-\vartheta_{j}t}\chi_{i}(x)\chi_{i}(z)\chi_{j}(x)\chi_{j}(z)d\mu_{n}(z)

(2.79)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}^{n}e^{-\vartheta_{i}(t+s)}\chi_{i}(x)\chi_{i}(y)\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{n}}\chi_{i}(z)\chi_{j}(z)d\mu_{n}(z)=\sum_{i}^{n}e^{-\vartheta_{i}(t+s)}\chi_{i}(x)\chi_{i}(y)\equiv h(x-y,t+s)

and (2.77) follows. This property holds over a closed domain
{\mathbf{Q}}
.
∎

The following lemma (Jensen’s inequality) is required for Theorem (2.22)

Lemma 2.21
.

Let
\Phi
be a concave function and
f:{\mathbf{Q}}\rightarrow\mathbf{R}
. Then

(2.80)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\Phi(f(x))d\mu_{n}(x)\leq\Phi\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}f(x)d\mu_{n}(x)\right)

If
\varphi(f)=f^{p}
for
p\geq 2
then

(2.81)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|f(x)|^{p}d\mu_{n}(x)\leq\left(\int f(x)d\mu_{n}(x)\right)^{p}

Then if
f(x)=\varphi(x)\varphi(x)

(2.82)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\varphi(x)\varphi(x)|^{p}d\mu_{n}(x)\leq\left(\int\varphi(x)\varphi(x)d\mu_{n}(x)\right)^{p}

A bound on the
L_{p}
moments of the heat kernel were discussed in Theorem 2.8. A comparable estimate can be derived from the eigenfunction expansion.

Theorem 2.22
.

The
L_{p}
moments of the heat kernel have the estimate

(2.83)

\displaystyle(\|h(x,\bullet,t)\|_{L_{p}})^{p}=\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}h(x-y,t)|^{p}d\mu_{n}(y)\leq|h(x-x,t)|^{p/2}\lambda(t)=\frac{\lambda(t)}{(4\pi t)^{pn/4}}

where
\lambda(t)=\exp(-\xi t)/(1-\exp(-\xi t))
and
\xi
is a constant.

Proof.

The proof utilises the Cauchy Schwartz and Jensen inequalities

\displaystyle(\|h(x-\bullet,t)\|_{L_{p}})^{p}=\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}h(x-y,t)|^{p}d\mu_{n}(y)=\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\left|\sum_{k=1}^{n}\exp(-\vartheta_{k}t)\chi_{k}(x)\chi_{k}(y)\right|^{p}d\mu_{n}(y)

\displaystyle\equiv=\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\left|\sum_{k=1}^{n}\exp(-\tfrac{1}{2}\vartheta_{k}t)\chi_{k}(x)\exp(-\tfrac{1}{2}\vartheta_{k}t)\chi_{k}(y)\right|^{p}d\mu_{n}(y)

\displaystyle\leq=\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left|\exp(-\tfrac{1}{2}\vartheta_{k}t)\chi_{k}(x)\right|^{2}\right)^{p/2}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left|\exp(-\tfrac{1}{2}\vartheta_{k}t)\chi_{k}(y)\right|^{2}\right)^{p/2}d\mu_{n}(y)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\exp(-\vartheta_{k}t)\chi_{k}(x)\chi_{k}(x)\right)^{p/2}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}\exp(-\vartheta_{k}t)\chi_{k}(y)\chi_{k}(y)\right)^{p/2}d\mu_{n}(y)

\displaystyle=|h(x-x,t)|^{p/2}\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\exp(-\vartheta_{k}t)\chi_{k}(y)\chi_{k}(y)\right)^{p/2}d\mu_{n}(y)

\displaystyle\leq|h(x-x,t)|^{p/2}\int_{{\mathbf{q}}}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}\exp(-\vartheta_{k}t)\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\chi_{k}(y)\chi_{k}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{p/2}

(2.84)

\displaystyle\leq|h(x-x,t)|^{p/2}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\exp(-\vartheta_{k}t)\right)^{p/2}

The sum
\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\exp(-\vartheta_{k}t)\right)^{p/2}
is essentially
a geometric series and converges. Since
\vartheta_{1}<\vartheta_{2}<\vartheta_{3}...
, it can be bounded by a convergent series of the form

(2.85)

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\exp(-\vartheta_{k}t)\right)^{p/2}\leq\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\exp(-k\alpha t)\right)^{p/2}\equiv\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}|\exp(-\alpha t)|^{k}\right)^{p/2}

where
\alpha
is a constant. Since
\exp(-\alpha t)\leq 1
for all
t>0
, the series converges so that

(2.86)

\displaystyle\lambda(t)=\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}e^{-k\alpha t}\right)^{p/2}\equiv\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}|e^{-\alpha t}|^{k}\right)^{p/2}=\frac{1}{1-e^{-\alpha t}}

and
S\rightarrow 1
as
t\rightarrow\infty
. Hence, we have the estimate

\displaystyle(\|h(x-y,t)\|_{L_{p}})^{p}=\int h(x-y,t)|^{p}d\mu_{n}(y)\leq|h(x-x,t)|^{p/2}\frac{1}{1-e^{-\alpha t}}

(2.87)

\displaystyle=|h(x-x,t)|^{p/2}\lambda(t)\sim|h(x,x,t)|

∎

Corollary 2.23
.

The estimate can be compared to the standard estimate given in (2.38). For
p=2

(2.88)

\displaystyle(\|h(x-y,t)\|_{L_{2}})^{2}\leq\left|\Lambda_{p}t^{-\frac{n}{2}(1-\frac{1}{2})}\right|^{2}=\Lambda_{p}t^{-n/4}

whereas (2.87) gives

(2.89)

\displaystyle(\|h(x-y,t)\|_{L_{2}})^{2}=\frac{1}{4\pi t^{2n/4}}\sim t^{-n/4}

and so the estimates are comparable.

2.2.
Mean value properties on a heat ball

Definition 2.24
.

Let
{\mathbf{U}}\subset{\mathbf{R}}^{n}
be an open and bounded domain and fix a time interval
(0,T]
. Then:

(1)

The parabolic cylinder is defined as
{\mathrm{U}}_{T}={\mathbf{U}}\times(0,T]
.

(2)

The parabolic boundary is
\Gamma_{T}=\widehat{{\mathbf{U}}}_{T}-{\mathbf{U}}_{T}

{\mathbf{U}}_{T}
is defined as the parabolic interior of
\widetilde{{\mathbf{U}}}_{T}\times(0,T]
and includes the top
\mathbf{U}_{T}\times\{t=T\}
. The boundary includes the vertical sides and bottom but not the top
\mathbf{[44]}
.

The mean-value property and maximum principle for the elliptic Laplace equation can be defined with respect to a ball
{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)\subset{\mathbf{R}}^{n}
. An analogous mean-value property and maximum principle for the heat equation can be defined with respect to a ”heat ball”
\bm{\mathrm{I\!B}}(x,t;R)\subset{\mathbf{U}}_{T}
.

Definition 2.25
.

For fixed
x\in{\mathbf{R}}^{n},t\in{\mathbf{R}}^{+}
and
R>0
define a heat ball as the region

(2.90)

\bm{\mathrm{I\!B}}(x,t;R)=\bigg\{(y,s)\in\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n+1}|s\leq t,h(x-y,t-s)\geq\frac{1}{R^{n}}\bigg\}\subset{\mathbf{U}}_{T}

where
h(x-y,t-s)=\tfrac{1}{4\pi|t-s|^{3/2}}\exp\left(-\|x-y\|^{2}/4|t-s|\right)
is the heat kernel or fundamental solution. The point
(x,t)
is the centre of the top of the heat ball
\mathbf{[44]}
.

Theorem 2.26
.

(
\underline{Mean-value~property~of~heat~equation}
)
Let
u\in C^{2}({\mathbf{U}}_{T})
solve the heat equation then for all
\bm{\mathrm{I\!B}}(x,t;R)\subset{\mathbf{U}}_{T}

(2.91)

u(x,t)=\frac{1}{4R^{n}}\iint_{\bm{\mathrm{I\!B}}(x,t;R)}u(y,s)\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{|t-s|^{2}}d^{3}yds

Values of
u(x,t)
depend on past times
s\leq t
.

The proof is given in Evans [39].

3.
Gradient estimates: Li-Yau and Harnack inequalities

The solution of the heat equation and its gradient on a generic Riemannian manifold satisfies some Harnack-Like inequalities which are important within differential geometry and
geometric analysis
\mathbf{[53]}
. Firstly, there are also some basic formulas arising from the positivity of solutions.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
{\mathbf{M}}=\bm{\mathbf{R}}^{n}
and
u=u(x,t)
is a solution of the heat equation
on
{\mathbf{R}}^{n}
or
{\mathbf{Q}}\subset\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}
. Then the following
equalities hold

(3.1)

\displaystyle{\square}(\log u)=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\Delta_{x}\right)(\log u)=\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|u|^{2}}

(3.2)

\displaystyle{\square}(u\log u)=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\Delta_{x}\right)(u\log u)=-\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|u|}

or equivalently

(3.3)

\displaystyle|u|^{2}{\square}(\log u)=|u|^{2}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\Delta_{x})\right)(\log u)=|\nabla\psi|^{2}

(3.4)

\displaystyle-|u|{\square}(u\log u)=-|u|\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\Delta_{x}\right)(u\log u)={|\nabla u|^{2}}

In terms of the convolution integral solution

(3.5)

\displaystyle{\square}(\log u)=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\Delta_{x}\right)(\log u)=\frac{\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\nabla h(x-y,t)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{2}}{\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{2}}

(3.6)

\displaystyle{\square}(u\log u)=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\Delta_{x}\right)(u\log u)=-\frac{\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\nabla h(x-y,t)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{2}}{\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|}

or equivalently

(3.7)

\displaystyle|u|^{2}{\square}(\log u)=|u|^{2}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\Delta_{x}\right)(\log u)={\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\nabla h(x-y,t)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{2}}

(3.8)

\displaystyle-|u|{\square}(u\log u)=-|u|\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\Delta_{x}\right)(u\log u)={\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\nabla h(x-y,t)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{2}}

Proof.

The proof follows easily by direct calculation so that

\displaystyle{\square}(\log u)=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\Delta u\right))(\log u)\equiv\frac{\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u}{u}-\nabla(\nabla\log u)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta u}{u}-\nabla\left(\frac{\nabla u}{u}\right)=\frac{\Delta u}{u}-\frac{(\nabla\nabla u)u-\nabla u\nabla u}{|u|^{2}}

(3.9)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta u}{u}-\frac{\Delta u}{u}+\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|u|^{2}}=\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|u|^{2}}

and similary for (3.2).
∎

Theorem 3.2
.

(
\underline{Li-Yau~inequality}
)
Let
{\mathbf{M}}
be an n-dimensional manifold with
\bm{\mathrm{Ric}}({\mathbf{M}})\geq k^{2}
. The solution
u(x,t)
of the heat equation

\displaystyle{\square}u(x,t)\equiv\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\Delta\right)u(x,t)=0,~x\in{\mathbf{M}},t>0

(3.10)

\displaystyle u(x,0)=u(x),~x\in{\mathbf{M}},t=0

then satisfies the estimate

(3.11)

\displaystyle\frac{\|\nabla u(x,t)\|^{2}}{\|u(x,t)\|^{2}}-\alpha\frac{\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u(x,t)}{u(x,t)}\leq\frac{1}{2}n\alpha^{2}\left\{\frac{1}{t}+\frac{k^{2}}{2(\alpha-1)}\right\}

On Euclidean space with
{\mathbf{M}}={\mathrm{R}}^{n}
then
\mathbf{g}_{ij}=\delta_{ij}
and
k=0
. Letting
\alpha\rightarrow 1

(3.12)

\displaystyle\frac{\|\nabla u(x,t)\|^{2}}{\|u(x,t)\|^{2}}-\frac{\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u(x,t)}{u(x,t)}\leq\frac{1}{2}n\frac{1}{t}

or equivalently

(3.13)

\displaystyle|\nabla\log u(x,t)|^{2}-|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}logu(x,t)|\leq\frac{n}{2t}

Proof.

Define
\mathcal{Y}=\log u
and
\mathcal{W}=t(|\nabla u|^{2}-\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u)
. For any function
\psi\in C^{\infty}({\mathbf{M}})
the Bochner formula is

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}{\Delta}|{\nabla}\psi|^{2}=|\nabla^{2}\psi|+\big\langle\nabla(\Delta\psi),\nabla\psi\big\rangle-\bm{\mathrm{Ric}}(\nabla\psi,\nabla\psi)

(3.14)

\displaystyle|\nabla^{2}\psi|+\big\langle\nabla(\Delta\psi),\nabla\psi\big\rangle\equiv|\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\psi|+\big\langle\nabla(\Delta\psi),\nabla\psi\big\rangle

where
\langle A,B\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}B^{i}
is the inner product.
Application to
\mathcal{Y}
gives

(3.15)

\displaystyle{\Delta}|\nabla\mathcal{Y}|^{2}\geq 2|\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\mathcal{Y}|+2\bigg\langle{\nabla}({\Delta}\mathcal{Y}),\nabla\mathcal{Y}\bigg\rangle

Since
\mathcal{Y}=\log u
and since
u
is a solution of the heat equation
{\square}u(x,t)=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-{\Delta}\right)u=0
, then taking the Laplacian
{\Delta}\mathcal{Y}=\Delta\log u=\tfrac{1}{u}\nabla_{i}\nabla^{i}u-\tfrac{1}{u^{2}}\nabla_{i}u\nabla_{i}u
gives

(3.16)

\displaystyle{\Delta}\mathcal{Y}=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mathcal{Y}-|{\nabla}\mathcal{Y}|^{2}=-\frac{1}{t}\mathcal{W}

The B-W formula the gives

(3.17)

\displaystyle{\Delta}|\nabla\mathcal{Y}|^{2}\geq 2|{\nabla}_{i}{\nabla}\mathcal{Y}_{j}\mathcal{Y}|+2\left\langle{\nabla}\left(-\frac{1}{t}\mathcal{W}\right),{\nabla}\mathcal{Y}\right\rangle

or

(3.18)

\displaystyle{\Delta}|t\nabla\mathcal{Y}|^{2}\geq 2t|\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\mathcal{Y}-2\bigg\langle\nabla\mathcal{W},\nabla\mathcal{Y}\bigg\rangle

Now since
|\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\mathcal{Y}|^{2}\geq\frac{1}{n}|{\Delta}\mathcal{Y}|^{2}
and using (3.17)

(3.19)

\displaystyle|\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\mathcal{Y}|^{2}\geq\frac{1}{n}|{\Delta}\mathcal{Y}|^{2}\equiv\frac{1}{n}\frac{\mathcal{W}^{2}}{t^{2}}

so that

(3.20)

\displaystyle{\Delta}(t|\nabla\mathcal{Y}|^{2})\geq\frac{2}{nt}|\mathcal{W}\|^{2}-2\big\langle\nabla\mathcal{W},{\nabla}\mathcal{Y}\big\rangle

Since

(3.21)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}(t|{\nabla}\mathcal{Y}|^{2})=|{\nabla}\mathcal{Y}|^{2}+t\frac{\partial}{\partial t}(|\nabla\mathcal{Y}|^{2})

it follows that

(3.22)

\displaystyle-{\square}\mathcal{Y}=\left({\Delta}-\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\right)(t|\nabla\mathcal{Y}|^{2})\geq\frac{2}{nt}|\mathcal{W}|^{2}-2\bigg\langle{\nabla}\mathcal{W},{\nabla}\mathcal{Y}\bigg\rangle-|\nabla\mathcal{Y}|^{2}-t\frac{\partial}{\partial t}(|\nabla\mathcal{Y}|^{2})

However, using (3.18)

\displaystyle-{\square}\mathcal{Y}=\left(\Delta-\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\right))(t|\nabla\mathcal{Y}|^{2})=t\Delta(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mathcal{Y})-t\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}\mathcal{Y}-\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mathcal{Y}

(3.23)

\displaystyle=t\frac{\partial}{\partial t}(\Delta\mathcal{Y})-t\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}\mathcal{Y}-\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mathcal{Y}=t\frac{\partial}{\partial t}(|\nabla\mathcal{Y}|^{2})-\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mathcal{Y}

From this and
\mathcal{W}=t(|\nabla\mathcal{Y}|^{2}-\partial\mathcal{Y})
we have

(3.24)

\displaystyle-{\square}\mathcal{W}=\left(\Delta-\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\right)\mathcal{W}\geq\frac{2}{nt}|\mathcal{W}|^{2}-2\bigg\langle\nabla\mathcal{W},\nabla u\bigg\rangle-|\nabla\mathcal{Y}|^{2}+\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mathcal{Y}

(3.25)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{nt}|\mathcal{W}|^{2}-2\bigg\langle\nabla\mathcal{W},\nabla\mathcal{Y}\bigg\rangle-\frac{\mathcal{W}}{t}

The Maximum Principle implies that
\mathcal{Z}<n/2
so that
t(|\nabla\mathcal{Y}|^{2}-\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mathcal{Y})<n/2
and the inequality follows.

Theorem 3.3
.

(
Li-Yau inequality for convolution integral
)
Let
u(x,t)=\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}h(x-y,t)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

be a solution of the heat equation
{\square}u(x,t)=0
for

x\in{\mathbf{Q}},t>0
for initial data
u(x,0)=\phi(x)
. Then the Li-Yau inequality (3.12) implies the following bounds

\displaystyle\left|\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\nabla_{x}h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right|\leq\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/2}

(3.26)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}nt^{-1}\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)

and also

\displaystyle\Lambda_{1}^{2}t^{-n}\int_{\bm{\mathbf{Q}}}\left(-\frac{2|x-y|}{\Lambda_{1}t}\right)^{2}\exp(-\frac{2|x-y|^{2}}{\Lambda_{1}t})d\mu_{n}(y)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/2}

(3.27)

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda_{2}^{2}t^{-n}\int_{\bm{\mathbf{Q}}}\exp(-\frac{2|x-y|^{2}}{\Lambda_{2}t})d\mu_{n}(y)

utilising the double-sided integral bound (2.7).

Proof.

Using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality

(3.28)

\displaystyle|\nabla_{x}u(x,t)|^{2}=\left|\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\nabla_{x}h(x-y,t)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{2}\leq\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\nabla_{x}h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\phi(y)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)

(3.29)

\displaystyle|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u(x,t)|=\left|\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}h(x-y,t)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{2}\leq\left(\int_{\bm{\mathbf{Q}}}|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{\bm{\mathbf{Q}}}|\phi(y)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/2}

(3.30)

\displaystyle|u(x,t)|=\left|\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}h(x-y,t)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|\leq\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\phi(y)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/2}

The Li-Yau inequality then becomes

\displaystyle\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\nabla_{x}h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\phi(y)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\phi(y)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/2}

(3.31)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}nt^{-1}\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\phi(y)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)

Cancelling the
\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\phi(y)|^{2}d^{n}y
terms then gives (3.26). Applying
the double-sided bound then gives (3.27).
∎

Lemma 3.4
.

Consider the CIVP for the heat equation on
{\mathbf{R}}^{n}
with constant initial data

(3.32)

\displaystyle{\square}u(x,t)=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\Delta\right)u(x,t)=0,x\in{\mathbf{R}}^{n},t>0

(3.33)

\displaystyle u(x,0)=\lambda,~x\in{\mathbf{R}}^{n},t=0

with solution

(3.34)

\displaystyle u(x,t)=\frac{\lambda}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{n}}e^{-\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{4t}}d\mu_{n}(y)\equiv\lambda\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{n}}h(x-y,t)d\mu_{n}(y)

Then the Li-Yau inequality (3.12)becomes

(3.35)

\displaystyle\frac{\left|\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{n}}{\nabla}_{x}h(x-y,t)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{2}}{\left|\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{n}}h(x-y,t)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{2}}-\frac{\left|\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{n}}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}h(x-y,t)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|}{\left|\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{n}}h(x-y,t)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|}\leq\frac{n}{2t}

or equivalently

(3.36)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{n}}{\nabla}_{x}\log(u(x,t))d\mu_{n}(x)\right|^{2}-\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{n}}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\log(u(x,t))d\mu_{n}(x)

For
n=1
this gives the bound

(3.37)

\displaystyle\frac{\exp(^{-}\frac{x^{2}}{2t})}{\pi}\frac{1}{|erf\left(\frac{x}{2t^{1/2}}\right)+1|^{2}}+\frac{x\exp(-\frac{x^{2}}{4t})}{2\pi^{1/2}t^{1/2}}\frac{1}{|erf\left(\frac{x}{2t^{1/2}}\right)+1|}\leq\frac{1}{2}

Then:

(1)

At
x=0
for any finite t (3.37) becomes
\frac{1}{\pi}\leq\frac{1}{2}
.

(2)

At any finite
x
in the limit as
t\rightarrow\infty
(3.37) becomes
\frac{1}{\pi}\leq\frac{1}{2}
.

which is clearly satisfied.

∎

A consequence or corollary of the Li-Yau estimate is the parabolic Harnack inequality (PHI) for the heat equation.

Theorem 3.5
.

(
parabolic Harnack inequality
)
Let
(\mathbf{M},\bm{g})
be a Riemannian manifold with nonvanishing Ricci curvarture.
Let
u(x,t)
be a solution of the heat equation
{\square}u(x,t)=(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-{\Delta})u(x,t)=0
, for all
x\in{\mathbf{M}}
and
t>0
. The PHI is then either of the following

\displaystyle u(y,t_{2})\geq u(x,t_{1})\left|\frac{t_{1}}{t_{2}}\right|^{n/2}\exp\left(-{|d(x,y)|^{2}}/{4|t_{2}-t_{1}|}\right)

(3.38)

\displaystyle u(x,t_{1})\geq u(y,t_{2})\left|\frac{t_{2}}{t_{1}}\right|^{n/2}\exp\left({|d(x,y)|^{2}}/{4|t_{2}-t_{1}|}\right)

for all
(x,y)\in{\mathbf{M}}
and
0<t_{1}<t_{2}
, and where
d(x,y)
is
the distance between
x
and
y
. On Euclidean space
{\mathbf{M}}=\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{m}
with zero curvature
d(x,y)=|x-y|
and

\displaystyle u(y,t_{2})\geq u(x,t_{1})\left|\frac{t_{1}}{t_{2}}\right|^{n/2}\exp(-{\|x-y\|^{2}}/{4|t_{2}-t_{1}|})

(3.39)

\displaystyle u(x,t_{1})\geq u(y,t_{2})\left|\frac{t_{2}}{t_{1}}\right|^{n/2}\exp({\|x-y|^{2}}/{4|t_{2}-t_{1}\|})

Proof.

The estimate can be established using a curve or geodesic
\gamma(t)
joining

\gamma(t_{1})=x
to
\gamma(t_{2})=y
with
|d\gamma(t)/dt|=d(x,y)
or

|d\gamma(t)/dt|=|x-y|
. Therefore
\gamma:(t_{1},t_{2})\rightarrow\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}
.Now
the Li-Yau inequality in logarithmic form is

(3.40)

\displaystyle|\nabla\log u(x,t)|^{2}-|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u(x,t)|+\frac{n}{2t}\geq 0

which also implies that
|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u(x,t)|\geq-\frac{n}{2t}
so that
\psi
does not
decrease too quickly. Recall Young’s inequality such that
|ab|\leq\tfrac{1}{p}|a|^{p}+\tfrac{1}{q}|b|^{q}
with
\tfrac{1}{p}+\tfrac{1}{q}
. For
p=q=2

|ab|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}|a|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}|b|^{2}
or

|ab|\geq-\tfrac{1}{2}|a|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}|b|^{2}
. Then

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\log u=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\log u+\left\langle{\nabla}\log u,\frac{d\gamma(t)}{dt}\right\rangle\equiv\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\log u+{\nabla}\log u\frac{d\gamma(t)}{dt}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\log u+\left(\sqrt{2}\nabla\log u\right)\left(\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{d\gamma(t)}{dt}\right)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\log u-\left(\frac{1}{2}\left|\sqrt{2}\nabla\log u\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{d\gamma(t)}{dt}\right|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\log u-\left(|\nabla\log u|^{2}+\frac{1}{4})\left|\frac{d\gamma(t)}{dt}\right|^{2}\right)

(3.41)

\displaystyle=\left(|\nabla\log u|^{2}-\frac{n}{2t}\right)-\left(|\nabla\log u|^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\left|\frac{d\gamma(t)}{dt}\right|^{2}\right)=-\frac{n}{2t}-\frac{1}{4}\left|\frac{d\gamma(t)}{dt}\right|^{2}

Integrating from
\gamma(t_{1})=x_{1}
to
\gamma(t_{2})=x_{2}

(3.42)

\displaystyle\int_{x}^{y}d\log u(\gamma(t),t)\geq-\frac{n}{2}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\frac{dt}{t}-\frac{1}{4}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\left|\frac{d\gamma(t)}{dt}\right|^{2}dt

so that

(3.43)

\displaystyle\log\left|\frac{u(y,t_{2})}{u(x,t_{1})}\right|\geq-\frac{n}{2}\log\left|\frac{t_{2}}{t_{1}}\right|-\frac{1}{4}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\left|\frac{d\gamma(t)}{dt}\right|^{2}dt

The inequality can be optimized by choosing the straight line from
x
to
y
and the result
then follows.
∎

Corollary 3.6
.

Let
u(x,t)
and
u(y,t)
satisfy the heat equations

(3.44)

\displaystyle{\square}u(x,t)=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-{\Delta}_{x}\right)u(x,t)=0,x\in\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n},t>0

(3.45)

\displaystyle{\square}u(y,t)=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-{\Delta}_{y}\right)u(y,t)=0,x\in\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n},t>0

with initial Cauchy data
u(x,0)=\lambda
and
u(y,))=\lambda
and Laplacians

{\Delta}_{x}=\tfrac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{i}\partial x^{i}}
and

{\Delta}_{y}=\tfrac{\partial^{2}}{\partial y_{i}\partial y^{i}}

The (unique) solutions are

(3.46)

\displaystyle u(x,t)=\frac{\lambda}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}\int_{\bm{\mathbf{Q}}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-z|^{2}}{4t}\right)d^{n}z\equiv\lambda\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}h(x,z,t)d^{n}z

(3.47)

\displaystyle u(y,t)=\frac{\lambda}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}\int_{\bm{\mathbf{Q}}}\exp\left(-\frac{|y-z|^{2}}{4t}\right)d^{n}z\equiv\lambda\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}h(y,z,t)d^{n}z

Then

(3.48)

\displaystyle\frac{\int_{\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}}h(x,z,t_{1})d^{n}z}{\int_{\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}}h(y,z,t_{2})d^{n}z}\geq\left|\frac{t_{1}}{t_{2}}\right|^{n/2}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4|t_{2}-t_{1}|}\right)

On
\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{+}
with
n=1
this becomes

(3.49)

\displaystyle\frac{\int_{0}^{\infty}h(x,z,t_{1})d^{n}z}{\int_{0}^{\infty}h(y,z,t_{2})d^{n}z}\geq\left|\frac{t_{1}}{t_{2}}\right|^{1/2}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4|t_{2}-t_{1}|}\right)

which is

(3.50)

\displaystyle\frac{(erf(y/2\sqrt{t_{2}})+1)}{(erf(x/2\sqrt{t_{2}})+1)}\geq\left|\frac{t_{1}}{t_{2}}\right|^{1/2}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4|t_{2}-t_{1}|}\right)

or equivalently

(3.51)

\displaystyle(erf(y/2\sqrt{t_{2}})+1)\geq\left|\frac{t_{1}}{t_{2}}\right|^{1/2}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4|t_{2}-t_{1}|}\right){(erf(x/2\sqrt{t_{2}})+1)}

Letting
y\rightarrow\infty
gives
2>0
.

4.
The Cole-Hopf transform to the heat equation

Solutions of the CIVP for the linear heat equation also enable solutions to be generated for certain classes of quasilinear parabolic PDEs including the Burger’s equation. This is done via the Cole-Hopf transform
\mathbf{[44]}
.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
A>0
and
B\in\bm{\mathrm{R}}
and consider the Cauchy problem for the following quasi-linear parabolic PDE

(4.1)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-a{\Delta}\right)\psi(x,t)+b|{\nabla}\psi(x,t)|^{2}=0,~x\in{\mathbf{R}}^{n},t>0

(4.2)

\displaystyle\psi(x,0)=I(x),x\in{\mathbf{R}}^{n},t=0

Using the Cole-Hopf transform

(4.3)

u(x,t)=\exp\left(-\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\psi(x,t)\right),~~u(x,0)=\exp\left(-\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\psi(x,0)\right)\equiv\exp\left(-\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)I(x)\right)

the function
u(x,t)
immediately satisfies the global Cauchy initial value problem for the linear heat equation (with ’conductance’
A
) such that

(4.4)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-a{\Delta}\right)u(x,t),~x\in{\mathbf{R}}^{n},t>0

(4.5)

\displaystyle u(x,0)=\exp\left(-\frac{b}{a}I(x)\right)\equiv\phi(x),~~x\in\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n},t=0

This has the solution

\displaystyle u(x,t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{n}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{4t}\right)\exp\left(-\left(\frac{B}{A}\right)I(x)\right)

(4.6)

\displaystyle\equiv,\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{4t}\right)\phi(x),~x\in\mathbf{R}^{n},t>0

It follows from (4.3) that the solution to the CIVP for the quasilinear parabolic system (4.1) is then

(4.7)

\displaystyle\psi(x,t)=-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)\log\left(\frac{1}{4\pi at)^{n/2}}\int_{\mathrm{R}^{n}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4at}\right)\exp\left(-\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)I(y)\right)d\mu_{n}(y)\right)

Proof.

Details are given in Evans
\mathbf{[44]}

∎

As a corollary, the solution to the nonlinear Burgers equation can be found which has been studied extensively in relation to turbulence and other problems
\mathbf{[54]}
. The PPDE contains a nonlinear advection term associated with conservation laws and also the diffusion term associated with the heat equation. These two effects tend to be in conflict: the nonlinearity steepens fronts into discontinuous shocks while diffusion tends to smooth them
out. Again, a solution can be found via the Cole-Hopf transform the CIVP for the heat equation.

Corollary 4.2
.

Let
n=1
and
A>0
. The CIVP for the Burgers equation is

(4.8)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-a{\partial^{2}}{\partial x}^{2}\right)\psi(x,t)+|\psi(x,t)\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\psi(x,t)|=0,~x\in\mathbf{R},t>0

(4.9)

\displaystyle\psi(x,0)=I(x),~x\in\mathbf{R},t=0

Setting

(4.10)

\displaystyle\Xi(x,t)=\int_{\infty}^{x}\psi(y,t)dy,~~\psi(x,t)=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\Xi(,t)

(4.11)

\displaystyle\Xi(x,0)=J(x)=\int_{\infty}^{x}\psi(x,0)dy\equiv\int_{\infty}^{x}I(y)dy

then gives the quasilinear parabolic PDE

(4.12)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-a\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}\right)\Xi(x,t)+\frac{1}{2}\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\Xi(x,t)\right|^{2}=0

which is (4.1) with
n=1
and
b=\tfrac{1}{2}
. The solution is given by (4.7) as

\displaystyle\Xi(x,t)=-2a\log(u(x,t))

(4.13)

\displaystyle=-2a\log\left((4\pi at)^{1/2}\right)\int_{\bm{\mathrm{R}}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right)\exp\left(-\frac{J(x)}{2a})dy\right)

where
u(x,t)
solves the CIVP for the heat equation on
\bm{\mathrm{R}}
such that

(4.14)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial_{x}}-a\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{2}}\right)u(x,t)=0,~x\in\bm{\mathrm{R}},t>0

(4.15)

\displaystyle u(x,0)=\exp\left(-\frac{J(x)}{2a}\right)\equiv\phi(x),~x\in\bm{\mathrm{R}},t=0

Now since
u(x,t)=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\Xi(x,t)

\displaystyle u(x,t)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\Xi(x,t)=-2A\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\log(u(x,t))

\displaystyle=-2a\frac{\partial}{\partial_{x}}\log\left({(4\pi At)^{-1/2}}\int_{\bm{\mathrm{R}}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right)\exp\left(-\frac{J(x)}{2a}\right)dy\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\int_{\bm{\mathrm{R}}}\frac{|x-y|}{t}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right)\exp\left(-\frac{J(x)}{2a}\right)dy}{\int_{\bm{\mathrm{R}}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right))\exp\left(-\frac{J(x)}{2a}\right)dy}

(4.16)

\displaystyle=\frac{\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{|x-y|}{t}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right)\exp\left(-\frac{J(x)}{2a}\right)dy}{\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right)\exp\left(-\frac{J(x)}{2a}\right)dy}

It may then be possible to extend this formalism to incorporate random or stochastic initial data.

4.1.
Greens functions and equilibrium

Given a heat flow problem on a bounded domain
\bm{\mathbf{Q}}\subset\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}
, one would expect a convergence to equilibrium as
t\uparrow\infty
so that

\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}u(x,t)=0
,
u(x,t)\rightarrow u(x)
and
{\Delta}_{x}u(x)=0
. For a heat source £
f(x)
one would expect convergence to a Poisson equation
\Delta u(x)=f(x)
. This turns out to be true only when
n\geq 3
. This can be established via the Greens function for the heat kernel.

Definition 4.3
.

Given a heat kernel
h(x-y,t)
on
{\mathbf{M}}=\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}
, for all
(x,y)\in{\mathbf{R}}^{n},t\in(0,\infty)
with
x\neq y
then the Greens function
g(x-y)
is defined as

(4.17)

g(x-y)=\int_{0}^{\infty}h(x-y,t)dt<\infty

and the integral must converge.

Lemma 4.4
.

When
n\geq 3
then
g(x-y)=\int_{0}^{\infty}h(x-y,t)dt
is equivalent to the fundamental solution of the Laplace equation as
t\rightarrow\infty
so that
\Delta g(x-y)=-\delta^{3}(x-y)
. Then

(4.18)

\displaystyle g(x-y)=\int_{0}^{\infty}h(x-y,t)dt=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\exp\left(-\|x-y\|^{2}/4t\right)}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}dt=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}-1\right)}{4\pi^{n/2}\|x-y\|^{n-2}}

If
n=3
then
\Gamma(\frac{1}{2})=\pi^{1/2}
and

(4.19)

\displaystyle g(x-y)=\frac{1}{4\pi\|x-y\|}

When
n<2
, the integral
\int_{0}^{\infty}h(x-y,t)dt
does not converge.

Proof.

First, let
n\geq 3
and let
\vartheta=t^{-1}
then

(4.20)

\displaystyle g(x-y)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right)}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}dt\equiv\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\exp\left(-\frac{\vartheta\|x-y\|^{2}}{4}\right)}{(4\pi)^{n/2}}\vartheta^{n/2}\frac{d\vartheta}{\vartheta^{2}}

(4.21)

\displaystyle\equiv\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\exp\left(-\frac{\vartheta\|x-y\|^{2}}{4}\right)}{(4\pi)^{n/2}}\vartheta^{\frac{n}{2}-2}d\vartheta

Setting
\kappa=(\vartheta\|x-y\|^{2})/4
then gives

\displaystyle g(x-y)=\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{n/2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp(-\kappa)\left(\frac{4\kappa}{\|x-y\|^{2}}\right)^{\frac{n}{2}-2}\frac{4}{\|x-y\|^{2}}d\kappa

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{n/2}}\left(\frac{4\xi}{\|x-y\|^{2}}\right)^{\frac{n}{2}-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp(-\kappa)\kappa^{\frac{n}{2}-2}d\xi=\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{n/2}}\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}-1\right)\left(\frac{4\kappa}{\|x-y\|^{2}}\right)^{\frac{n}{2}-1}

(4.22)

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}-1\right)}{4\pi^{n/2}\|x-y\|^{n-2}}

which gives (1.71) when
n=3
. However, for
n\leq 2
the integral diverges logarithmically.
∎

We have now established the following theorem.

Theorem 4.5
.

Let
\mathbf{Q}\subset\mathbf{{R}}^{n}
be a compact bounded domain and let

f\in C^{2}(\mathbf{Q})
and
u\in C^{2}(\mathbf{Q})
be differentiable
functions. Let
u(x,t)
solve the Cauchy IVP for the heat equation on
{\mathbf{Q}}
such that
{\square}u(x,t)=(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}-{\Delta}_{x})u(x,t)=f(x),~x\in{\mathbf{Q}},t>0
and
u(x,0)=u(x),~x\in\mathbf{Q},t=0
with the boundary condition
u(x,t)=u(x),x\in\partial{\mathbf{Q}}
. If
n\geq 3
then in the limit
t\rightarrow\infty
, the system converges to (thermal) equilibrium so that
\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}u(x,t)=0
. The equilibrium scenario is then described by the Poisson equation with Dirichlet boundary conditions so that

(4.23)

\displaystyle{\Delta}u(x)=f(x),~x\in{\mathbf{Q}},t>0

(4.24)

\displaystyle u(x)=\phi(x),~x\in\partial{\mathbf{Q}},t=0

If
\mathbf{{Q}}=\mathbf{{B}}_{R}(0)
, an Euclidean ball of radius
R
, center zero, then the solution of (4.23) is the Poisson integral formula so that

(4.25)

\displaystyle u(x)=\int_{\mathbf{{B}}_{R}(0)}g(x-y)f(y)d^{n}y+\frac{R^{2}-\|x\|^{2}}{area(\partial\mathbf{{B}}_{1}(0))R}\int_{\partial\mathbf{{B}}_{R}(0)}\frac{\phi(y)d^{n-1}y}{\|x-y\|^{n}}

\displaystyle\equiv\int_{\mathbf{{B}}_{R}(0)}\int_{0}^{\infty}h(x-y,t)f(y)dtd^{n}y

(4.26)

\displaystyle+\frac{R^{2}-\|x\|^{2}}{area(\partial\mathbf{{B}}_{1}(0))R}\int_{\partial\mathbf{{B}}_{R}(0)}\frac{\phi(y)d^{n-1}y}{\|x-y\|^{n}},~~x\in\bm{{B}}_{R}(0),y\in\partial\mathbf{{B}}_{R}(0)

If
f(x)=0
then
u(x)
satisfies the Laplace equation
{\Delta}u(x)=0
and

(4.27)

\displaystyle u(x)=\frac{R^{2}-\|x\|^{2}}{area(\partial{\mathbf{B}}_{1}(0))R}\int_{\partial{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)}\frac{\phi(y)d^{n-1}y}{\|x-y\|^{n}}

In Section (7), this equilibrium problem is considered in relation to random boundary conditions.

5.
Randomly perturbed solutions-the Cauchy problem with random field initial data

In this section, the following stochastic Cauchy initial-value problem is studied for the parabolic heat equation on a domain
\bm{\mathbf{Q}}\subset{\mathbf{R}}^{n}
with randomised initial data.

\displaystyle{\square}\widehat{u(x,t)}

\displaystyle\equiv\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\bm{\Delta}_{x}\right)\widehat{u(x,t)}=0,~x\in{\mathbf{Q}},t>0

\displaystyle\widehat{u(x,0)}=\phi(x)+\bm{\mathscr{J}}(x),~x\in{\mathbf{Q}},t=0

where
\phi(x)\in C^{\infty}({\mathbf{Q}})
is the initial data/function and
\mathscr{J}(x)
is a classical Gaussian random scaler field such that
\mathbb{E}\llbracket{\mathscr{J}}(x)\rrbracket=0
with a regulated covariance
\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)\rrbracket=\zeta J(x,y;\ell)
for all
(x,y)\in{\mathbf{Q}}
. The correlation length is
\ell
and
\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x)\otimes\mathscr{J}(x)\rrbracket=\zeta<\infty
.
