The general perturbed solution
\widehat{u(x,t)}
can be represented as stochastic convolution integral. This leads to stochastic extensions and versions of classical results for the heat equation, so far discussed; in particular, a stochastically averaged Li-Yau differential Harnack inequality as well as moments an estimates.

5.1.
Preliminary theorems and lemmas

First, the following preliminary definitions, theorems and lemmas are given for random Gaussian fields which will be utilised throughout. Gaussian random fields (GRFS) and random perturbations are defined. (More details are given in Appendix A.)

Definition 5.1
.

Let
(\Omega,\mathbb{F},\bm{\mathcal{P}})
be a probability triplet with
\omega\in\Omega
and
x\in\bm{\mathcal{D}}\subset\mathrm{R}^{n}
. A GRSF can be defined by a map
\mathcal{M}

such that
\mathcal{M}:(\omega,x)\rightarrow\mathscr{J}(x,\omega)
, with expected values

\mathbb{E}\llbracket\bullet\rrbracket=\int_{\Omega}\llbracket\bullet\rrbracket d\bm{\mathcal{P}}
. Then:

(1)

For a Gaussian random field, with Gaussian-distributed values, the expectation vanishes and only the 2-point correlation is relevant so that for all
(x,y)\in{\mathbf{Q}}

(5.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x)\rrbracket=0

(5.2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)\rrbracket=\zeta J(x,y,\ell)

where
\zeta>0
and
\ell
is a correlation length with
J(x,y,\ell)\rightarrow 0
for

|x-y|>\ell
and
J(x,y,\ell)\rightarrow 1
as
x\rightarrow y
. The GRSF is regulated so that

\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x)\otimes\mathscr{J}(x)\rrbracket=\zeta<\infty
.

(2)

The first and second derivatives exist such that the random vector field
\mathscr{V}_{i}=\nabla_{i}\mathscr{J}(x)
and the random tensor field
\mathscr{U}_{ij}(x)=\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\mathscr{J}(x)
are also Gaussian and have the statistical properties

(5.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{V}_{i}(x)\rrbracket=0

(5.4)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{V}_{i}(x)\otimes\mathscr{V}_{j}(y)\rrbracket=\sigma\delta_{ij}S(x,y,\ell)

and

(5.5)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{U}_{ij}(x)\rrbracket=0

(5.6)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{U}_{ij}(x)\otimes\mathscr{U}_{kl}(y)\rrbracket=\chi(\delta_{ij}\delta_{kl}+\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}+\delta_{il}\delta_{jk})J(x,y,\ell)

(3)

The cross-correlations vanish so that
\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x)\otimes\nabla_{i}\mathscr{J}(x)\rrbracket=0
and
\mathbb{E}\llbracket\nabla_{i}\mathscr{J}(x)\otimes\nabla_{j}\nabla_{k}\mathscr{J}(x)\rrbracket=0
, and so on.

(4)

The integrals
\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\mathscr{J}(x)|^{p}d\mu_{n}(x)
and

\int_{\partial\mathbf{Q}}|\mathscr{J}(x)|^{p}d^{n-1}x
exist for all
p\geq 1
.(See below).

(5)

If
\psi(x)
is a smooth and continuous deterministic scalar field or function then additive and multiplicative random perturbations of
\psi(x)
are defined as

(5.7)

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi(x)}=\psi(x)+\alpha\mathscr{J}(x)

(5.8)

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi(x)}_{*}=\psi(x)\otimes\mathscr{J}(x)

for some
\alpha\geq 1
, so that
\widehat{\psi(x)}
and
\widehat{\psi(x)}_{*}
are also GRSFs.

Lemma 5.2
.

(
\underline{stochastic~Jensen~inequality}
)
Let
\mathscr{J}(x)
be a GRSF or random function existing for all
x\in\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}
and let
\Phi(\mathscr{J}(x))
be a function of
\mathscr{J}
. Then

(5.9)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket\Phi(\mathscr{J}(x))\rrbracket\geq\Phi(\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x)\rrbracket),~~\Phi~convex

(5.10)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket\Phi(\mathscr{J}(x))\rrbracket\leq\Phi(\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x)\rrbracket),~~\Phi~concave

Theorem 5.3
.

(
\underline{Fubini~Thm}
)
If
\mathscr{J}(x)
is a GRSF then the expectation of an integral of a function

\Psi(\mathscr{J}(x))
can be taken under the integral so that

(5.11)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\int_{\bm{R}^{n}}\Phi(\mathscr{J}(x))d\mu_{n}(x)\right\rrbracket=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\mathbb{E}\llbracket\Phi(\mathscr{J}(x))d\mu_{n}(x)\rrbracket\geq\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\Phi(\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket|\mathscr{J}(x)\right\rrbracket d\mu_{n}(y),~\Phi~convex

(5.12)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\int_{\bm{R}^{n}}\Phi(\mathscr{J}(x))d\mu_{n}(x)\right\rrbracket=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\mathbb{E}\llbracket\Phi(\mathscr{J}(x))d\mu_{n}(x)\rrbracket\leq\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\Phi(\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket|\mathscr{J}(x)\right\rrbracket d\mu_{n}(x),~\Phi~concave

If
x_{\xi}\in{\mathbf{Q}}_{\xi}
and
{\mathbf{Q}}=\bigcup_{\xi=1}^{M}{\mathbf{Q}}_{\xi}
is a partition of
{\mathbf{Q}}
then the Fubini Theorem for sums is

(5.13)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\sum_{i=1}^{M}\Phi(\mathscr{J}(x_{\xi}))d\mu_{n}(x)\right\rrbracket=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\mathbb{E}\llbracket\Phi(\mathscr{J}(x_{\xi}))\rrbracket

Lemma 5.4
.

(
Holder inequality for sums
)
Let
(x_{\xi})_{\xi=1}^{M}
be a set of discrete points in
{\mathbf{Q}}\subset\mathrm{R}^{n}
such that
x_{\xi}\in{\mathbf{Q}}
for all
\xi=1...M
. Let
\psi:\mathbf{Q}\rightarrow\mathrm{R}
be a deterministic field or function which is smooth and continuous let
\mathscr{J}(x)
be a GRSF
\mathscr{J}\times\Omega:\rightarrow\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}
. The fields have values
\psi(x_{\xi})
and
\mathscr{J}(x_{\xi})
at
x_{\xi}
. If
\tfrac{1}{p}+\tfrac{1}{q}=1
then the stochastic Holder inequality for sums is then

(5.14)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\sum_{\xi=1}^{M}\psi(x_{\xi})\otimes\mathscr{J}(x_{\xi})\right\rrbracket\leq\left(\sum_{\xi=1}^{M}|\psi(x_{\xi}|^{q}\right)^{1/q}\left(\sum_{\xi=1}^{M}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\mathscr{J}(x_{\xi})|^{p}\rrbracket\right)^{1/p}

Proof.

Define the norms

\displaystyle\|\psi(\bullet_{\xi})\|_{L_{p}^{\Sigma}(\mathbf{Q})}=\left(\sum_{\xi=1}^{M}|\psi(x_{\xi})|^{p}\right)^{1/p}

(5.15)

\displaystyle\|\mathscr{J}(\bullet_{\xi})\|_{L_{p}^{\Sigma}(\mathbf{Q})}=\left(\sum_{\xi=1}^{M}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\mathscr{J}(x_{\xi})|^{p}\rrbracket\right)^{1/p}

If
Y(x_{\xi})
and
\mathscr{Z}(x_{\xi})
are generic deterministic and random scalar fields or functions respectively then

(5.16)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket|Y(x_{\xi})\mathscr{Z}(x_{\xi})|\rrbracket\ \leq\frac{|Y(x)|^{q}}{q}+\frac{\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\mathscr{Z}(x)|^{p}\rrbracket}{p}

Set

(5.17)

\displaystyle Y(x_{\xi})=\frac{\psi(x_{\xi})}{\|\psi(\bullet_{\xi})\|_{L_{p}^{\Sigma}(\mathbf{Q})}},~~~~\mathscr{Z}(x_{\xi})=\frac{\mathscr{J}(x_{\xi})}{\|\mathscr{J}(\bullet_{\xi})\|_{L_{p}^{\Sigma}(\mathbf{Q})}}

Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\sum_{\xi=1}^{M}Y(x_{\xi}\mathscr{Z}(x_{xi})\right\rrbracket=\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\sum_{\xi=1}^{M}\frac{\psi(x_{\xi})}{\|\psi(\bullet_{\xi})\|_{L_{p}^{\Sigma}(\mathbf{Q})}}\frac{\mathscr{J}(x_{\xi})}{\|\mathscr{J}(\bullet_{\xi})\|_{L_{p}^{\Sigma}(\mathbf{Q})}}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\sum_{\xi=1}^{M}\frac{|\psi(x_{\xi})|^{q}}{\|q\psi(\bullet_{\xi})\|_{L_{p}^{\Sigma}(\mathbf{Q})}}+\sum_{\xi=1}^{M}|\frac{\mathscr{J}(x_{\xi})|^{p}}{p\|\mathscr{J}(\bullet_{\xi})\|_{L_{p}^{\Sigma}(\mathbf{Q})}}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\sum_{\xi=1}^{M}\frac{|\psi(x_{\xi})|^{q}}{\|q\psi(\bullet_{\xi})\|_{L_{p}^{\Sigma}(\mathbf{Q})}}+\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\sum_{\xi=1}^{M}|\frac{|\mathscr{J}(x_{\xi})|^{p}}{p\|\mathscr{J}(\bullet_{\xi})\|_{L_{p}^{\Sigma}(\mathbf{Q})}}\right\rrbracket

(5.18)

\displaystyle=\frac{(\|\psi(\bullet_{\xi})\|_{L_{p}^{\Sigma}(\mathbf{Q})})^{q}}{q\left(\|\psi(\bullet_{\xi})\|_{L_{p}^{\Sigma}(\mathbf{Q})}\right)^{q}}+\frac{(\|\mathscr{J}(\bullet_{\xi})\|_{L_{p}^{\Sigma}(\mathbf{Q})})^{p}}{p\left(\|\mathscr{J}(\bullet_{\xi})\|_{L_{p}^{\Sigma}(\mathbf{Q})}\right)^{p}}=\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{p}=1

and so (5.14) follows.
∎

The following preliminary lemma will be required, which is a Holders integral inequality or estimate.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
{\mathbf{Q}}\subset{\mathbf{R}}^{n}
and let
\psi:\mathbf{Q}\rightarrow{\mathbf{R}}
be a smooth, continuous and deterministic function. Let

\mathscr{J}(x)
be a regulated GRF with
\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x)\rrbracket=0
and
\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x)\otimes\mathscr{J}(x)\rrbracket=\xi
. The integrals
\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\mathscr{J}(x)d\mu_{n}(x)

and
\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\mathscr{J}(x)|^{p}d\mu_{n}(x)
exist and well defined as
Riemann sums over a partition of
\bm{\mathbf{Q}}
. If
\tfrac{1}{p}+\tfrac{1}{q}=1

then the Holder inequality is

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\psi(x)\otimes\mathscr{J}(x)d\mu_{n}(x)\right\rrbracket\leq\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\psi(x)|^{q}d\mu_{n}(x)\right)^{1/q}\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\mathbb{E}|\mathscr{J}(x)|^{p}d\mu_{n}(x)\right)^{1/p}

(5.19)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}[\zeta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{p/2}]v(\bm{\mathbf{Q}})\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\psi(x)|^{p/p-1}d\mu_{n}(x)\right)^{(p-1)/p}

where
V(\mathbf{Q})=\int_{\mathbf{Q}}d\mu_{n}(x)
.

Proof.

Define the
L_{p}
and
\mathscr{L}_{p}
norms

(5.20)

\displaystyle\|\psi(\bullet)\|_{L_{p}}=\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\psi(x)|^{p}d\mu_{n}(x)\right)^{1/p}

(5.21)

\displaystyle\|\mathscr{J}(\bullet)\|_{\mathscr{L}_{p}}=\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\mathscr{J}(x)|^{p}\rrbracket d\mu_{n}(x)\right)^{1/p}

then one must establish that

(5.22)

\displaystyle\|\psi(\bullet)\|_{L_{p}}=\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\psi(x)|^{p}d\mu_{n}(x)\right)^{1/p}

(5.23)

\displaystyle\|\mathscr{J}(\bullet)\|_{\mathscr{L}_{p}}=\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\mathscr{J}(x)|^{p}\rrbracket d\mu_{n}(x)\right)^{1/p}

(5.24)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\psi(x)\otimes\mathscr{J}(x)d\mu_{n}(x)\right\rrbracket\leq\|\psi(\bullet)\|_{L_{q}}\circ\|\mathscr{J}(\bullet)\|_{\mathscr{L}_{p}}

Next, Young’s inequality states that for two functions
Y(x),Z(x)
and

\tfrac{1}{p}+\tfrac{1}{q}=1
one has
Y(x)Z(x)\leq\frac{|Y(x)|^{q}}{q}+\frac{|Z(x)|^{p}}{p}
. If
Z(x)
is replaced by a GRF
\mathscr{Y}(x)
then

(5.25)

\displaystyle Y(x)\mathscr{Z}(x)\leq\frac{|Y(x)|^{q}}{q}+\frac{|\mathscr{Z}(x)|^{p}}{p}

and the expectation is

(5.26)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket Y(x)\mathscr{Z}(x)\right\rrbracket\leq\frac{|Y(x)|^{q}}{q}+\frac{\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket|\mathscr{Z}(x)|^{p}\right\rrbracket}{p}

Now let

(5.27)

\displaystyle Y(x)=\frac{\psi(x)}{\|\psi(\bullet)\|_{L_{q}(\mathbf{Q})}},~~~\mathscr{Z}(x)=\frac{\mathscr{J}(x)}{\|\mathscr{J}(\bullet)\|_{\mathscr{L}_{p}(\mathbf{Q})}}

Equation (5.24) then becomes

(5.28)

\displaystyle Y(x)\mathscr{Z}(x)\leq\frac{|\psi(x)|^{q}}{q\|\psi(\bullet)\|^{q}_{L_{q}(\mathbf{Q})}}+\frac{|\mathscr{J}(x)|^{p}}{p\|\mathscr{J}(\bullet)\|^{p}_{\mathscr{L}_{p}(\mathbf{Q})}}

with expectation

(5.29)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket Y(x)\mathscr{Z}(x)\rrbracket\leq\frac{|\psi(x)|^{q}}{q\|\psi(\bullet)\|^{q}_{L_{q}(\mathbf{Q})}}+\frac{\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\mathscr{J}(x)|^{p}\rrbracket}{p\|\mathscr{J}(\bullet)\|^{p}_{\mathscr{L}_{p}(\mathbf{Q})}}

Integrating and using the Fubini Theorem

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\mathbb{E}\llbracket Y(x)\mathscr{Z}(x)\rrbracket d\mu_{n}(x)=\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\int_{\mathbf{Q}}Y(x)\mathscr{Z}(x)d\mu_{n}(x)\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\frac{|\psi(x)|^{q}}{q\|\psi(\bullet)\|^{q}_{L_{q}(\mathbf{Q})}}\mu_{n}(x)+\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\frac{\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\mathscr{J}(x)|^{p}\rrbracket}{p\|\mathscr{J}(\bullet)\|^{p}_{\mathscr{L}_{p}(\mathbf{Q})}}d\mu_{n}(x)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{q\|\psi(\bullet)\|^{q}_{L_{q}(\mathbf{Q})}}\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\psi(x)|^{q}d\mu_{n}(x)+\frac{1}{p\|\mathscr{J}(\bullet)\|^{p}_{\mathscr{L}_{p}(\mathbf{Q})}}\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\mathscr{J}(x)|^{p}\rrbracket d\mu_{n}(x)

(5.30)

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\|\psi(\bullet)\|^{q}_{L_{q}(\mathbf{Q})}}{q\|\psi(\bullet)\|^{q}_{L_{q}(\mathbf{Q})}}+\frac{\|\mathscr{J}(\bullet)\|^{p}_{\mathscr{L}_{p}(\mathbf{Q})}}{p\|\mathscr{J}(\bullet)\|^{p}_{\mathscr{L}_{p}(\mathbf{Q})}}=\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{p}=1

Now using (5.26)

(5.31)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\frac{\psi(x)}{\|\psi(\bullet)\|_{L_{q}(\mathbf{Q})}}\frac{\mathscr{J}(x)}{\|\mathscr{J}(\bullet)\|_{\mathscr{L}_{p}(\mathbf{Q})}}d\mu_{n}(x)\right\rrbracket\leq 1

and so the Holder inequality (5.19) is established. The stochastic integral or norm is

\displaystyle\|\mathscr{J}(\bullet)\|_{\mathscr{L}_{p}}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\mathscr{J}(x)|^{p}\rrbracket d\mu_{n}(x)\right)^{1/p}=\left(\frac{1}{2}[\zeta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{p/2}]\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}d\mu_{n}(x)\right)^{1/p}

(5.32)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{2}[\zeta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{p/2}]v({\mathbf{Q}})\right)^{1/p}

and
q=p/(p-1)
so (5.19) is established.
∎

Theorem 5.6
.

The moments
\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}^{p}|\rrbracket
are estimated
(with
u(x,t)=0
as and are smoothed out or dissipated since

(5.33)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\bm{\mathcal{M}}_{p}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}^{p}|\rrbracket=0

The moments estimate can also be deduced via the Riemann-Stieltjes sum representation
and the Holder inequality for sums the p-moments are

Proof.

Let
{\mathbf{Q}}=\bigcup_{\xi=1}^{M}{\mathbf{Q}}_{\xi}
be a partition of

{\mathbf{Q}}\subset{\mathbf{R}}^{n}
with
\omega\in\Omega
, and

\tfrac{1}{p}+\tfrac{1}{q}=1
. The heat kernel is
h(x-y,t)
. As a Riemann-Stieltjes sum

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}\rrbracket\leq\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)\otimes{\mathscr{J}}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\lim_{v(\mathbf{Q}_{\xi})\uparrow 0}\lim_{M\uparrow\infty}\int_{\Omega}\left|\sum_{\xi=1}^{M}h(x-y_{\xi},t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y_{\xi};\omega)v(\mathbf{Q}_{\xi})\right|^{p}d\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\lim_{v(\mathbf{Q}_{\xi})\uparrow 0}\lim_{M\uparrow\infty}\int_{\Omega}\left|\sum_{\xi=1}^{M}h(x-y_{\xi},t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y_{\xi};\omega)|v(\mathbf{Q}_{\xi})|^{\tfrac{1}{p}+\tfrac{1}{q}}\right|^{p}d\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\lim_{v({\mathbf{Q}}_{\xi})\uparrow 0}\lim_{M\uparrow\infty}\int_{\Omega}\left|\sum_{\xi=1}^{M}h(x-y_{\xi},t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y_{\xi};\omega)|v({\mathbf{Q}}_{\xi})|^{\tfrac{1}{p}}|v({\mathbf{Q}}_{\xi})|^{\tfrac{1}{q}}|\right|^{p}d\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}(\omega)\leq

\displaystyle=\lim_{v({\mathbf{Q}}_{\xi})\uparrow 0}\lim_{M\uparrow\infty}\int_{\Omega}\left|\underbrace{\left(\sum_{\xi=1}^{M}|h(x-y_{\xi},t)|^{q}|v({\mathbf{Q}}_{\xi})|\right)^{1/q}\otimes\left(\sum_{\xi=1}^{M}|\mathscr{J}(y_{\xi};\omega)|^{p}|v({\mathbf{Q}}_{\xi})|\right)^{1/p}}_{via~Holder~ineq.~for~sums}\right|^{p}d\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\lim_{v({\mathbf{Q}}_{\xi})\uparrow 0}\lim_{M\uparrow\infty}\int_{\Omega}\left(\sum_{\xi=1}^{M}|h(x-y_{\xi},t)|^{q}|v({\mathbf{Q}}_{\xi})|\right)^{p/q}\otimes\left(\sum_{\xi=1}^{M}|\mathscr{J}(y_{\xi};\omega)|^{p}|v({\mathbf{Q}}_{\xi})|\right)d\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\lim_{v({\mathbf{Q}}_{\xi})\uparrow 0}\lim_{M\uparrow\infty}\left(\sum_{\xi=1}^{M}|h(x-y_{\xi},t)|^{q}|v({\mathbf{Q}}_{\xi})|\right)^{p/q}\otimes\left(\sum_{\xi=1}^{M}\int_{\Omega}|\mathscr{J}(y_{\xi};\omega)|^{p}d\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}(\omega)|v({\mathbf{Q}}_{\xi})|\right)

\displaystyle\equiv\lim_{v({\mathbf{Q}}_{\xi})\uparrow 0}\lim_{M\uparrow\infty}\left(\sum_{\xi=1}^{M}|h(x-y_{\xi},t)|^{q}|v({\mathbf{Q}}_{\xi})|\right)^{p/q}\otimes\left(\sum_{\xi=1}^{M}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\mathscr{J}(y_{\xi};\omega)|^{p}\big\rrbracket|v({\mathbf{Q}}_{\xi})|\right)

\displaystyle\equiv\lim_{v({\mathbf{Q}}_{\xi})\uparrow 0}\lim_{M\uparrow\infty}\frac{1}{2}[\zeta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{p/2}]\left(\sum_{\xi=1}^{M}|h(x-y_{\xi},t)|^{q}|v(\mathbf{Q}_{\xi})|\right)^{p/q}

\displaystyle\equiv\lim_{v({\mathbf{Q}}_{\xi})\uparrow 0}\lim_{M\uparrow\infty}\frac{1}{2}[\zeta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{p/2}]v(\mathbf{Q})\left(\sum_{\xi=1}^{M}|h(x-y_{\xi},t)|^{q}|v(\mathbf{Q}_{\xi})|\right)^{p/q}

(5.34)

\displaystyle\equiv\lim_{v(\mathbf{Q}_{\xi})\uparrow 0}\lim_{M\uparrow\infty}\frac{1}{2}[\zeta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{p/2}]v(\mathbf{Q})\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x-y,t)|^{p}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{p/q}

which agrees exactly with (5.32)
∎

The final preliminary lemma is

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
\Lambda:\mathbf{Q}\rightarrow{\mathbf{R}}
and and let
p\geq 2
. If
\tfrac{p}{p-1}+\tfrac{\ell}{\ell-1}=1
then

(5.35)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\Lambda(x)|^{\frac{p}{p-1}}d\mu_{n}(x)\leq\bigg(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\Lambda(x)d\mu_{n}(x)\bigg)^{\frac{p}{p-1}}(v(\mathbf{Q}))^{\frac{\ell}{\ell-1}}\bigg)

(5.36)

\displaystyle\bigg(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\Lambda(x)|^{\frac{p}{p-1}}d\mu_{n}(x)\bigg)^{p-1}\leq\bigg(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\Lambda(x)d\mu_{n}(x)\bigg)^{p}(v(\mathbf{Q}))^{\frac{\ell(p-1)}{\ell-1}}\bigg)

Proof.

Let
\Psi(x)=|\Lambda(x)|^{\frac{p}{p-1}}
and let
\tfrac{p}{p-1}=\tfrac{\ell}{\ell-1}
then
using the Holder inequality

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\Lambda(x)|^{\frac{p}{p-1}}d\mu_{n}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{Q}}(\Psi(x).1)d\mu_{n}(x)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int|\Psi(x)|^{\frac{p-1}{p}}\right)^{\frac{p}{p-1}}\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}1.d\mu_{n}(x)\right)^{\frac{\ell}{\ell-1}}=\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\Psi(x)|^{\frac{p-1}{p}}d\mu_{n}(x)\right)^{\frac{p}{p-1}}({v}(\mathbf{Q})^{\frac{\ell}{\ell-1}}

(5.37)

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}f(x)d\mu_{n}(x)\right)^{\frac{p}{p-1}}({v}(\mathbf{Q}))^{\frac{\ell}{\ell-1}}

where
{v}(\mathbf{Q})=\int_{\mathbf{Q}}d\mu_{n}(x)<\infty
.
∎

5.2.
stochastic perturbations and estimates

The main theorem for the general global stochastic Cauchy problem with random initial data is then as follows.

Theorem 5.8
.

The global Cauchy initial value problem for the homogenous heat equation, with Gaussian randomized initial data is

(5.38)

\displaystyle{\square}\widehat{u(x,t)}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial_{x}}-{\Delta}\right)\widehat{u(x,t)}=0,~(x\in\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n},t\in(0,\infty))

(5.39)

\displaystyle\widehat{u(x,0)}=\phi(x)+\mathscr{J}(x),~(x\in\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n},t=0)

where
\mathscr{J}(x)
is a Gaussian random scalar field (GRSF)existing for all
x\in\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}
. The GRSF has the statistical properties:

(1)

The stochastic expectation vanishes so that

\mathbb{E}\llbracket[\mathscr{J}(x)\rrbracket=0
. The covariance is

(5.40)

\mathbb{K}(\mathscr{J}(x)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)\otimes y)=\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)\rrbracket=\zeta J(x,y;\ell)

The GRF is regulated so that
\mathbb{K}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x)\otimes\mathscr{J}(x)\rrbracket=\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x)\otimes\mathscr{J}(x)\rrbracket=\zeta<\infty.

(2)

The stochastic integrals and convolution integrals exist, namely

(5.41)

\left|\int_{\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}}\mathscr{J}(x)d\mu_{n}(x)\right|,~~\left|\int_{\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|

where
k(x-y)
is any kernel dependent on the separation
\|x-y\|
, which includes the heat kernel such that
k(x-y)=h(x-y,t)
. Integrals over GRFs are also GRFs. Their expectations vanish by Fubini’s theorem and the Gaussian property.

(5.42)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\int_{\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}}\mathscr{J}(x)d\mu_{n}(x)\right\rrbracket=\int_{\mathrm{R}^{n}}\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x)\big\rrbracket d\mu_{n}(x)=0

(5.43)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg\llbracket\int_{\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}}\mathscr{J}(y)K(x-y)d\mu_{n}(y)\bigg\rrbracket=\int_{\mathrm{R}^{n}}\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket\mathscr{J}(y)\big\rrbracket h(x-y,t)d\mu_{n}(y)=0

(3)

The initial Cauchy random data is now correlated as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket\widehat{u(z,0)}\otimes\widehat{u(z^{\prime},0)}\rrbracket=\phi(x)\phi(y)+\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)\rrbracket

(5.44)

\displaystyle=\phi(x)\phi(y)+\zeta J(x,y;\ell)

The solution of the Cauchy problem is then

\displaystyle\widehat{u(x,t)}

\displaystyle=(4\pi t)^{-n/2}\int_{\mathrm{R}^{n}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{4t}\right)\widehat{\phi(y)}d\mu_{n}(y)

\displaystyle=(4\pi t)^{-n/2}\int_{\mathrm{R}^{n}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{4t}\right)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

\displaystyle+{(4\pi t)^{-n/2}}\int_{\mathrm{R}^{n}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{4t}\right)\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

(5.45)

\displaystyle\equiv\int_{\mathrm{R}^{n}}h(x-y,t)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)+\int_{\mathrm{R}^{n}}h(x-y,t)\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\equiv u(x,t)+\mathscr{U}(x,t)

Proof.

The heat kernel
h(x-y,t)
solves the heat equation so that
\square h(x-y,t)=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}h(x-y,t)-{\Delta}h(x-y,t)=0
. The heat kernel
h(x-y,t)
is also smooth and infinitely differentiable so that integrals and derivative can be interchanged. Then from (5.44)

(5.46)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\widehat{u(x,t)}=\int_{\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}h(x-y,t)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)+\int_{\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

(5.47)

\displaystyle{\Delta}_{x}\widehat{u(x,t)}=\int_{\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}}\Delta_{x}h(x-y,t)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)+\int_{\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}}\Delta_{x}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

so that

\displaystyle{\square}\widehat{u(x,t)}=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-{\Delta}_{x}\right)\widehat{u(x,t)}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathrm{R}}\big[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}h(x-y,t)+{\Delta}_{x}h(x-y,t)\big]\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathrm{R}}\big[\frac{\partial}{\partial_{x}}h(x-y,t)+\Delta_{x}h(x-y,t)\big]\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

(5.48)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathrm{R}^{n}}{\square}h(x-y,t)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)+\int_{\mathrm{R}^{n}}{\square}h(x-y,t)\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)=0

since
\square u(x,t)=0
. Hence (5.45) solves (5.38)
∎

Lemma 5.9
.

The stochastic average of (5.45) is
\mathbb{E}\llbracket\widehat{u(x,t)}\rrbracket=u(x,t)
since
\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x)\rrbracket=0
.

From (5.44)

Proof.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket\widehat{u(x,t)}\rrbracket=\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}\int_{\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{4t}\right)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}\mathbb{E}\bigg\llbracket\int_{\mathrm{R}^{n}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{4t}\right)\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\bigg\rrbracket

\displaystyle={(4\pi t)^{-n/2}}\int_{\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}}\exp(\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{4t}\right)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{3/2}}\int_{\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}}\exp(\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{4t}\right)\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket\mathscr{J}(y)\big\rrbracket d\mu_{n}(y)

(5.49)

\displaystyle={(4\pi t)^{-n/2}}\int_{\mathrm{R}^{n}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{4t}\right)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

In terms of a stochastic Riemann-Stieljes sum

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket\widehat{u(x,t)}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}h(x-y,t)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

\displaystyle+\lim_{v({\mathbf{Q}}_{\xi})\uparrow 0}\lim_{M\uparrow\infty}\sum_{\xi=1}^{M}\int_{\Omega}{(4\pi t)^{-n/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y_{\xi};\omega)v({\mathbf{Q}}_{\xi})d\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}h(x-y,t)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

(5.50)

\displaystyle+\lim_{v({\mathbf{Q}}_{\xi})\uparrow 0}\lim_{M\uparrow\infty}\sum_{\xi=1}^{M}{(4\pi t)^{-n/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right)\int_{\Omega}\mathscr{J}(y_{\xi};\omega)d\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}(\omega)v({\mathbf{Q}}_{\xi})=0

since
\int_{\Omega}\mathscr{J}(y_{\xi};\omega)d\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}(\omega)=0

∎

Remark 5.10
.

It is well established that a GRF
\mathscr{J}(x)
can be ’Gaussian smoothed’ on a scale

\zeta
with respect to a Gaussian kernel
k(x-y,\zeta)
such that

(5.51)

\displaystyle\mathscr{J}(x,\zeta)=\int_{\mathbf{Q}}k(x-y,\zeta)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)=\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{\zeta^{2}}\right)\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

and this stochastic convolution integral is well defined as a Riemann sum.(Appendix A.) As an example, in cosmology, Gaussian random fields can be used to represent density
fluctuations in the early (expanding) universe
\mathbf{[30,31]}
. A GRF
\mathscr{J}(r)
can be Gaussian smoothed on a (comoving) scale
R
such that

(5.52)

\displaystyle\mathscr{J}(r,R)=(2\pi R^{2})^{-3/2}\int\exp\left(-\frac{\|r-r^{\prime}\|^{2}}{2R^{2}}\right)\mathscr{J}(r^{\prime})d^{3}r^{\prime}

For the solution (5.44), the heat kernel
h(x-y,t)
is also a Gaussian and so increasingly Gaussian smooths the GRF
\mathscr{J}
as t increases. The stochastic convolution integral gives a time-dependent random field
\mathscr{G}(x,t)

(5.53)

\displaystyle\mathscr{G}(x,t)=\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)=\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}\int_{\bm{\mathbf{Q}}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{4t}\right)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y),~~t>0

which solves the SCIVP
{\square}\mathscr{G}(x,t)=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mathscr{G}(x,t)-\Delta_{x}\mathscr{G}(x,t)=0
with initial random data
\mathscr{G}(x,0)=\mathscr{G}(x)
. The heat kernel smooths the initial-value random field to zero so that

(5.54)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\uparrow 0}\mathscr{G}(x,t)=\lim_{t\uparrow 0}\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{4t}\right)\otimes\mathscr{J}d\mu_{n}(y)=0

Lemma 5.11
.

Since the heat equation is a linear PDE, the expectation of the randomly perturbed equation
gives the deterministic heat equation.

(5.55)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-{\Delta}\right)\widehat{u(x,t)}\right\rrbracket=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-{\Delta}\right)u(x,t)=0

Proof.

Since
\mathbb{E}\llbracket\widehat{u(x,t)}\rrbracket=u(x,t)
then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-{\Delta}\right)\widehat{u(x,t)}\right\rrbracket=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-{\Delta}\right)\mathbb{E}\llbracket\widehat{u(x,t)}\rrbracket

(5.56)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-{\Delta}\right)u(x,t)=0

One can also take the derivatives since
\mathbb{E}\llbracket\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\widehat{u(x,t)}\rrbracket=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u(x,t)
and
\mathbb{E}\llbracket{\Delta}_{x}\widehat{u(x,t)}\rrbracket={\Delta}_{x}u(x,t)

∎

Lemma 5.12
.

Using (2.63), the randomly perturbed solution the SCIVP can be expressed in terms of the
eigenvalues and eigenfunctions of the Laplacian such that

(5.57)

\displaystyle\widehat{u(x,t)}=\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\exp(-\vartheta_{k}t)\chi_{k}(x)\chi_{k}(y)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)+\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\exp(-\vartheta_{k}t)\chi_{k}(x)\chi_{k}(y)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

Proof.

The derivatives are

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\widehat{u(x,t)}=\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\exp(-\vartheta_{k}t)(-\vartheta_{k}\chi_{k}(x))\chi_{k}(y)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

(5.58)

\displaystyle+\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\exp(-\vartheta_{k}t)(-\vartheta_{k}\chi_{k}(x))\chi_{k}(y)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

\displaystyle{\Delta}_{x}\widehat{u(x,t)}

\displaystyle\equiv\nabla^{2}\widehat{u(x,t)}=\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\exp(-\vartheta_{k}t)({\Delta}_{x}\chi_{k}(x))\chi_{k}(y)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

\displaystyle+\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}exp(-\vartheta_{k}t)({\Delta}_{x}\chi_{k}(x))\chi_{k}(y)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}exp(-\vartheta_{k}t)(-\vartheta_{k}\chi_{k}(x))\chi_{k}(y)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

(5.59)

\displaystyle+\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}exp(-\vartheta_{k}t)(-\vartheta_{k}\chi_{k}(x))\chi_{k}(y)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

since
{\Delta}_{x}\chi_{k}(x)=-\vartheta_{k}\chi_{k}(x)
. Hence

(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}-{\Delta}_{x})u(x,t)=0
as required.
∎

The
L_{p}
norm of the stochastic convolution integral dissipates as
t\rightarrow\infty
.

Lemma 5.13
.

Let
{\mathbf{Q}}\subset\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}
. Given the stochastic convolution integral solution of the SCIVP on
{\mathbf{Q}}
then

(5.60)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\|\widehat{u(x,t)}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbf{Q})}=\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\left(\|\widehat{u(x,t)}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbf{Q})}\right)^{p}=0

(5.61)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\llbracket\|\widehat{u(x,t)}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbf{Q})}\rrbracket=\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\llbracket\left(\|\widehat{u(x,t)}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbf{Q})}\right)^{p}\rrbracket=0

where
\|\mathscr{J}(x)\|_{L_{p}(\mathbf{Q}}<\infty

Proof.

Young’s inequality states that for a convolution
f*g
of two functions with
1+\tfrac{1}{p}=\tfrac{1}{q}+\tfrac{1}{r}
then

(5.62)

\|f*g\|_{L_{p}}\leq\|f\|_{L_{r}}\|g\|_{L_{q}}

so that

(5.63)

\left\|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(\bullet-y,t)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right\|_{L_{p}}\leq\|h(\bullet-y,t)\|_{L_{r}}\|\phi(y)\|_{L_{q}}

Then

\displaystyle\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\|\widehat{u(x,t)}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbf{Q})}

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p}d\mu_{n}(x)\right)^{1/p}

\displaystyle+\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p}d\mu_{n}(x)\right)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\equiv\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\left\|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(\bullet-y,t)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right\|_{L_{p}(\mathbf{Q})}+\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\left\|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(\bullet-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right\|_{L_{p}(\mathbf{Q})}

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\|h(\bullet-y,t)\|_{L_{r}(\mathbf{Q}}\|\phi(y)\|_{L_{q}(\mathbf{Q})}+\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\|h(\bullet-y,t)\|_{L_{r}}\otimes\|\mathscr{J}(y)\|_{L_{q}(\mathbf{Q})}

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\|h(\bullet-y,t)\|_{L_{r}}\big(\|\phi(y)\|_{L_{q}(\mathbf{Q})}+\|\mathscr{J}(y)\|_{L_{q}(\mathbf{Q})}\big)

(5.64)

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\left(\frac{1}{q^{n/2q}(4\pi t)^{\tfrac{n}{2}(1-t\frac{1}{p})}}\right)\big(\|\phi(y)\|_{L_{q}(\mathbf{Q})}+\|\mathscr{J}(y)\|_{L_{q}(\mathbf{Q})}\big)=0

Taking the expectation is superfluous, however the result is equivalent. Since

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket\|\mathscr{J}(\bullet)\|\rrbracket_{L_{p}({\mathbf{Q}})}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\mathscr{J}(x)|^{p}d\mu_{n}(x)\right)^{1/p}\right\rrbracket\equiv\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x)|^{p}\big\rrbracket d\mu_{n}(x)\right)^{1/p}

(5.65)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{2}v({\mathbf{Q}})[\zeta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{p/2}]\right)^{1/p}

one has

\displaystyle\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\|\widehat{u(x,t)}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbf{Q})}

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p}d\mu_{n}(x)\right)^{1/p}

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p}d\mu_{n}(x)\right)^{1/p}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle\equiv\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\left\|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(\bullet-y,t)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right\|_{L_{p}(\mathbf{Q})}+\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left\|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(\bullet-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right\|_{L_{p}(\mathbf{Q})}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\|h(\bullet-y,t)\|_{L_{r}(\mathbf{Q}}\|\phi(y)\|_{L_{q}(\mathbf{Q})}+\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\|h(\bullet-y,t)\|_{L_{r}}\otimes\mathbb{E}\llbracket\|\mathscr{J}(y)\|_{L_{q}(\mathbf{Q})}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\|h(\bullet-\bullet,t)\|_{L_{r}}\big(\|\phi(\bullet)\|_{L_{q}(\mathbf{Q})}+\mathbb{E}\llbracket\|\mathscr{J}(\bullet)\|_{L_{q}(\mathbf{Q})}\rrbracket\|_{L_{q}(\mathbf{Q})}\big)

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\left(\frac{1}{q^{n/2q}(4\pi t)^{\tfrac{n}{2}(1-t\frac{1}{p})}}\right)\big(\|\phi(\bullet)\|_{L_{q}(\mathbf{Q})}+\mathbb{E}\llbracket\|\mathscr{J}(\bullet)\|_{L_{q}(\mathbf{Q})}\rrbracket\big)=0

(5.66)

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\left(\frac{1}{q^{n/2q}(4\pi t)^{\tfrac{n}{2}(1-t\frac{1}{p})}}\right)\big(\|\phi(\bullet)\|_{L_{q}(\mathbf{Q})}+\left(\frac{1}{2}v(\mathbf{Q})[\zeta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{p/2}]\right)^{1/p}\big)=0

∎

Lemma 5.14
.

Let
u\in C^{\infty}(\mathrm{R}^{n}\times(0,\infty))
satisfy Lemma (2.11) so that

|\nabla u(x,t)|\leq(c(n)/\sqrt{t})\|\phi\|_{\infty}
. For the Cauchy problem with randomized initial data
\widehat{u(x,0)}=\phi(x)+\mathscr{J}(x)
then

(5.67)

\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|\rrbracket\leq\frac{c(n)}{\sqrt{t}}\|\phi\|_{\infty}

Proof.

The solution is given by (5.44) so that

\displaystyle{\nabla}\widehat{u(x,t)}={\nabla}u(x,t)+\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}\int_{\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}}{\nabla}_{x}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{4t}\right)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

(5.68)

\displaystyle={\nabla}u(x,t)-{(4\pi t)^{-n/2}}\int_{\mathrm{R}^{n}}\frac{\|x-y\|}{2t}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{2t}\right)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

Hence, taking the expectation

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket|{\nabla}\widehat{u(x,t)}|\rrbracket={\nabla}u(x,t)+\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}\int_{\mathrm{R}^{n}}{\nabla}_{x}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{4t}\right)\mathbb{E}\llbracket\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)\rrbracket d\mu_{n}(y)

(5.69)

\displaystyle={\nabla}u(x,t)\leq\frac{c(n)}{\sqrt{t}}\|\phi\|_{\infty}

∎

Lemma 5.15
.

If Thm 2.14 holds such that
\sup|u|_{\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}}\times(0,\infty)=\sup|\phi|_{\mathrm{R}^{n}}
and
u(x,t)\leq A\exp(a|x|^{2})
then for the Cauchy problem one has

(5.70)

\sup\mathbb{E}\llbracket\widehat{u(x,t)}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{R}^{n}}\times(0,\infty)=\sup\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{\phi}|\rrbracket_{\mathrm{R}^{n}}

Proof.

Writing
\widehat{u(x,t)}=u(x,t)+\mathscr{U}(x,t)
then
\mathbb{E}\llbracket\widehat{u(x,t)}\rrbracket=u(x,t)\leq A\exp(a|x|^{2})
since
\mathbb{E}\llbracket{\mathscr{U}(x,t)}\rrbracket=0
.

(5.71)

\sup\mathbb{E}\llbracket u(x,t)+\mathscr{U}(x,t)\rrbracket_{\mathrm{R}^{n}\times(0,\infty)}=\sup\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\phi+\mathscr{J}(x)|\rrbracket_{\mathrm{R}^{n}}=\sup|u|_{\mathrm{R}^{n}\times(0,\infty)}=\sup|\phi|_{\mathrm{R}^{n}}

∎

The following preliminary lemma appears in Davies
\mathbf{[55]}
.

Lemma 5.16
.

Let
({\mathbf{M}},\bm{g})
be a Riemannian manifold and let

{\mathbf{Q}},{\mathbf{Q}}^{\prime}
be two compact sets or domains in

{\mathbf{M}}
. If
v(\mathbf{Q}),v(\mathbf{Q}^{\prime})
are the volumes of
{\mathbf{Q}}
and
{\mathbf{Q}}^{\prime}
. If

d^{2}({\mathbf{Q}},{\mathbf{Q}}^{\prime})
is the distance between the centres of

({\mathbf{Q}},{\mathbf{Q}}^{\prime})
then the following estimate holds

(5.72)

\displaystyle\iint_{{\mathbf{Q}},\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}}h(x-y,t)d\mu_{n}(x)d\mu_{n}(y)\leq\sqrt{v({\mathbf{Q!}})v({\mathbf{Q}}^{\prime})}\exp\left(-\frac{d^{2}({\mathbf{Q}},{\mathbf{Q}}^{\prime})}{4t}\right)

On Euclidean space
{\mathbf{M}}={\mathbf{R}}^{n}
the distance
d^{2}({\mathbf{Q}},{\mathbf{Q}}^{\prime})

is just the usual Euclidean distance between the centres of the domains. If

{\mathbf{Q}}=\bm{\mathbf{Q}}^{\prime}
and the centres coincide then

(5.73)

\displaystyle\iint_{{\mathbf{Q}},{\mathbf{Q}}}h(x-y,t)d\mu_{n}(x)d\mu_{n}(y)\leq\sqrt{v({\mathbf{Q}})v({\mathbf{Q}})}=v(\mathbf{Q})

The following lemma is then the stochastic version of Lemma 5.16 for
L_{2}
estimates of the solution of the heat equation.

Theorem 5.17
.

Let
u(x,t)
solve the Cauchy IVP for the heat equation such that

{\square}u(x,t)=(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-{\Delta})u(x,t)=0,x\in{\mathbf{Q}},t>0
with initial data
u(x,0)=\phi(x),x\in{\mathbf{Q}},t=0
. The
L_{2}({\mathbf{Q}})
norms satisfy the estimate
\|u(\bullet,t)\|_{L_{2}({\mathbf{Q}})}\leq\|\phi(\bullet)\|_{L_{2}({\mathbf{Q}})}
or equivalently
(\|u(\bullet,t)\|_{L_{2}({\mathbf{Q}})})^{2}\leq(\|\phi(\bullet)\|_{L_{2}({\mathbf{Q}})})^{2}
. If
\widehat{u(x,t)}
solves the randomly perturbed heat equation
{\square}\widehat{u(x,t)}=0
with random initial data
\widehat{\phi(x)}=\phi(x)+\mathscr{J}(x)
then
\widehat{u(x,t)}=\int_{\bm{\mathbf{Q}}}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)
the following stochastically averaged estimate

(5.74)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket(\|\widehat{(u(\bullet,t)}\|_{L_{2}({\mathbf{Q}})})^{2}\rrbracket\leq\mathbb{E}\llbracket(\|\widehat{\phi(\bullet)}\|_{L_{2}({\mathbf{Q}})})^{2}\rrbracket

or equivalently

(5.75)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\int_{\bm{\mathbf{Q}}}|\widehat{(u(x,t)}|^{2}d\mu_{n}(x)\right\rrbracket\leq\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\int_{\bm{\mathbf{Q}}}|\widehat{(\phi(x)}|^{2}d\mu_{n}(x)\right\rrbracket

or

(5.76)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\left|\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}h(x-y,t)\otimes\widehat{\phi(x)}d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{2}d\mu_{n}(x)\right)^{1/2}\right\rrbracket\leq\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\widehat{\phi(x)}|^{2}d\mu_{n}(x)\right)^{1/2}\right\rrbracket

holds provided that

(5.77)

\displaystyle\iint_{{\mathbf{Q}},v{\mathbf{Q}}}h(x-y,t)d\mu_{n}(x)d\mu_{n}(y)\leq\sqrt{v({\mathbf{Q}})v({\mathbf{Q}})}=v(\bm{\mathbf{Q}})

which is Lemma (5.16)

Proof.

First, apply the Cauchy Schwarz inequality to
\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}h(x-y,t)d\mu_{n}(y)
to derive the estimate

\displaystyle\int_{\bm{\mathbf{Q}}}h(x-y,t)d\mu_{n}(y)\equiv\int_{\bm{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x-y,t)\times\mathds{1}|d\mu_{n}(y)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(x)\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\mathds{1}|^{2})d\mu_{n}(x)\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(x)\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}d\mu_{n}(x)\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(x)\right)^{1/2}(v({\mathbf{Q}}))^{1/2}\leq\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(x)\right)^{1/2}(v({\mathbf{Q}}))

(5.78)

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(x)\right)(v({\mathbf{Q}}))

Then if (5.74) holds

(5.79)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\mathbb{E}\llbracket(\|\widehat{(u(\bullet,t)}\|_{L_{2}({\mathbf{Q}})})^{2}\equiv\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\widehat{(u(x,t)}|^{2}d\mu_{n}(x)\right\rrbracket\leq\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\phi(x)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(x)+\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\mathscr{J}(x)|^{2}\rrbracket d\mu_{n}(x)

or

(5.80)

\displaystyle(\|u(\bullet,t)\|_{L_{2}({\mathbf{Q}})})^{2}+\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{2}\right\rrbracket d\mu_{n}(x)\leq\left(\|\phi(\bullet)\|_{L_{2}({\mathbf{Q}})}\right)^{2}+\zeta v({\mathbf{Q}})

Applying Cauchy Schwartz to the stochastic integral on the rhs

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{2}\right\rrbracket d\mu_{n}(x)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)\left(\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\mathscr{J}(y)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right\rrbracket\right)d\mu_{n}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\mathscr{J}(y)|^{2}\rrbracket d\mu_{n}(y)\right)d\mu_{n}(x)

(5.81)

\displaystyle=\zeta\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)v(\bm{\mathbf{Q}})d\mu_{n}(x)

The estimate now becomes

(5.82)

\displaystyle\zeta\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)v({\mathbf{Q}})d\mu_{n}(x)\leq\left(\|\phi(\bullet)\|_{L_{2}({\mathbf{Q}})}\right)^{2}+\zeta v({\mathbf{Q}})

so it is required that

(5.83)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)v({\mathbf{Q}})d\mu_{n}(x)\leq+v({\mathbf{Q}})

Now the estimate can be expressed as

(5.84)

\displaystyle\iint_{\bm{\mathbf{Q}}}h(x-y,t)d\mu_{n}(x)d\mu_{n}(y)\leq\int_{\bm{\mathbf{Q}}}\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)v(\bm{\mathbf{Q}})d\mu_{n}(x)\leq+v({\mathbf{Q}})

which is (5.73).
∎

The general global solution for the stochastic initial Cauchy problem for the inhomogenous equation is as follows.

Theorem 5.18
.

Let
f\in C^{2}(\mathbf{Q}\times(0,\infty)),\phi\in C^{2}({\mathbf{Q}})
and consider the stochastic initial-value problem for the inhomogeneous equation

(5.85)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{Q}}\widehat{u(x,t)}=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\widehat{u(x,t)}-{\Delta}_{x}\widehat{u(x,t)}+f(x,t),~~(x\in\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n},t>0)

(5.86)

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi(x,0)}=\phi(x)+\mathscr{J}(x),~~(x\in\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n},t=0)

where
\mathscr{J}(x)
is the GRSF with the same properties as before. If
u(x,t)
solves the PDE
\square u(x,t)=(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}-{\Delta}_{x})u(x,t)=f(x,t)
,
then the solution to this problem is given by the stochastic convolution integral

\displaystyle\widehat{u(x,t)}=\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{3/2}}\int_{0}^{R}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\frac{e^{\frac{-\|x-y\|^{2}}{|t-s|^{2}}}}{|t-s|^{2}}f(y,s)d\mu_{n}(y)ds

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{4t}\right)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(x)+\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{4t}\right)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

\displaystyle\equiv\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}h(x-y,t-s)dsd\mu_{n}(y)+\int_{\mathrm{R}^{n}}\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)+\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

(5.87)

\displaystyle\equiv u(x,t)+\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

Proof.

(5.88)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\widehat{u(x,t)}=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u(x,t)+\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}h(x-y,t)\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

(5.89)

\displaystyle{\Delta}_{x}\widehat{u(x,t)}={\Delta}_{x}u(x,t)+\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}{\Delta}_{x}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

so that

\displaystyle{\square}\widehat{u(x,t)}=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-{\Delta}_{x}\right)\widehat{u(x,t)}=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u(x,t)-\Delta_{x}u(x,t)

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}h(x-y,t)-{\Delta}_{x}h(x-y,t)]\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}(x,t)

(5.90)

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}h(x-y,t)-\Delta_{x}h(x-y,t)]\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)=f(x,t)

since
{\square}h(x-y,t)=\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}h(x-y,t)-{\Delta}_{x}h(x-y,t)=0
for the heat kernel or fundamental solution.
∎

5.3.
Stochastically averaged mean value theorem in a heat ball

Proposition 5.19
.

For fixed
x\in\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n},t\in\bm{\mathrm{R}}
and
R>0
define a ’noisy’ or stochastic heat ball as the region

(5.91)

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathscr{B}}(x,t;R)}=\bigg\{(y,s)\in\mathbf{R}^{n}\times[0,\infty)|s\leq t,h(x-y,t-s)\geq\frac{1}{R^{n}},\mathscr{J}(x),\mathscr{J}(y)\bigg\}

where
\mathscr{J}(x)
is a time-independent GRSF existing at all points in the heat ball and all points in
\mathbf{R}^{n}
.

Proposition 5.20
.

Let
u(x,t)\in C^{2}(\mathbf{U}_{T})
solve the initial Cauchy value problem for the heat equation and let
\widehat{(x,t)}=u(x,t)+\mathscr{U}(x,t)
solve the Cauchy problem with random initial data and where
\mathscr{U}(x,t)
is a stochastic convolution. Then if
u(x,t)
satisfies the MVP, the stochastically averaged mean-value property is

(5.92)

\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket\widehat{u(x,t)}\big\rrbracket=\frac{1}{4R^{n}}\iint_{\boldsymbol{\mathscr{B}}(x,t;R)}\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket\widehat{u(y,s)}\big\rrbracket\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{|t-s|^{2}}d\mu_{n}(y)ds

Proof.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket\widehat{u(x,t)}\big\rrbracket

\displaystyle=u(x,t)+\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket\mathscr{U}(x,t)\big\rrbracket=u(x,t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4R^{n}}\iint_{\boldsymbol{\mathscr{B}}(x,t;R)}\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket\widehat{u(y,s)}\big\rrbracket\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{|t-s|^{2}}d^{3}yds

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4R^{n}}\iint_{\boldsymbol{\mathscr{B}}(x,t;R)}u(y,s)\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{|t-s|^{2}}d\mu_{n}(y)ds+\frac{1}{4R^{n}}\iint_{{\mathrm{I\!H}}(x,t;R)}\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket{\mathscr{U}(y,s)}\big\rrbracket\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{|t-s|^{2}}d^{3}yds

(5.93)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4R^{n}}\iint_{\boldsymbol{\mathscr{B}}(x,t;R)}u(y,s)\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{|t-s|^{2}}d\mu_{n}(y)ds

since
\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket{\mathscr{U}(y,s)}\big\rrbracket=0
.
∎

Theorem 5.21
.

Given the SCIVP on
{\mathbf{Q}}\subset{\mathbf{R}}^{n}
with random initial data
\widehat{u(x,0)}=u(x)+\mathscr{J}(x)
then the perturbed heat equation is

{\square}\widehat{u(x,t)}=0
. Then the stochastic version of (2.56) is

(5.94)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}\llbracket\widehat{\bm{\mathrm{I\!E}}}_{I}(t)\rrbracket=\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}d\mu_{n}(x)\right\rrbracket<0

and is also non decreasing.

Proof.

The randomly perturbed solution is the convolution integral

(5.95)

\displaystyle\widehat{u(x,t)}=u(x,t)+\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

Then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}\llbracket{\widehat{\bm{\mathrm{I\!E}}}_{I}(t)}\rrbracket=\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\int\left|u(x,t)+\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{2}d\mu_{n}(x)\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}\llbracket\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|u(x,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(x)\rrbracket+\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\int_{\mathbf{Q}}u(x,t)\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d^{n}y\right\rrbracket d\mu_{n}(x)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{2}\right\rrbracket d\mu_{n}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}{\bm{\mathrm{I\!E}}}_{I}(t)+\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{2}\right\rrbracket d\mu_{n}(x)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}{\bm{\mathrm{I\!E}}}_{I}(t)+\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\underbrace{\bigg(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\bigg)\bigg(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\mathscr{J}(y)|^{2}\rrbracket d\mu_{n}(y)\bigg)}_{via~CS~ineq.}d\mu_{n}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}{\bm{\mathrm{I\!E}}}_{I}(t)+\frac{1}{2}\zeta v({\mathbf{Q}})\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\frac{d}{dt}\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)d\mu_{n}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}{\bm{\mathrm{I\!E}}}_{I}(t)+\frac{1}{2}\zeta v(\bm{\mathbf{Q}})\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\frac{d}{dt}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d^{n}y\right)d\mu_{n}(x)

(5.96)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}{\bm{\mathrm{I\!E}}}_{I}(t)+\frac{1}{2}\zeta v(\mathbf{Q})\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\bigg(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\bigg(\frac{-t^{-(n+2)}(2nt-(y-x)^{2}))e^{-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{2t}}}{2(4\pi)^{n}}\bigg)d\mu_{n}(y)\bigg)d\mu_{n}(x)<0

∎

6.
Stochastic Li-Yau estimate and parabolic Harnack inequalities

The Li-Yau estimate and parabolic Harnack inequalities for solutions of the
heat equation were presented in Section 3. Here, stochastic versions are presented for
a randomly perturbed heat equation where the initial data is a Gaussian random field.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
u=u(x,t)
be a solution of
{\square}u\equiv(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}-\Delta)u=0
for all
x\in{\mathbf{Q}}\subset{\mathbf{R}}^{n}
.
with initial data
u(x,0)=\phi(x)
. From Lemma (3.1), the following equalities hold such that
{\square}(\log u)=\tfrac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|u|^{2}}
and

{\square}(u\log u)=-\tfrac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|u|}
. Suppose now that
the initial data is randomised as
\widehat{u(x,0)}=\widehat{\phi(x)}=\phi(x)+\mathscr{J}(x)
. Then the randomly perturbed solution is
\widehat{u(x,t)}=u(x,t)+\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d^{n}y\equiv u(x,t)+\mathscr{U}(x,t)
. Then
\widehat{u}=u+\mathscr{U}=u(x,t)+\mathscr{U}(x,t)

satisfies the inequalities:

(6.1)

\displaystyle{\square}(\log\widehat{u})=(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\Delta)(\log\widehat{u})=\frac{|\nabla\widehat{u}|^{2}}{|\widehat{u}|^{2}}

(6.2)

\displaystyle{\square}(\widehat{u}\log\widehat{u})=(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\Delta)(\widehat{u}\log\widehat{u})=-\frac{|\nabla\widehat{u}|^{2}}{|\widehat{u}|}

or equivalently

(6.3)

\displaystyle{|\widehat{u}|^{2}}{\square}(\log\widehat{u})={|\widehat{u}|^{2}}(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\Delta)(\log\widehat{u})={|\nabla\widehat{u}|^{2}}

(6.4)

\displaystyle-|\widehat{u}|{\square}(\widehat{u}\log\widehat{u})=-|\widehat{u}|(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\Delta)(\widehat{u}\log\widehat{u})={|\nabla\widehat{u}|^{2}}

which is

(6.5)

\displaystyle|{u+\mathscr{U}|^{2}}{\square}(\log(u+\mathscr{U})={|{u}+\mathscr{U}|^{2}}(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\Delta)(\log(u+\mathscr{U})={|\nabla u+\nabla\mathscr{U}|^{2}}

(6.6)

\displaystyle-{|{u+\mathscr{U}}|}{\square}(\log(u+\mathscr{U})=-{|{u}+\mathscr{U}}|(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\Delta)(\log(u+\mathscr{U})={|\nabla u+\nabla\mathscr{U}|^{2}}

If
\phi(x)=0
then
\widehat{u}=\mathscr{U}
and so

(6.7)

\displaystyle{|{\mathscr{U}}|^{2}}{\square}(\log(\mathscr{U})={|\mathscr{U}|^{2}}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\Delta\right)(\log(\mathscr{U})={|\nabla\mathscr{U}|^{2}}

(6.8)

\displaystyle-{|{\mathscr{U}}|}{\square}(\log(\mathscr{U})=-{|\mathscr{U}}|\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\Delta\right)(\log(\mathscr{U})={|\nabla\mathscr{U}|^{2}}

Proof.

As in Lemma (3.1), the proof follows easily by direct calculation. The derivates

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mathscr{U}
and
\nabla\mathscr{U}
exist so that

\displaystyle{\square}(\log\widehat{u})=(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\widehat{u}-\Delta\widehat{u})(\log u)\equiv\frac{\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\widehat{u}}{\widehat{u}}-\nabla(\nabla\log\widehat{u})

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta\widehat{u}}{\widehat{u}}-\nabla\left(\frac{\nabla\widehat{u}}{\widehat{u}}\right)=\frac{\Delta\widehat{u}}{\widehat{u}}-\frac{(\nabla\nabla u)u-\nabla\widehat{u}\nabla\widehat{u}}{|\widehat{u}|^{2}}

(6.9)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta\widehat{u}}{\widehat{u}}-\frac{\Delta\widehat{u}}{\widehat{u}}+\frac{|\nabla\widehat{u}|^{2}}{|\widehat{u}|^{2}}=\frac{|\nabla\widehat{u}|^{2}}{|\widehat{u}|^{2}}

and similarly for (6.2).
∎

Corollary 6.2
.

(6.10)

\displaystyle|\widehat{u}|^{2}{\square}(\log\widehat{u})=-|\widehat{u}|{\square}(\widehat{u}\log\widehat{u})

Corollary 6.3
.

The expectations of (6.1) and (6.2) are

(6.11)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket{|\widehat{u}|^{2}}{\square}(\log\widehat{u})\rrbracket=\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket{|\widehat{u}|^{2}}(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\Delta)(\log\widehat{u})\right\rrbracket=\mathbb{E}\llbracket{|\nabla\widehat{u}|^{2}}\rrbracket

(6.12)

\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u}|{\square}(\widehat{u}\log\widehat{u})=-\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket|\widehat{u}|(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\Delta)(\widehat{u}\log\widehat{u})\right\rrbracket=\mathbb{E}\llbracket{|\nabla\widehat{u}|^{2}}\rrbracket

and for
\phi(x)=0

(6.13)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket{|{\mathscr{U}}|^{2}}{\square}(\log(\mathscr{U})\rrbracket=\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket{|\mathscr{U}|^{2}}(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\Delta)(\log(\mathscr{U})\right\rrbracket=\mathbb{E}\llbracket{|\nabla\mathscr{U}|^{2}}

(6.14)

\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}\llbracket{|{\mathscr{U}}|}{\square}(\mathscr{U}\log(\mathscr{U})\rrbracket=-\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket{|\mathscr{U}}|(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\Delta)(\mathscr{U}\log(\mathscr{U})\right\rrbracket=\mathbb{E}\llbracket{|\nabla\mathscr{U}|^{2}}\rrbracket

Corollary 6.4
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket{|{\mathscr{U}}|^{2}}{\square}(\log(\mathscr{U})\rrbracket=-\mathbb{E}\llbracket{|{\mathscr{U}}|}{\square}(\mathscr{U}\log(\mathscr{U})\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{2}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\mathscr{J}(y)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)

\displaystyle=\zeta v(\mathbf{Q})\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)

(6.15)

\displaystyle=\zeta v(\mathbf{Q})\Lambda_{2}^{2}t^{-n}\int_{\mathbf{Q}}e^{-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{\Lambda_{2}t}}d\mu_{n}(y)

where the Cauchy Schwarz inequality and the bound (2.18) have been used.

The main theorem for the stochastic extension of the Li-Yau inequality is as follows.

Theorem 6.5
.

Let
{\mathbf{Q}}\subset{\mathbf{R}}^{n}
be a compact set or
domain with boundary
\partial{\mathbf{Q}}
. Let
u(x,t)
be a solution
of the deterministic heat equation
{\square}u(x,t)=0
for

x\in{\mathbf{Q}}
and
t>0
, with initial Cauchy data

u(x,0)=\phi(x)
, for some function
\phi(x)
. The solution for t¿0 is the
convolution integral
u(x,t)=\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}h(x-y,t)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)
. The Li-Yau estimate is then

(6.16)

\displaystyle\frac{|\nabla_{x}u(x,t)|^{2}}{|u(x,t)|^{2}}-\frac{|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u(x,t)|}{|u(x,t)|}\leq\frac{1}{2}nt^{-1}

or

(6.17)

\displaystyle|\nabla_{x}u(x,t)|^{2}-|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u(x,t)u(x,t)|\leq\frac{1}{2}nt^{-1}|u(x,t)|^{2}

which from Thm 3.4 this is equivalent to

\displaystyle\left|\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\nabla_{x}h(x-y,t)|^{2}d^{n}y\right|\leq\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{\bm{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d^{n}y\right)^{1/2}

(6.18)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}nt^{-1}\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)

and also

\displaystyle\Lambda_{1}^{2}t^{-n}\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\left(-\frac{2|x-y|}{\Lambda_{1}t}\right)^{2}e^{-\frac{2|x-y|^{2}}{\Lambda_{1}t}}d^{n}y\leq\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}h(x-y,t)|^{2}d^{n}y\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/2}

(6.19)

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda_{2}^{2}t^{-n}\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\exp\left(-\frac{2|x-y|^{2}}{\Lambda_{2}t}\right)d\mu_{n}(y)

If the initial data is now a GRF such that
\widehat{u(x,0)}=\phi(x)+\mathscr{J}(x)
with
\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x)\otimes\mathscr{J}(x)\rrbracket=\zeta
then
the randomly perturbed solution
\widehat{u(x,t)}
satisfies

{\square}\widehat{u(x,t)}=0
, for
x\in{\mathbf{Q}}
and
t>0
. Then

\widehat{u(x,t)}
satisfies a stochastic Li-Yau inequality of the form

(6.20)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\frac{|\nabla_{x}\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}}{|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}}\right\rrbracket-\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\frac{|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\widehat{u(x,t)}|}{|\widehat{u(x,t)}|}\right\rrbracket\leq\frac{1}{2}nt^{-1}

or the equivalent forms

(6.21)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\nabla_{x}\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}\rrbracket-\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\widehat{u(x,t)}\widehat{u(x,t)}|\rrbracket\leq\frac{1}{2}nt^{-1}|\mathbb{E}\llbracket\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}\rrbracket

(6.22)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket\nabla\log(\widehat{u(x,t)})|\rrbracket-\mathbb{E}\llbracket\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\log(\widehat{u(x,t)})|\rrbracket

only if (6.18) or (6.19) are also satisfied.

Proof.

The simplest proof utilises the stochastic Jensen inequality for concave functions. If

\Psi(x)
is a concave function and
\hat{X}
a random variable with expectation
\mathbb{E}\llbracket X\rrbracket
then
\mathbb{E}\llbracket[\Psi(X)]\leq\Psi(\mathbb{E}\llbracket X\rrbracket)
. Hence

(6.23)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket{\nabla}_{i}\log\widehat{u(x,t)}\rrbracket-\mathbb{E}\llbracket\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\log\widehat{u(x,t)}\rrbracket\leq|{\nabla}\log(\mathbb{E}\llbracket\widehat{u(x,t)}\rrbracket|^{2}-\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\log\mathbb{E}\llbracket\widehat{u(x,t)}\rrbracket|

But
\mathbb{E}\llbracket\widehat{u(x,t)}\rrbracket=u(x,t)
since
\mathbb{E}\llbracket\widehat{u(x,t)}\rrbracket=u(x,t)+\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(y)\rrbracket d\mu_{n}(y)=u(x,t)
.
Hence

(6.24)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket{\nabla}_{i}\log\widehat{u(x,t)}\rrbracket-\mathbb{E}\llbracket\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\log\widehat{u(x,t)}\rrbracket\leq|{\nabla}\log u(x,t)|^{2}-\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\log u(x,t)\leq\frac{1}{2}nt^{-1}

A second proof follows from a direct ’brute force’ computation of the derivatives. First

(6.25)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket|{\nabla}_{x}\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}\rrbracket=|{\nabla}{x}u(x,t)|^{2}+\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}{\nabla}_{x}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{2}\right\rrbracket

(6.26)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}\rrbracket=|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u(x,t)|^{2}+\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{2}\right\rrbracket

Using Cauchy Schwartz

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket|{\nabla}_{x}\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}\rrbracket=|u(x,t)|^{2}+\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{2}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle\leq|u(x,t)|^{2}+\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(y)|^{2}\rrbracket d^{n}y\right)

(6.27)

\displaystyle=|u(x,t)|^{2}+\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)\zeta v({\mathbf{Q}})

Similarly

(6.28)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket|{\nabla}_{x}\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}\rrbracket\leq{\nabla}u(x,t)|^{2}+\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|{\nabla}_{x}h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)\mu v({\mathbf{Q}}

(6.29)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}\right\rrbracket\leq|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u(x,t)|^{2}+\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)\mu v({\mathbf{Q}})

The estimate (6.21) can be expressed as

(6.30)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket{|{\nabla}\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}}{}\right\rrbracket-\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket{\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\widehat{u(x,t)}}\otimes{\widehat{u(x,t)}}\right\rrbracket\leq\frac{1}{2}nt^{-1}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}\rrbracket

The term
\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}\widehat{u(x,t)}\otimes{\widehat{u(x,t)}}\right\rrbracket

can be decomposed as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\widehat{u(x,t)}\otimes{\widehat{u(x,t)}}\right\rrbracket=\left((\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u(x,t)\right)u(x,t)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\underbrace{\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right)}_{Apply~Cauchy~Schwartz}\underbrace{\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right)}_{Apply~Cauchy~Schwartz}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle\leq(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u(x,t))u(x,t)+\mathbb{E}\bigg\llbracket\left[\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\mathscr{J}(y)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1.2}\right]

\displaystyle\times\left[\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\mathscr{J}(y)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/2}\right]\bigg\rrbracket

\displaystyle\leq(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u(x,t))u(x,t)+\left[\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/2}\right]

\displaystyle\left[\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d^{n}y\right)^{1/2}\mathbb{E}\bigg\llbracket\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\mathscr{J}(y)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)\right]\bigg\rrbracket

\displaystyle=(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u(x,t))u(x,t)+\left[\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/2}\right]

\displaystyle\left[\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket|\mathscr{J}(y)|^{2}\big\rrbracket d\mu_{n}(y)\right)\right]\bigg\rrbracket

\displaystyle=(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u(x,t))u(x,t)+\left[\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/2}\right]

\displaystyle\left[\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\zeta d\mu_{n}(y)\right)\right]\bigg\rrbracket

\displaystyle=(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u(x,t))u(x,t)

(6.31)

\displaystyle+\left[\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/2}\right]\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/2}\zeta v(\mathbf{Q})

Hence, the estimate becomes

\displaystyle|\nabla u(x,t)|^{2}+\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|{\nabla}_{x}h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)\zeta v({\mathbf{Q}})-(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u(x,t))u(x,t)

\displaystyle-\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/2}\zeta v({\mathbf{Q}})

(6.32)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}nt^{-1}+\frac{1}{2}nt^{-1}\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\zeta v({\mathbf{Q}})

Since
u(x,t)
satisfies the estimate (6.18), it is required that

\displaystyle\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|{\nabla}_{x}h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)\zeta v({\mathbf{Q}}

\displaystyle-\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/2}\zeta v({\mathbf{Q}})

(6.33)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}nt^{-1}\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\zeta v({\mathbf{Q}})

which is (cancelling the
v(\mathbf{Q})
terms)

\displaystyle\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|{\nabla}_{x}h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)

\displaystyle-\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{1/2}

(6.34)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}nt^{-1}\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x,y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)

which also agrees exactly with (6.18).
∎

Theorem 6.6
.

The randomly perturbed solution
\widehat{u(x,t)}
also satisfies the following Li-Yau inequality

(6.35)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\nabla\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}\rrbracket}{\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}\rrbracket}-\frac{\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\widehat{u(x,t)}|\rrbracket}{\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|\rrbracket}\leq\frac{1}{2}nt^{-1}

provided that

(6.36)

\displaystyle\frac{|\nabla{u(x,t)}|^{2}}{|{u(x,t)}|^{2}}-\frac{|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}{u(x,t)}|}{|{u(x,t)}|}\leq\frac{1}{2}nt^{-1}

Proof.

The perturbed solution
\widehat{u(x,t)}
has the derivatives

(6.37)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\widehat{u(x,t)}=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u(x,t)+\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

(6.38)

\displaystyle\nabla\widehat{u(x,t)}=\nabla u(x,t)+\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\nabla h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

Clearly,
\mathbb{E}\llbracket\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\widehat{u(x,t)}\rrbracket=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u(x,t)
and
\mathbb{E}\llbracket\nabla\widehat{u(x,t)}\rrbracket=\nabla u(x,t)
. Using the Cauchy Schwarz inequality (or the Holder inequality with
p=q=2
)
gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}\rrbracket=|\nabla u(x,t)|^{2}+\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\nabla h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{2}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle\leq|\nabla u(x,t)|^{2}+\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\nabla h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\mathscr{J}(y)|^{2}\rrbracket d\mu_{n}(y)\right)

(6.39)

\displaystyle=|\nabla u(x,t)|^{2}+\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\nabla h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)\zeta v({\mathbf{Q}})

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}\rrbracket=|\nabla u(x,t)|^{2}+\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{2}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle\leq|u(x,t)|^{2}+\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\mathscr{J}(y)|^{2}\rrbracket d\mu_{n}(y)\right)

(6.40)

\displaystyle=|u(x,t)|^{2}+\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)\zeta v(\mathbf{Q})

If the Li-Yau-Harnack (6.36) inequality holds then

(6.41)

\displaystyle\frac{|\nabla{u(x,t)}|^{2}+\zeta v(\mathbf{Q})\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\nabla h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)}{|{u(x,t)}|^{2}+\zeta v(\mathbf{Q})\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)}-\frac{\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u(x,t)}{u(x,t)}\leq\frac{1}{2}nt^{-1}

Since
\zeta v({\mathbf{Q}})\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x,y-t)d^{n}y\geq 0
for all

(x,y)\in{\mathbf{Q}}
and
t>0

\displaystyle\frac{|\nabla{u(x,t)}|^{2}+\zeta V(\mathbf{Q})\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\nabla h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)}{|{u(x,t)}|^{2}+\zeta v(\mathbf{Q})\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)}-\frac{\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u(x,t)}{u(x,t)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|\nabla{u(x,t)}|^{2}+\zeta v(\mathbf{Q})\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\nabla h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)}{|{u(x,t)}|^{2}}-\frac{\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u(x,t)}{u(x,t)}

(6.42)

\displaystyle=\frac{|\nabla{u(x,t)}|^{2}}{|{u(x,t)}|^{2}}+\underbrace{\frac{\zeta v(\mathbf{Q})\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\nabla h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)}{|u(x,t)|^{2}}}_{positive~term}-\frac{\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u(x,t)}{u(x,t)}\leq\frac{1}{2}nt^{-1}

Hence

(6.43)

\displaystyle\frac{|\nabla{u(x,t)}|^{2}}{|{u(x,t)}|^{2}}-\frac{\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u(x,t)}{u(x,t)}\leq\frac{1}{2}nt^{-1}-\underbrace{\frac{\zeta v(\mathbf{Q})\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\nabla h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)}{|u(x,t)|^{2}}}\leq\frac{1}{2}nt^{-1}

where we have used the simple inequality
\frac{A+B}{C+D}\leq\frac{A+B}{C}=\frac{A}{C}+\frac{B}{C}
if
A,B,C,D\geq 0
. Note also that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\nabla|h|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)=\frac{1}{4\pi t}^{n/2}\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\left|\nabla\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{4t}\right)\right|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)

(6.44)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi t}^{n/2}\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\left|\frac{-\|x-y\|}{2t}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{4t}\right)\right|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\geq 0

∎

Theorem 6.7
.

(
\underline{Stochastic~parabolic~Harnackinequality}
)
Let
u(x,t)
and
u(y,t)
be solutions of the heat equations
{\square}u(x,t)=0=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-{\Delta}_{x}u(x,t)=0
and
{\square}u(y,t)=0=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-{\Delta}_{y}u(y,t)=0
with initial data
u(x,0)=u(y,0)=\phi(x)
. If
0<t_{1}<t_{2}
then the parabolic Harnack estimate is given by Thm 3.5 so that
|u(y,t_{2})|^{2}\geq|u(x,t_{1})|^{2}\left|\tfrac{t_{1}}{t_{2}}\right|^{n}\exp(-\tfrac{|x-y|^{2}}{2t})
. If the initial Cauchy data is randomly perturbed
as
\widehat{\phi(x)}=\phi(x)+\mathscr{J}(x)
then the perturbed solution

\widehat{u(x,t)}
solves
\left(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}-{\Delta}_{x}\right)\widehat{u(x,t)}=0
and similarly
\widehat{u(y,t)}
solves
\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\Delta_{y}\right)\widehat{u(y,t)}=0
. Then the stochastic parabolic Harnack inequality is

(6.45)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(y,t_{2})}|^{2}\rrbracket\geq\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t_{1})}|^{2}\rrbracket\left|\frac{t_{1}}{t_{2}}\right|^{n}\exp\left(-\tfrac{|x-y|^{2}}{2t}\right)

provided that
\exp\left(-\tfrac{-|x-y|^{2}}{2t}\right)\leq 1
which is always true since

\exp(-X)\in[0,1]
for
X\in[0,\infty]
.

Proof.

The expectations of the squares (volatility) of the stochastic convolution integrals are

(6.46)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}\rrbracket=|u(x,t)|^{2}+\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}h(x,z,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(z)d^{n}z\right|^{2}\right\rrbracket

(6.47)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(y,t)}|^{2}\rrbracket=|u(y,t)|^{2}+\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}h(y,z,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(z)d^{n}z\right|^{2}\right\rrbracket

Using Cauchy Schwartz

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}\rrbracket=|u(x,t)|^{2}+\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}h(x,z,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(z)d^{n}z\right|^{2}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle\leq|u(x,t)|^{2}+\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x,z,t)|^{2}d^{n}z\right)\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\mathscr{J}(z)|^{2}d^{n}z\right)\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle\equiv|u(x,t)|^{2}+\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x,z,t)|^{2}d^{n}z\right)\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\mathscr{J}(z)|^{2}\rrbracket d^{n}z\right)

\displaystyle\equiv|u(x,t)|^{2}+\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x,z,t)|^{2}d^{n}z\right)\zeta\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}d^{n}z\right)

\displaystyle\equiv|u(x,t)|^{2}+\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x,z,t)|^{2}d^{n}z\right)\zeta v({\mathbf{Q}})

(6.48)

\displaystyle\leq|u(x,t)|^{2}+\zeta h(x,x,2t)v({\mathbf{Q}})=|u(x,t)|^{2}+\frac{\zeta v({\mathbf{Q}})}{|8\pi t|^{n/2}}

using the semi-group property from Lemma 2.21. Similarly,

(6.49)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(y,t)}|^{2}\rrbracket\leq|u(y,t)|^{2}+\frac{\zeta v({\mathbf{Q}})}{|8\pi t|^{n/2}}

The estimate (1.37) is then

(6.50)

\displaystyle\left(|u(y_{2},t)|^{2}+\frac{\zeta v({\mathbf{Q}})}{|8\pi t|^{n/2}}\right)\geq\left|\frac{t_{1}}{t_{2}}\right|^{n}\left(|u(x_{2},t)|^{2}+\frac{\zeta v(\bm{\mathbf{Q}})}{|8\pi t|^{n/2}}\right)\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|}{2|t_{2}-t_{1}|}\right)

Since
|u(y,t_{2})|^{2}\geq|u(x,t_{1})|^{2}\left|\tfrac{t_{1}}{t_{2}}\right|^{n}\exp(-\tfrac{|x-y|^{2}}{2t})
, this is equivalent to

(6.51)

\displaystyle\frac{|8\pi t_{1}|^{n/2}}{|8\pi t_{2}|^{n/2}}\geq\left|\frac{t_{1}}{t_{2}}\right|^{n}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|}{2|t_{2}-t_{1}|}\right)

or

(6.52)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{t_{1}}{t_{2}}\right|^{n/2}\geq\left|\frac{t_{1}}{t_{2}}\right|^{n}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|}{2|t_{2}-t_{1}|}\right)>\left|\frac{t_{1}}{t_{2}}\right|^{n/2}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|}{2|t_{2}-t_{1}|}\right)

which is simply
\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|}{2|t_{2}-t_{1}|}\right)\leq 1
which is always true for all
|x-y|\geq 0
and
|t_{2}-t_{1}|>0
. Hence, the estimate (6.45) is valid.
∎

7.
Estimates and decay properties for p-moments and volatility on
\mathbf{Q}
,
\mathrm{R}^{+}
,
\mathbf{B}_{R}(0)

In this section, the p-moments
\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}\rrbracket
and volatility
\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}\rrbracket
are estimated from the general solution
\widehat{u(x,t)}
to the stochastic Cauchy initial-value problem on a finite domain
\mathbf{Q}\subset\mathbf{R}^{n}
. The estimates for a general domain
\mathbf{Q}
is first derived, then the specific cases are calculated explicitly for a segment on the real line
\mathbf{Q}=\mathbf{L}\subset{\mathbf{R}}^{+}
with
\mathbf{L}=[0,L]
, a ball
\mathbf{B}_{R}(0)\subset{\mathbf{R}}^{n}
of radius R. The Cauchy evolution or development from the random initial data, is then stable or unstable and will either decay or grow without bound, or blow up with time.

Proposition 7.1
.

(Reprising Proposition 1.1). Given the SCIVP on
\mathbf{Q}\subset\bm{\mathbf{R}}^{n}
with Gaussian random initial data
\widehat{u(x,0)}=\widehat{\phi(x)}=\phi(x)+\mathscr{J}(x)
and solution
\widehat{u(x,t)}
then:

(1)

The solution is stable and is smoothed out and decays if

(7.1)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}=0,~~\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}=0

or

(7.2)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}\leq K,~~\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}\leq K

for some
K>0

(2)

The solution is unstable and grows without bound if

(7.3)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}=\infty,~~\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}=\infty

(3)

The solution blows up if for some finite
t_{B}\in[0,\infty)

(7.4)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\uparrow t_{B}}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}=\infty,~~\lim_{t\uparrow t_{B}}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}=\infty

One expects scenario (1) as the heat equation should smooth out the initial randomness so that the moments and volatility decay to zero.

Theorem 7.2
.

Let
\widehat{u(x,t)}
satisfy the stochastic Cauchy initial-value problem on a finite domain
\mathbf{Q}\subset\mathbf{R}^{n}
with random initial Cauchy data, for the heat equation

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\widehat{u(x,t)}={\Delta}\widehat{u(x,t)},~~(x\in\mathbf{Q},t\in[0,\infty)

(7.5)

\displaystyle\widehat{u(x,0)}=\phi(x)+\mathscr{J}(x,~~(x\in\mathbf{Q},t=0)

with Gaussian random scalar field
\mathscr{J}(x)
and
\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\mathscr{J}(x)|^{p}\rrbracket=\frac{1}{2}[\zeta^{\frac{p}{2}}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{\frac{p}{2}}]
. Then the moments are finite and bounded with the estimate

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}\big\rrbracket\leq 2^{p-1}\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\phi(y)|^{p}d^{n}p\right)+\frac{1}{2}[\zeta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{p/2}]v(\mathbf{Q}\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}{(4\pi t))^{-n/2}}e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{p}

(7.6)

\displaystyle\equiv 2^{p-1}\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\phi(y)|^{p}d^{n}p\right)+\frac{1}{2}[\zeta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{p/2}]v(\mathbf{Q}\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{p}

If
\phi(x)=C
then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}\big\rrbracket\leq 2^{p-1}|C|^{p}v(\mathbf{Q})+\frac{1}{2}[\zeta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{p/2}]v(\mathbf{Q}\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\frac{1}{(4\pi t))^{n/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right)d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{p}

(7.7)

\displaystyle\equiv 2^{p-1}|C|^{p}v(\mathbf{Q})+\frac{1}{2}[\zeta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{p/2}]\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{p}

Proof.

Using the basic estimate
|a+b|^{p}\leq 2^{p-1}|a|^{p}+2^{p-1}|b|^{p}
and the results of Lemma (5.5)

\displaystyle\bm{\mathcal{M}}_{p}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}\rrbracket=\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)+\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(x)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\frac{1}{2^{p}}\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p}+\frac{1}{2^{p}}\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(x)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2^{p}}\underbrace{\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p}}_{apply~Holder~inequality}+\frac{1}{2^{p}}\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(x)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p}\right\rrbracket}_{apply~stoc.~Holder~inequality}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2^{p}}\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|h(x-y,t)|^{q}\right)^{p/q}\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\phi(y)|^{p}d\mu_{n}(y)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2^{p}}\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|h(x-y,t)|^{q}\right)^{p/q}\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\mathscr{J}(y)|^{p}\rrbracket d\mu_{n}(y)

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{2^{p}}\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|h(x-y,t)|^{q}\right)^{p/q}\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\phi(y)|^{p}d\mu_{n}(y)+\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\mathscr{J}(y)|^{p}\rrbracket d\mu_{n}(y)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{p}}\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|h(x-y,t)|^{q}\right)^{p/q}\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\phi(y)|^{p}d\mu_{n}(y)+\frac{1}{2}[\zeta^{p/2}+f\zeta^{p/2}]\int_{\mathbf{Q}}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{p}}\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|h(x-y,t)|^{q}\right)^{p/q}\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\phi(y)|^{p}d\mu_{n}(y)+\frac{1}{2}[\zeta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{p/2}]v({\mathbf{Q}})\right)

(7.8)

\displaystyle\equiv\left(\|h(x-\bullet,t)\|_{L_{q}(\mathbf{Q}}\right)^{p}\left(\|\phi(\bullet)\|_{L_{p}(\mathbf{Q})}+\frac{1}{2}[\zeta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{p/2}]v({\mathbf{Q}})\right)

where
\tfrac{1}{p}+\tfrac{1}{q}=1
. Since
\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\|(x-\bullet,t)\|_{L_{q}(\mathbf{Q}}=0
it follows that the p-moments are smoothed and decay as
t\rightarrow\infty
so that
\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u}(x,t)|^{p}\rrbracket=0
.
∎

Corollary 7.3
.

If
\phi(x)=C
then

(7.9)

\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}\big\rrbracket\leq 2^{p-1}|C|^{p}V(\bm{\mathbf{Q}})+\frac{1}{2}[\zeta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{p/2}]v(\mathbf{Q})\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}{(4\pi t)^{-n/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right)d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{p}

A second estimate can be derived via the binomial theorem.

Theorem 7.4
.

Let
\widehat{u(x,t)}
satisfy the stochastic Cauchy initial-value problem on a finite domain
\mathbf{Q}\subset\mathbf{R}^{n}
with random initial Cauchy data, for the heat equation

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\widehat{u(x,t)}={\Delta}\widehat{u(x,t)},~~(x\in\mathbf{Q},t\in[0,\infty)

(7.10)

\displaystyle\widehat{u(x,0)}=\phi(x)+\mathscr{J}(x)=C+\mathscr{J}(x),~~(x\in\mathbf{Q},t=0)

with Gaussian random scalar field
\mathscr{J}(x)
and
\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\mathscr{J}(x)|^{p}\rrbracket=\frac{1}{2}[\zeta^{\frac{p}{2}}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{\frac{p}{2}}]
. Then the moments are finite and bounded with the estimate

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\beta=0}^{p}\binom{p}{\beta}|C|^{p-\beta}(\zeta^{\frac{p}{2}}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{\frac{p}{2}})|v(\bm{\mathbf{Q}})|^{p}\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}{(4\pi t)^{-n/2}}e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}}d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{2p-\beta}

(7.11)

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\beta=0}^{p}\binom{p}{\beta}|C|^{p-\beta}(\zeta^{\frac{p}{2}}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{\frac{p}{2}})|{v}({\mathbf{Q}})|^{p}\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)|d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{2p-\beta}

and it is known that
\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)|d^{n}y<\infty
for any finite domain
{\mathbf{Q}}
.

Proof.

Using the binomial theorem

\displaystyle(X+Y)^{p}=\sum_{\beta=1}^{p}\binom{p}{\beta}X^{p-\beta}Y^{p}

with
\tfrac{1}{p}+\tfrac{1}{q}=1
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}\rrbracket=\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}{C}h(x-y,t)d\mu_{n}(y)+\int_{\mathbf{Q}}{C}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\sum_{\beta=1}^{p}\binom{p}{\beta}\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}{C}h(x-y,t)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p-\beta}\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle\equiv\sum_{\char 16\relax=1}^{p}\binom{p}{\beta}\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}{C}h(x-y,t)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p-\xi}\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle\equiv\sum_{\char 16\relax=1}^{p}\binom{p}{\beta}C^{p-\beta}\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p-\beta}\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta=1}^{p}\binom{p}{\beta}C^{p-\beta}\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t))d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p-\beta}

\displaystyle\times\bigg(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\mathscr{J}(y)|^{p}\rrbracket d\mu_{n}(y)\bigg)\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\bigg(\bigg|h(x-y,t)d\mu_{n}(y)\bigg|^{q}\bigg)^{p/q}

\displaystyle\equiv\sum_{\char 16\relax=1}^{p}\binom{p}{\beta}C^{p-\beta}\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p-\beta}

\displaystyle\times\bigg(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\mathscr{J}(y)|^{p}\rrbracket d\mu_{n}(y)\bigg)\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\bigg(\bigg|h(x-y,t)|^{\frac{p}{p-1}}d\mu_{n}(y)\bigg)^{p-1}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\beta=1}^{p}\binom{p}{\beta}C^{p-\beta}\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p-\beta}

\displaystyle\times[\zeta^{\frac{p}{2}}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{\frac{p}{2}}]\bigg(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}d\mu_{n}(y)\bigg)^{p}\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\bigg(\bigg|h(x-y,t)|^{\frac{p}{p-1}}d\mu_{n}(y)\bigg)^{p-1}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\beta=1}^{p}\binom{p}{\beta}C^{p-\beta}\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p-\beta}

\displaystyle\times[\beta^{\frac{p}{2}}+(-1)^{p}\beta^{\frac{p}{2}}]|v(\mathbf{Q})|^{p}\bigg(\bigg|h(x-y,t)|^{p/p-1}d\mu_{n}(y)\bigg)^{p-1}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\beta=1}^{p}\binom{p}{\beta}C^{p-\beta}\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)d^{n}y\right|^{p-\beta}

\displaystyle\times[\zeta^{\frac{p}{2}}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{\frac{p}{2}}]|v(\mathbf{Q})|^{p}\bigg(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)d\mu_{n}(y)\bigg)^{p}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\beta=1}^{p}\binom{p}{\beta}C^{p-\beta}[\zeta^{\frac{p}{2}}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{\frac{p}{2}}]|v(\mathbf{Q})|^{p}\bigg(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)d\mu_{n}(y)\bigg)^{2p-\beta}

(7.12)

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\beta=1}^{p}\binom{p}{\beta}C^{p-\beta}[\zeta^{\frac{p}{2}}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{\frac{p}{2}}]|v(\mathbf{Q})|^{p}\bigg(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)d\mu_{n}(y)\bigg)^{2p-\beta}

∎

Corollary 7.5
.

The volatility follows for
p=2
so that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\beta=0}^{2}\binom{2}{\beta}|C|^{2-\beta}(\zeta^{\frac{2}{2}}+(-1)^{2}\zeta^{\frac{2}{2}})|v(\mathbf{Q})|^{2}\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{4-\beta}
