\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\beta=0}^{2}\binom{2}{\beta}|C|^{2-\beta}(\zeta^{\frac{2}{2}}+(-1)^{2}\zeta^{\frac{2}{2}})|v(\mathbf{Q})|^{2}\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)|d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{4-\beta}

(7.13)

\displaystyle\equiv\sum_{\beta=0}^{2}\binom{2}{\beta}|C|^{2-\beta}\zeta|v(\mathbf{Q})|^{2}\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)|d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{4-\beta}

Corollary 7.6
.

Since

(7.14)

\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right)d^{n}y=0

it follows that the moments decay to zero as
t\rightarrow\infty
so the convolution integral solution is stable

\displaystyle\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\beta=0}^{p}\binom{p}{\beta}|C|^{p-\beta}(\zeta^{\frac{p}{2}}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{\frac{p}{2}})|v(\mathbf{Q})|^{p}\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{2p-\beta}

(7.15)

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\beta=0}^{p}\binom{p}{\beta}|C|^{p-\beta}(\zeta^{\frac{p}{2}}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{\frac{p}{2}})|v(\mathbf{Q})|^{p}\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)|d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{2p-\beta}=0

Similarly, for the volatility (p=2)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\beta=0}^{2}\binom{2}{\beta}|C|^{2-\beta}(\zeta^{\frac{2}{2}}+(-1)^{2}\zeta^{\frac{2}{2}})|v(\mathbf{Q})|^{2}\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{4-\beta}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\beta=0}^{2}\binom{2}{\beta}|C|^{2-\beta}(\zeta^{\frac{2}{2}}+(-1)^{2}\zeta^{\frac{2}{2}})|v(\mathbf{Q})|^{2}\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)|d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{4-\beta}

(7.16)

\displaystyle\equiv\sum_{\beta=0}^{2}\binom{2}{\beta}|C|^{2-\beta}\zeta|v(\mathbf{Q})|^{2}\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}h(x-y,t)|d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{4-\beta}=0

The moments and volatility can be computed on an interval of the real line since the integral is simply Gaussian.

Corollary 7.7
.

If
{\mathbf{Q}}={\mathbf{L}}=[0,L]

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\beta=0}^{p}\binom{p}{\beta}|C|^{p-\beta}(\zeta^{\frac{p}{2}}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{\frac{p}{2}})|L|^{p}\left|\int_{0}^{L}{(4\pi t)^{-1/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right)dy\right|^{2p-\beta}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\beta=0}^{p}\binom{p}{\beta}|C|^{p-\beta}(\zeta^{\frac{p}{2}}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{\frac{p}{2}})|L|^{p}\left|\int_{0}^{L}h(x-y,t)|dy\right|^{2p-\beta}

(7.17)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\beta=0}^{p}\binom{p}{\beta}|C|^{p-\beta}(\zeta^{\frac{p}{2}}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{\frac{p}{2}})|L|^{p}\left(\frac{erf(\frac{x}{2\sqrt{t}})-erf(\frac{x-L}{2\sqrt{t}})}{4^{1/2}\pi t}\right)

where
erf(x)
is the error function with
erf(0)=0
and
erf(\infty)=1
. And so

(7.18)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}\rrbracket=\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\beta=0}^{p}\binom{p}{\beta}|C|^{p-\beta}(\zeta^{\frac{p}{2}}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{\frac{p}{2}})|L|^{p}\left(\frac{erf(\frac{x}{2\sqrt{t}})-erf(\frac{x-L}{2\sqrt{t}})}{4^{1/2}\pi t}\right)=0

(7.19)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}\rrbracket=\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\sum_{\beta=0}^{2}\binom{2}{\beta}|C|^{2-\beta}\zeta|L|^{2}\left(\frac{erf(\frac{x}{2\sqrt{t}})-erf(\frac{x-L}{2\sqrt{t}})}{4^{1/2}\pi t}\right)=0

Theorem 7.8
.

(
Double-sided bound on volatility and p-moments
)
Let
u(x,t)
be a solution of the (deterministic) heat equation
\square u(x,t)=0
for
x\in{\mathbf{Q}},t>0
, with initial Cauchy data
\psi(x,0)=\phi(x)
. Then
u(x,t)=\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}h(x-y,t)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)
. Now consider the randomly
perturbed solution
\widehat{u}(x,t)
which satisfies
\square\widehat{u(x,t)}=0
for random initial Cauchy data
\widehat{\phi(x)}=\mathscr{J}(x)
. The perturbed solution is

\widehat{u(x,t)}=\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)
.
If the following double-sided integral bounds from Lemma (2.5) hold

(7.20)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{1}^{2}t^{-n}\int_{{\mathbf{R}}}\exp\left(-\frac{2|x-y|^{2}}{\Lambda_{1}t}\right)d\mu_{n}(y)~\leq~\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{n}}|h(x-y;t)|^{2}d^{n}y~\leq~\Lambda_{2}^{2}t^{-n}\int_{{\mathbf{R}}}\exp\left(-2\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Lambda_{2}t}\right)d\mu_{n}(y)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{1}^{2}t^{-pn/2}\int_{\bm{\mathrm{R}}}\exp\left(-\frac{p|x-y|^{2}}{\Lambda_{1}t}\right)d\mu_{n}(y)~\leq~\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{n}}|h(x-y;t)|^{p}d\mu_{n}(y)~

(7.21)

\displaystyle\leq~\Lambda_{2}^{2}t^{-pn/2}\int_{\bm{\mathrm{R}}}\exp\left(-p\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Lambda_{2}t}\right)d\mu_{n}(y)

then the volatility and p-moments satisfy the double-sided bounds

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\Lambda_{1}t^{-n/2}\int_{\bm{\mathrm{R}}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Lambda_{1}t}\right)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d^{n}y\right|^{2}\right\rrbracket~\leq~\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\int_{\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}}|h(x-y;t)|\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d^{n}y\right|^{2}\right\rrbracket~

(7.22)

\displaystyle\leq~\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\Lambda_{2}^{2}t^{-n/2}\int_{\bm{\mathrm{R}}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Lambda_{2}t}\right)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{2}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\Lambda_{1}t^{-n/2}\int_{\bm{\mathrm{R}}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Lambda_{1}t}\right)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p}\right\rrbracket~\leq~\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\int_{\mathrm{R}^{n}}|h(x-y;t)|\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d^{n}y\right|^{p}\right\rrbracket~

(7.23)

\displaystyle\leq~\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\Lambda_{2}^{2}t^{-n/2}\int_{\bm{\mathrm{R}}}e^{-p\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Lambda_{2}t}}\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p}\right\rrbracket

Proof.

Apply the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality to each term to get

\displaystyle\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\Lambda_{1}^{2}t^{-n}\exp\left(-2\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Lambda_{1}t}\right)d^{n}y\right)\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\mathscr{J}(x)|^{2}\rrbracket d\mu_{n}(y)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}}|h(x-y;t)|^{2}d^{n}y\right)\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\mathscr{J}(x)|^{2}\rrbracket d\mu_{n}(y)\right)

(7.24)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\Lambda_{2}^{2}t^{-n}\exp\left(-2\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Lambda_{2}t}\right)d^{n}y\right)\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\mathscr{J}(x)|^{2}\rrbracket d\mu_{n}(y)\right)

Cancelling the integrals
\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\mathscr{J}(x)|^{2}\rrbracket d^{n}y
then gives (6.20). The bound (6.21) is established in
the same manner.
∎

7.1.
Multiplicative random perturbations or noise

The previous estimates considered additive random perturbations or noise on the initial data such that
\widehat{u(x,0)}=u(x,0)+\mathscr{J}(x)
. Here, the case of
the stochastic CIVP is considered for multiplicative noise such that
\widehat{u(x,t)}=u(x,0)\mathscr{J}(x)
.

Theorem 7.9
.

Let
\widehat{u(x,t)}
satisfy the stochastic Cauchy initial-value problem on a finite domain
{\mathbf{Q}}\subset{\mathbf{R}}^{n}
with random initial Cauchy data, for the heat equation

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\widehat{u(x,t)}={\Delta}\widehat{u(x,t)},~~(x\in{\mathbf{Q}},t\in[0,\infty)

(7.25)

\displaystyle\widehat{u(x,0)}=\phi(x)\otimes\mathscr{J}(x),~~(x\in{\mathbf{Q}},t=0)

with Gaussian random scalar field
\mathscr{J}(x)
and
\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket|\mathscr{J}(x)|^{p}\big\rrbracket=\frac{1}{2}[\zeta^{\frac{p}{2}}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{\frac{p}{2}}]
. Then:

(1)

The solution is the stochastic convolution integral

(7.26)

\widehat{u(x,t)}={(4\pi t)^{-n/2}}\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4p}\right)\phi(y)\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\equiv\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}h(x-y,t)\phi(y)\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

(2)

The p-moments are finite and bounded with the estimate

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}\big\rrbracket

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}[\zeta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{p/2}]v(\mathbf{Q})

(7.27)

\displaystyle\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}{(4\pi t)^{-n/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right)d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{p}\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\phi(y)|d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{p}<\infty

(3)

The volatility is given for
p=2
so that

(7.28)

\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}\big\rrbracket\leq|C|^{2}\beta v({\mathbf{Q}})\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}{(4\pi t)^{-n/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right)d^{n}y\right)^{2}\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\phi(y)|d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{2}<\infty

Proof.

To prove (1)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\widehat{u(x,t)}=\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}h(x-y,t)\phi(y)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

(7.29)

\displaystyle{\Delta}_{x}\widehat{u(x,t)}=\int_{\mathbf{Q}}{\Delta}_{x}h(x-y,t)(y)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

so that

(7.30)

\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-{\Delta}_{x}\right)\widehat{u(x,t)}=\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-{\Delta}_{x}\right)h(x-y,t)\phi(y)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d^{n}y=0

since the fundamental solution or heat kernel
h(x-y,t)
satisfies the heat equation
To prove (2),use the holder inequality for three functions with
\tfrac{1}{p}+\tfrac{1}{q}+\tfrac{1}{\ell}=1
and
\ell=q
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket\widehat{u(x,t)}\big\rrbracket=\left\llbracket\left|{(4\pi t)^{-n/2}}\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4p}\right)\phi(y)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d^{n}y\right|^{p}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)|^{p}d\mu_{n}(y)\right\rrbracket\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\left|{(4\pi t)^{-n/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right)\right|^{q}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{p/q}\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\phi(y)|^{\ell}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{p/\ell}

\displaystyle\equiv\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\mathscr{J}(y)|^{p}d\mu_{n}(y)\right\rrbracket\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\left|{(4\pi t)^{-n/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right)\right|^{q}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{p/q}\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\phi(y)|^{q}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{p/q}

\displaystyle\equiv\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\mathscr{J}(y)|^{p}d\mu_{n}(y)\right\rrbracket\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\left|{(4\pi t)^{-n/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right)\right|^{p/(p-1)}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{p-1}

\displaystyle\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\phi(y)|^{p/p-1}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{p-1}

\displaystyle\equiv\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket|\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)|^{p}\right\rrbracket d^{n}y\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\left|{(4\pi t)^{-n/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right)\right|d^{n}y\right)^{p}\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\phi(y)|d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{p}

(7.31)

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{2}[\zeta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{p/2}]v(\mathbf{Q})\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}{(4\pi t)^{-n/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right)d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{p}\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\phi(y)|d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{p}

∎

Corollary 7.10
.

If
\phi(x)=C
then

(7.32)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket\widehat{u(x,t)}\big\rrbracket\leq\frac{1}{2}|C|^{p}[\zeta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{p/2}]|v({\mathbf{Q}})|^{p+1}\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\left|{(4\pi t)^{-n/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right)\right|d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{p}

Corollary 7.11
.

The moments and volatility decay to zero as
t\rightarrow\infty
so that

\displaystyle\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket\widehat{|u(x,t)|^{p}}\big\rrbracket\leq\frac{1}{2}[\zeta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{p/2}]v(\mathbf{Q})

\displaystyle\otimes\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}{(4\pi t)^{-n/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right)d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{p}\left(\int_{\bm{\mathbf{Q}}}|\phi(y)|d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{p}=0

(7.33)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket\widehat{|u(x,t)|^{2}}\big\rrbracket\leq\zeta v(\mathbf{Q})\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}{(4\pi t)^{-n/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right)d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{2}\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\phi(y)|d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{2}=0

which is guaranteed since
\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}{(4\pi t)^{-n/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right)d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{2}=0
for all
(x,y)\in{\mathbf{Q}}
.

7.2.
Moments estimates in a ball
\mathbf{B}_{R}(0)\subset\mathbf{R}^{3}

The moments and volatility estimates can be evaluated specifically for an Euclidean ball
{\mathbf{Q}}={\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)\subset{\mathbf{R}}^{3}
of radius
R
.

Theorem 7.12
.

Let
{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)\subset{\mathbf{R}}^{3}
be an Euclidean ball of radius
R
. Then for the following stochastic Cauchy initial value problem

(7.34)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\Delta\right)\widehat{u(x,t)}=0,~~(x\in{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0),t>0)

(7.35)

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi(x,0)}=\phi(x)+\mathscr{J}(x)=C+\mathscr{J}(x),~~(x\in{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0),t=0)

(7.36)

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi(x,R)}=0,~~(x\in{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0),t>0)

where
\mathscr{J}(x)
is the usual GRSF. If
x=(0,0,a)
with
\|x\|=a
and
a\leq R
then the p-moment are estimated as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}\big\rrbracket

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2^{p+1}}{3}\pi R^{3}(|C|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}[\zeta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{p/2}]\bigg\{\frac{1}{2}\bigg(erf\left(\frac{R+a}{2\sqrt{t}}\right)+erf\left(\frac{R+a}{2\sqrt{t}}\right)\bigg)

(7.37)

\displaystyle-\sqrt{\frac{t}{\pi}}\frac{1}{a}\big(\exp\left(\frac{at}{t}\right)-1\big)\exp\left(-\frac{|R+a|^{2}}{4t}\right)\bigg\}^{p}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket|\widehat{\psi(a,t)}|^{p}\big\rrbracket

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{3}\pi R^{3}\sum_{\beta=0}^{p}\binom{p}{\beta}|C|^{p-\beta}[\zeta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{p/2}]\bigg\{\frac{1}{2}\bigg(erf\left(\frac{R+a}{2\sqrt{t}}\right)+erf\left(\frac{R+a}{2\sqrt{t}}\right)\bigg)

(7.38)

\displaystyle-\sqrt{\frac{t}{\pi}}\frac{1}{a}\big(\exp\left(\frac{at}{t}\right)-1\big)\exp\left(-\frac{|R+a|^{2}}{4t}\right)\bigg\}^{2p-\beta}

and
\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}\big\rrbracket=0

Proof.

The estimate (6.37) is

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket|\widehat{\psi(a,t)}|^{p}\big\rrbracket

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p-1}|C|^{p}v({\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0))+\frac{1}{2}[\zeta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{p/2}]v(\mathbf{B}_{R}(0))

(7.39)

\displaystyle\times\left(\int_{{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)}{(4\pi t)^{-n/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right)d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{p}

and the estimate(6.38)is

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}\big\rrbracket

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\char 16\relax=1}^{p}\binom{p}{\beta}C^{p-\beta}[\zeta^{\frac{p}{2}}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{\frac{p}{2}}]|v({\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0))|^{p}

(7.40)

\displaystyle\times\left(\int_{\bm{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)}{(4\pi t)^{-n/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right)d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{2p-\beta}

Choosing a point
x=(0,0,a)
on the z-axis then
\|x\|=a\leq L
and
|x-y|=\sqrt{(a^{2}-2ar\cos\theta+r^{2})}
. The volume integral is
\int_{\bm{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)}d\mu_{n}(y)\equiv 2\pi\int_{0}^{R}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}r^{2}dr\sin\theta d\theta d\phi
. It is only necessary to evaluate the Gaussian integral of the heat kernel or fundamental solution, over the ball

\int_{{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)}{(4\pi t)^{-n/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right)d\mu_{n}(y)

Then

\displaystyle\left(\int_{{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)}{(4\pi t)^{-3/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}\right)d^{3}y\right)

\displaystyle=2\pi{(4\pi t)^{-3/2}}\int_{0}^{R}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\exp\left(-(\frac{(a^{2}-2ar\cos\theta+r^{2}}{4t}\right))\sin d\theta r^{2}dr

\displaystyle=2\pi{(4\pi t)^{-3/2}}\int_{0}^{R}\left|\int_{-1}^{1}\exp\left(-((a^{2}-2ar\zeta+r^{2})/{4t}\right))d\zeta\right|r^{2}dr

\displaystyle=2\pi{(4\pi t)^{-3/2}}\int_{0}^{R}\left|\frac{2t}{ar}e^{-r^{2}/4t+(ar/2t)-(a^{2}/4t)}-\frac{2t}{ar}e^{-(r^{2}/4t)-(ar/2t)-(a^{2}/4t)}\right|r^{2}dr

(7.41)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\bigg(erf\left(\frac{R+a}{2\sqrt{t}}\right)+erf\left(\frac{R-a}{2\sqrt{t}}\right)\bigg)-\sqrt{\frac{t}{\pi}}\frac{1}{a}\big(e^{\frac{at}{t}}-1\big)e^{-\frac{|R+a|^{2}}{4t}}

Where
\zeta=\cos\theta
and the error function has the properties
erf(-\infty)=-1,erf(0)=0,erf(\infty)=1
. Note that the integral over all space is unity as required, so that

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbf{R}}{(4\pi t)^{-3/2}}e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}}d^{3}y\right)=2\pi{(4\pi t)^{-3/2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}e^{-((a^{2}-2ar\cos\theta+r^{2})/{4t}})\sin d\theta r^{2}dr

\displaystyle=2\pi{(4\pi t)^{-3/2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left|\int_{-1}^{1}e^{-((a^{2}-2ar\zeta+r^{2})/{4t}})d\zeta\right|r^{2}dr

\displaystyle=2\pi{(4\pi t)^{-3/2}}\int_{0}^{R}\left|\frac{2t}{ar}e^{-r^{2}/4t+(ar/2t)-(a^{2}/4t)}-\frac{2t}{ar}e^{-(r^{2}/4t)-(ar/2t)-(a^{2}/4t)}\right|r^{2}dr

(7.42)

\displaystyle=\lim_{R\uparrow\infty}\frac{1}{2}\bigg(erf\left(\frac{R+a}{2\sqrt{t}}\right)+erf\left(\frac{R-a}{2\sqrt{t}}\right)\bigg)-\sqrt{\frac{t}{\pi}}\frac{1}{a}\big(e^{\frac{at}{t}}-1\big)e^{-\frac{|R+a|^{2}}{4t}}=0

Since
v(\mathbf{Q})=\int_{{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)}d^{3}y=2\pi\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\int_{0}^{R}r^{2}dr\sin\theta d\theta d\phi=\tfrac{4}{3}\pi R^{3}
, the estimates (6.37 )and(6.38) readily follow and the volatility is for
p=2
. The moments then decay rapidly to zero as
t\rightarrow\infty
.
∎

7.3.
Moments estimates for the inhomogeneous problem

The estimates for the p-moments and volatility can be readily extended to the inhomogeneous problem.

Theorem 7.13
.

Let
f\in C^{2}(\mathbf{Q}\times[0,\infty)
and
g\in C^{2}(\mathbf{Q})
and consider the stochastic IVP for the inhomogenous heat equation

(7.43)

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\widehat{u(x,t)}=\Delta\widehat{u(x,t)}+f(x,t),~~(x\in\mathbf{Q},t>0)

(1)

If
\widehat{\psi(x,0)}=\phi(x)+\mathscr{J}(x)
is the initial random Cauchy data then
the moments are estimated as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}\big\rrbracket\leq 3^{p-1}\left|\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\frac{e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}}}{(4\pi|t-s|)^{n/2}}f(y,s)d\mu_{n}(y)ds\right|^{p}

(7.44)

\displaystyle+3^{p-1}\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\phi(y)|^{p}d^{n}y+\frac{1}{2}[\zeta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{p/2}]v(\mathbf{Q})\right)\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{-n/2}}e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{p}

and the volatility is

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}\big\rrbracket\leq 3\left|\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\frac{e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}}}{(4\pi|t-s|)^{n/2}}f(y,s)d\mu_{n}(y)ds\right|^{2}

(7.45)

\displaystyle+3\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\phi(y)|^{2}d^{n}y+\beta v(\mathbf{Q})\right)\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{-n/2}}e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{2}

(2)

If
\widehat{u(x,0)}=\phi(x)\mathscr{J}(x)
is the initial random Cauchy data then
the moments are estimated as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}\big\rrbracket\leq 3^{p-1}\left|\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\frac{e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}}}{(4\pi|t-s|)^{n/2}}f(y,s)d\mu_{n}(y)ds\right|^{p}

(7.46)

\displaystyle+3^{p-1}\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\phi(y)|^{p}d^{n}\right)[\zeta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{p/2}]v(\mathbf{Q})\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{-n/2}}e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{p}

and the volatility is

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}\big\rrbracket\leq 3^{p-1}\left|\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\frac{e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}}}{(4\pi|t-s|)^{n/2}}f(y,s)d\mu_{n}(y)ds\right|^{p}

(7.47)

\displaystyle+3\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}|\phi(y)|^{p}d^{n}\right)\zeta v(\mathbf{Q})\left(\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{-n/2}}e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{2}

Proof.

The solution is the stochastic convolution integral so that for (1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}\big\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigg\llbracket\bigg|\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\frac{e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}}}{(4\pi|t-s|)^{n/2}}f(y,s)d\mu_{n}(y)ds+\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}}\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}}\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\bigg|^{p}\bigg\rrbracket

\displaystyle\leq 3^{p-1}\left|\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\frac{e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}}}{(4\pi|t-s|)^{n/2}}f(y,s)d\mu_{n}(y)ds\right|^{p}

(7.48)

\displaystyle+\underbrace{3^{p-1}\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}}\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p}+3^{p-1}\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}}\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p}\right\rrbracket}

The underbraced term is then estimated as before and the volatility estimate follows for
p=2
. For (2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}\big\rrbracket

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\frac{e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}}}{(4\pi|t-s|)^{n/2}}f(y,s)d\mu_{n}(y)ds+\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}}\phi(y)\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p}\right\rrbracket

(7.49)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p-1}\left|\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\frac{e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}}}{(4\pi|t-s|)^{n/2}}f(y,s)d\mu_{n}(y)ds\right|^{p}+\underbrace{2^{p-1}\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}}\phi(y)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p}\right\rrbracket}

and the underbraced term is estimated as before. The volatility estimates follows for
p=2
.
∎

Corollary 7.14
.

If

(7.50)

\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\left|\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\frac{e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}}}{(4\pi|t-s|)^{n/2}}f(y,s)d\mu_{n}(y)ds\right|=0

it follows that
\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}\big\rrbracket=0
for the estimates.

Theorem 7.15
.

(
\underline{Moments~estimate~for~a~ring}
)
Let
{\mathbf{S}}^{1}\subset{\mathbf{R}}^{1}
be a circle or ring and consider the
CIVP for the randomly perturbed heat equation with GRF initial data and periodic boundary conditions

\displaystyle{\square}\widehat{\psi(\theta,t)}=\partial_{\theta}\widehat{\psi(\theta,t)}-\partial_{\theta\theta}\widehat{\psi(\theta,t)}=0,~\theta\in{\mathbf{S}}^{1},t>0

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi(\theta,0)}=q(\theta)+\mathscr{J}(\theta),~\theta\in\bm{\mathbf{S}}^{1},t=0

\displaystyle\psi(\theta,t)=\psi(\theta+2\pi,t)

where
\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(\theta)\rrbracket=0
and

\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(|\theta)|^{p}\rrbracket=\frac{1}{2}[\eta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\eta^{p/2}]
. The solution is the (random) Fourier series

(7.51)

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi(\theta,t)}=A_{o}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}e^{-k^{2}t}(\widehat{A_{k}}\cos(k\theta)+\widehat{B_{k}}\sin(k\theta)]

where the stochastic Fourier coefficients are

(7.52)

\displaystyle\widehat{A_{k}}=A_{k}+\mathscr{A}_{k}=\pi^{-1}\oint q(\theta)\cos(k\theta)d\theta+\pi^{-1}\oint\mathscr{J}(\theta)\cos(k\theta)d\theta

(7.53)

\displaystyle\widehat{B_{k}}=B_{k}+\mathscr{B}_{k}=\pi^{-1}\oint Q(\theta)\sin(k\theta)d\theta+\pi^{-1}\oint\mathscr{J}(\theta)\sin(k\theta)d\theta

and where
\oint\equiv\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}
. Their expectations are then

(7.54)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket\widehat{A_{k}}\rrbracket=A_{k}+\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{A}_{k}\rrbracket=\pi^{-1}\oint q(\theta)\cos(k\theta)d\theta+\pi^{-1}\oint\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(\theta)\rrbracket\cos(k\theta)d\theta=A_{k}

(7.55)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket\widehat{B_{k}}\rrbracket=B_{k}+\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{B}_{k}\rrbracket=\pi^{-1}\oint q(\theta)\sin(k\theta)d\theta+\pi^{-1}\oint\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(\theta)\rrbracket\sin(k\theta)d\theta=B_{k}

The moments estimate is then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{\psi(\theta,t)}|^{p}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2^{3p}}\left|\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-k^{2}t}A_{k}\cos(k\theta)\right|^{p}+\frac{1}{2^{3p}}\left|\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-k^{2}t}B_{k}\sin(k\theta)\right|^{p}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2^{3p}}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\inf}\left|e^{-k^{2}t}\cos(k\theta)\right|^{q}\right)^{p/q}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left(\pi^{-1}\oint_{\mathbf{S}^{1}}|\cos(k\theta)|^{q}d\theta\right)^{p/q}\left(\pi[\eta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\eta^{p/2}]\right)

(7.56)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2^{3p}}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\inf}\left|e^{-k^{2}t}\sin(k\theta)\right|^{q}\right)^{p/q}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left(\pi^{-1}\oint_{\mathbf{S}^{1}}|\sin(k\theta)|^{q}d\theta\right)^{p/q}\left(\pi[\eta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\eta^{p/2}]\right)

and decay smoothly to zero such that

(7.57)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{\psi(\theta,t)}|^{p}\rrbracket=0

Hence, the heat equation smooths out the initial randomness in the initial data on the ring.

Proof.

Using the basic estimate
|\alpha+\beta|^{p}\leq\frac{1}{2^{p}}|\alpha|^{p}+\frac{1}{2^{p}}|\beta|^{p}
and the Holder inequality for sums, the p-moments can be estimated as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket|\widehat{\psi(\theta,t)}|^{p}\right\rrbracket=\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}e^{-k^{2}t}(\widehat{A_{k}}\cos(k\theta)+\widehat{B_{k}}\sin(k\theta)]\right|^{p}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\frac{1}{2^{p}}|A_{o}|^{p}+\frac{1}{2^{p}}\left|\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-k^{2}t}(\widehat{A_{k}}\cos(k\theta)+\widehat{B_{k}}\sin(k\theta)]\right|^{p}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\frac{1}{2^{p}}\left|\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-k^{2}t}(\widehat{A_{k}}\cos(k\theta)+\widehat{B_{k}}\sin(k\theta)]\right|^{p}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\frac{1}{2^{p}}\left|\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-k^{2}t}\widehat{A_{k}}\cos(k\theta)+\frac{1}{2^{p}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-k^{2}t}\widehat{B_{k}}\sin(k\theta)]\right|^{p}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\frac{1}{2^{2p}}\left|\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-k^{2}t}\widehat{A_{k}}\cos(k\theta)\right|^{p}+\frac{1}{2^{2p}}\left|\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-k^{2}t}\widehat{B_{k}}\sin(k\theta)]\right|^{p}\right\rrbracket=

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2^{p}}|\frac{1}{2^{2p}}\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}e^{-k^{2}t}\widehat{A_{k}}\cos(k\theta)\right|^{p}\right\rrbracket+\frac{1}{2^{2p}}\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-k^{2}t}\widehat{B_{k}}\sin(k\theta)]\right|^{p}\right\rrbracket=

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{2p}}\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-k^{2}t}(A_{k}+\mathscr{A}_{k})\cos(k\theta)\right|^{p}\right\rrbracket+\frac{1}{2^{2p}}\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-k^{2}t}(B_{k}+\mathscr{B}_{k})\sin(k\theta)]\right|^{p}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{2p}}\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-k^{2}t}A_{k}\cos(k\theta)+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}e^{-k^{2}t}\mathscr{A}_{k}\cos(k\theta)\right|^{p}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2^{2p}}\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-k^{2}t}B_{k}\sin(k\theta)+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}e^{-k^{2}t}\mathscr{B}_{k}\sin(k\theta)\right|^{p}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2^{2p}}\left(\frac{1}{2^{p}}\left|\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-k^{2}t}A_{k}\cos(k\theta)\right|^{p}+\frac{1}{2^{p}}\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\underbrace{\left|\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-k^{2}t}\mathscr{A}_{k}\cos(k\theta)\right|^{p}}_{apply~Holder~inequality}\right\rrbracket\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2^{2p}}\left(\frac{1}{2^{p}}\left|\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-k^{2}t}B_{k}\sin(k\theta)\right|^{p}+\frac{1}{2^{p}}\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\underbrace{\left|\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-k^{2}t}\mathscr{B}_{k}\sin(k\theta)\right|^{p}}_{apply~Holder~inequality}\right\rrbracket\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2^{3p}}\left|\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-k^{2}t}A_{k}\cos(k\theta)\right|^{p}+\frac{1}{2^{3p}}\left|\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-k^{2}t}B_{k}\sin(k\theta)\right|^{p}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2^{3p}}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\inf}\left|e^{-k^{2}t}\cos(k\theta)\right|^{q}\right)^{p/q}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\mathscr{A}_{k}|^{p}\rrbracket+\frac{1}{2^{3p}}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\inf}\left|e^{-k^{2}t}\sin(k\theta)\right|^{q}\right)^{p/q}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\mathscr{B}_{k}|^{p}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2^{3p}}\left|\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-k^{2}t}A_{k}\cos(k\theta)\right|^{p}+\frac{1}{2^{3p}}\left|\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-k^{2}t}B_{k}\sin(k\theta)\right|^{p}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2^{3p}}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\inf}\left|e^{-k^{2}t}\cos(k\theta)\right|^{q}\right)^{p/q}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\underbrace{\left|\pi^{-1}\oint_{\mathfrak{S}^{1}}\mathscr{J}(\theta)\cos(k\theta)d\theta\right|^{p}}_{apply~Holder~ineq~for~integrals}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2^{3p}}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\inf}\left|e^{-k^{2}t}\sin(k\theta)\right|^{q}\right)^{p/q}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\underbrace{\left|\pi^{-1}\oint_{\mathfrak{S}^{1}}\mathscr{J}(\theta)\sin(k\theta)d\theta\right|^{p}}_{apply~Holder~ineq.~for~integrals}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2^{3p}}\left|\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-k^{2}t}A_{k}\cos(k\theta)\right|^{p}+\frac{1}{2^{3p}}\left|\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-k^{2}t}B_{k}\sin(k\theta)\right|^{p}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2^{3p}}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\inf}\left|e^{-k^{2}t}\cos(k\theta)\right|^{q}\right)^{p/q}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left(\pi^{-1}\oint_{\mathbf{S}^{1}}|\cos(k\theta)|^{q}d\theta\right)^{p/q}\left(\oint_{\mathfrak{S}^{1}}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\mathscr{J}(\theta)|^{p}\rrbracket d\theta\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2^{3p}}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\inf}\left|e^{-k^{2}t}\sin(k\theta)\right|^{q}\right)^{p/q}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left(\pi^{-1}\oint_{\mathbf{S}^{1}}|\sin(k\theta)|^{q}d\theta\right)^{p/q}\left(\oint_{\mathbf{S}^{1}}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\mathscr{J}(\theta)|^{p}\rrbracket d\theta\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{3p}}\left|\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-k^{2}t}A_{k}\cos(k\theta)\right|^{p}+\frac{1}{2^{3p}}\left|\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-k^{2}t}B_{k}\sin(k\theta)\right|^{p}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2^{3p}}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\inf}\left|e^{-k^{2}t}\cos(k\theta)\right|^{q}\right)^{p/q}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left(\pi^{-1}\oint_{\mathbf{S}^{1}}|\cos(k\theta)|^{q}d\theta\right)^{p/q}\left(\frac{1}{2}\oint_{\mathbf{S}^{1}}[\eta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\eta^{p/2}]d\theta\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2^{3p}}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\inf}\left|e^{-k^{2}t}\sin(k\theta)\right|^{q}\right)^{p/q}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left(\pi^{-1}\oint_{\mathbf{S}^{1}}|\sin(k\theta)|^{q}d\theta\right)^{p/q}\left(\frac{1}{2}\oint_{\mathfrak{S}^{1}}[\eta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\eta^{p/2}]d\theta\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{3p}}\left|\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-k^{2}t}A_{k}\cos(k\theta)\right|^{p}+\frac{1}{2^{3p}}\left|\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-k^{2}t}B_{k}\sin(k\theta)\right|^{p}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2^{3p}}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\inf}\left|e^{-k^{2}t}\cos(k\theta)\right|^{q}\right)^{p/q}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left(\pi^{-1}\oint_{\mathbf{S}^{1}}|\cos(k\theta)|^{q}d\theta\right)^{p/q}\left(\pi[\eta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\eta^{p/2}]\right)

(7.58)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2^{3p}}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\inf}\left|e^{-k^{2}t}\sin(k\theta)\right|^{q}\right)^{p/q}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left(\pi^{-1}\oint_{\mathbf{S}^{1}}|\sin(k\theta)|^{q}d\theta\right)^{p/q}\left(\pi[\eta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\eta^{p/2}]\right)

This is smoothed out and decays as
t\rightarrow\infty
. The sums

(7.59)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left(\pi^{-1}\oint_{\mathbf{S}^{1}}|\cos(k\theta)|^{q}d\theta\right)^{p/q}

(7.60)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left(\pi^{-1}\oint_{\mathbf{S}^{1}}|\sin(k\theta)|^{q}d\theta\right)^{p/q}

are convergent and finite for all
(p,q)
such that
\tfrac{1}{p}+\tfrac{1}{q}=1
.
∎

7.4.
Alternative estimate for the p-moments and volatility

An alternative estimate can be made as follows

Theorem 7.16
.

Let
\bm{\mathbf{Q}}\subset{\mathbf{R}}^{n}
and
\widehat{u(x,t)}
satisfies the SCIVP. If
\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\phi(x)|^{p}d\mu_{n}(x)\leq\lambda
and
\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\mathscr{J}(x)|^{p}\rrbracket=\tfrac{1}{2}[\zeta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{p/2}]
then the a p-moments estimate is

(7.61)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}\rrbracket\leq 2^{p-2}\left[\lambda^{p}+\frac{1}{2}v({\mathbf{Q}}[\zeta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{p/2}]\right]\left(\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n}}\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{2t}}d\mu_{n}(y)\right)^{p}

Then:

(1)

For
\bm{\mathbf{Q}}=\bm{\mathfrak{L}}[0,\ell]\subset\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{+}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p-2}\left[\lambda^{p}+\frac{1}{2}\ell[\zeta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{p/2}]\right]\left(\frac{1}{(4\pi t)}\int_{\bm{\mathbf{Q}}}e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{2t}}dy\right)^{p}

(7.62)

\displaystyle=2^{p-2}\left[\lambda^{p}+\frac{1}{2}\ell[\zeta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{p/2}]\right]\left(\frac{(erf\left(\frac{x}{2t^{1/2}}\right)-erf\left(\frac{x-\ell}{2t^{1/2}}\right)}{4(\pi t)^{1/2}}\right)^{p}

(2)

For
\bm{\mathrm{D}}=\bm{\mathrm{B}}_{R}(0)\subset\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{3}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p-2}\left[\lambda^{p}+\frac{1}{2}\ell[\zeta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{p/2}]\right]\left(\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{3}}\int_{{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)}e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{2t}}d^{3}y\right)^{p}

(7.63)

\displaystyle=2^{p-2}\left[\lambda^{p}+\frac{2}{3}\pi R^{3}[\zeta^{p/2}+(-1)^{p}\zeta^{p/2}]\right]\left(\frac{e^{-\frac{R^{2}}{2t}}(\pi^{1/2}t^{3/2}e^{\frac{R^{2}}{2t}}erf\left(\frac{R}{2^{1/2}t}\right)-2^{1/2}tR}{2^{1/2}t}\right)^{p}

(3)

The moments decay as
t\rightarrow\infty
so that

(7.64)

\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}\rrbracket

The volatility is given for
p=2
.

Proof.

The p-moments are

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}\rrbracket=\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}h(x-y,t)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)+\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p}\right\rrbracket

(7.65)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p-1}\left|\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}h(x-y,t)\phi(y)d^{n}y\right|^{p}+2^{p-1}\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p}\right\rrbracket

Since
|h(x-y,t)\phi(y)|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}|\phi(y)|^{2}
then

\displaystyle|h(x-y,t)\phi(y)|^{p}\leq(\tfrac{1}{2}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}|\phi(y)|^{2})^{p}\leq 2^{p-1}(|\tfrac{1}{2}|h(x-y,t)|^{2})^{p}+2^{p-1}(|\tfrac{1}{2}|\phi(y)|^{2})^{p}

(7.66)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}|h(x-y,t)|^{2p}+\tfrac{1}{2}|\phi(y)|^{2p}

and

(7.67)

\displaystyle\int|h(x-y,t)\phi(y)|d\mu_{n}(y)\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\int|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d^{n}y+\tfrac{1}{2}\int|\phi(y)|^{2}d^{n}y

so that

\displaystyle\left|\int|h(x-y,t)\phi(y)|d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p}\leq\left|\tfrac{1}{2}\int|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d^{n}y+\tfrac{1}{2}\int|\phi(y)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p-1}\left|\tfrac{1}{2}\int|h(x-y,t)|^{2}dx\right|^{p}+2^{p-1}\left|\tfrac{1}{2}\int|\phi(y)|^{2}dx\right|^{p}=\tfrac{1}{2}\left|\int|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p}

(7.68)

\displaystyle+\tfrac{1}{2}\left|\int|\phi(y)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p}

The p-moments are then estimated as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{p}\rrbracket=\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}h(x-y,t)\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)+\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p-1}\left|\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\phi(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p}+2^{p-1}\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle=2^{p-1}\tfrac{1}{2}\left|\int|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p}+2^{p-1}\tfrac{1}{2}\left|\int|\phi(y)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p}

\displaystyle+2^{p-1}\tfrac{1}{2}\left|\int|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p}+2^{p-1}\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\int|\mathscr{G}(y)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)y\right|^{p}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle=2^{p-2}\left|\int|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p}+2^{p-2}\left|\int|\phi(y)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p}

(7.69)

\displaystyle+2^{p-2}\left|\int|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p}+2^{p-2}\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\int|\mathscr{J}(y)|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{p}\right\rrbracket

∎

Lemma 7.17
.

Given the general solution
\widehat{u(x,t)}
of the stochastic initial value problem
then the stochastically averaged Dirichlet energy integral over a domain
{\mathbf{Q}}

is

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket\widehat{\bm{\mathrm{I\!E}}_{I}[t]}\rrbracket=\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}d\mu_{n}(x)\right\rrbracket\equiv\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}\right\rrbracket d\mu_{n}(x)

(7.70)

\displaystyle=\bm{\mathrm{I\!E}}_{I}[t]+\zeta v({\mathbf{Q}})\int_{\mathbf{Q}}\left\{\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}(4\pi t)^{-n}e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{2t}}d\mu_{n}(y)\right\}d\mu_{n}(x)

with the derivative and asymptotic property

(7.71)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}\llbracket\widehat{\bm{\mathrm{I\!E}}_{I}[t]}\rrbracket\leq 0

(7.72)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\llbracket\widehat{\bm{\mathrm{I\!E}}_{I}[t]}\rrbracket\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\bm{\mathrm{I\!E}}_{I}[t]=0

For
\mathbf{Q}=[0,\ell]\subset\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{+}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket\widehat{\bm{\mathrm{I\!E}}_{I}[t]}\rrbracket=\bm{\mathrm{I\!E}}_{I}[t]+\zeta\ell\int_{0}^{\ell}\left\{\int_{0}^{\ell}(4\pi t)^{-1}e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{2t}}dy\right\}dx

(7.73)

\displaystyle=\bm{\mathrm{I\!E}}_{I}[t]+\zeta\ell\left\{\frac{e^{-\frac{\ell^{2}}{2t}}\left(2^{3/2}\pi^{1/2}(1-e^{\ell^{2}/2t})\right)}{2^{5/2}\pi^{3/2}}+\frac{2\pi\ell erf\left(\frac{\ell}{(2t)^{1/2}}\right)e^{\frac{\ell^{2}}{2t}}}{t^{1/2}}\right\}

Proof.

From Thm 7.2, the volatility follows for
p=2
giving

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}\rrbracket\leq|u(x,t)|^{2}+\zeta v(\bm{\mathbf{Q}})\int_{\bm{\mathbf{Q}}}(4\pi t)^{-n}e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{2t}}d\mu_{n}(y)

(7.74)

\displaystyle\equiv|u(x,t)|^{2}+(\|h(x-\bullet,t)\|_{L_{2}({\mathbf{Q}}}))^{1/2}

(7.70) then follows from the definition of the energy integral. The estimate then follows from performing the incomplete Gaussian integrals.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket\widehat{\bm{\mathrm{I\!E}}_{I}[t]}\rrbracket=\bm{\mathrm{I\!E}}_{I}[t]+\zeta\ell\int_{0}^{\ell}\left\{\int_{0}^{\ell}(4\pi t)^{-1}e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{2t}}dy\right\}dx

(7.75)

\displaystyle=\bm{\mathrm{I\!E}}_{I}[t]+\zeta\ell\int_{0}^{\ell}\left\{\frac{erf(\frac{x}{(2t)^{1/2}})-erf(\frac{x-\ell}{(2t)^{1/2}})}{2^{5/2}(\pi t)^{1/2}}\right\}dx

Performing the second integration then gives (7.70). The extra term then vanishes as

t\rightarrow\infty
.
∎

Definition 7.18
.

Given a stochastic process
\widehat{X(t)}
for the stochastic time evolution of a
physical system, subject say to noise or random perturbations, then the p-moment ’Lyupunov
characteristic exponent’ is defined as

(7.76)

\displaystyle\bm{\mathrm{I\!L}}_{p}[\widehat{X(t)}]=\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\frac{1}{t}\log\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{X(t)}|^{p}\rrbracket

For
p=2
,

(7.77)

\displaystyle\bm{\mathrm{I\!L}}_{2}[\widehat{X(t)}]=\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\frac{1}{t}\log\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{X(t)}|^{2}\rrbracket

Then

(1)

The stochastic system is stable or ’weakly intermittent’ if
\bm{\mathrm{I\!L}}_{2}[\widehat{X(t)}]\leq 0

as
t\rightarrow\infty
and the volatility decays with time.

(2)

The stochastic system is unstable if
\bm{\mathrm{I\!L}}_{2}[\widehat{X(t)}]>0
and the volatility grows with time.
as
t\rightarrow\infty
.

(3)

The stochastic system is ’superstable’ if
\bm{\mathrm{I\!L}}_{2}[\widehat{X(t)}]=-\infty
.

From the smoothing properties of the heat equation, it is expected that

\bm{\mathrm{I\!L}}_{2}[\widehat{u(x,t)}]\leq 0
.

Proposition 7.19
.

Let
\widehat{u(x,t)}
solve the heat equation on a domain
{\mathbf{Q}}
with random initial Cauchy data
\widehat{u(x,0)}=u(x,0)+\mathscr{J}(x)
, and
u(x,t)
solves the
heat equation with initial data
\widehat{u(x,0)}=u(x,0)
with

\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}|u(x,t)|^{2}=0
. Then the LCE estimate is

(7.78)

\displaystyle\bm{\mathrm{I\!L}}_{2}[\widehat{u(x,t)}]=\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\frac{1}{t}\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}\rrbracket\leq\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}\frac{1}{t}\log\left(|u(x,t)|^{2}+\frac{\zeta v({\mathbf{Q}})}{|8\pi t|^{n/2}}\right)=0

Hence, the perturbed solution of the heat equation is stable and decays to zero. The smoothing properties of the heat equation dissipate the effects of the initial data randomness.

Proof.

From previous results, the volatility/variance is estimated as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x,t)}|^{2}\rrbracket=|u(x,t)|^{2}+\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left|\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}h(x-y,t)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n}(y)\right|^{2}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle|u(x,t)|^{2}+\zeta v({\mathbf{Q}})\left(\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}|h(x-y,t)|^{2}d^{n}\right)

(7.79)

\displaystyle=|u(x,t)|^{2}+\zeta v({\mathbf{Q}})h(x-x,2t)=|u(x,t)|^{2}+\frac{\zeta v({\mathbf{Q}})}{|8\pi t|^{n/2}|}

where the semi-group property has been used. The result (6.78) the follows.
∎

7.5.
Cole-Hopf transform for random initial data

The Cole-Hopf transform was discussed in Section 4, and can potentially be extended to consider solutions of quasi-linear parabolic PDEs with random Gaussian field initial data.

Proposition 7.20
.

Let
a>0
and
b\in{\mathbf{R}}^{+}
and consider the Cauchy problem for the following quasi-linear parabolic PDE

(7.80)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-a{\Delta}\right)\widehat{\psi(x,t)}+B|{\nabla}\widehat{\psi(x,t)}|^{2}=0,~x\in{\mathbf{R}}^{n},t>0

(7.81)

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi(x,0)}=\phi(x)+\mathscr{J}(x)=\widehat{\phi(x)},~x\in\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n},t=0

where
\mathscr{J}(x)
is the usual GRSF. Using the Cole-Hopf transform

(7.82)

\displaystyle\widehat{u(x,t)}=\exp\left(-\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\right)\widehat{\psi(x,t)}

(7.83)

\displaystyle\widehat{u(x,0)}=\exp\left(-\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\right)\widehat{\psi(x,0)}\equiv\exp\left(-\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\right)\widehat{\phi(x)}

then
\widehat{u(x,t)}
immediately satisfies the global stochastic Cauchy initial value problem for the linear heat equation (with ’conductance’
A
) such that

(7.84)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-a{\Delta}\right)\widehat{u(x,t)}=0,~x\in\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n},t>0

(7.85)

\displaystyle\widehat{u(0,t)}=\exp\left(-\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\right)\widehat{\phi(x)},~x\in\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n},t=0

This has the stochastic convolution integral solution

(7.86)

\displaystyle\widehat{u(x,t)}=\frac{1}{(4\pi at)^{n/2}}\int_{\mathrm{R}^{n}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4at}\right)\exp\left(-\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\widehat{\phi(x)}\right)d\mu_{n}(y)

(7.87)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(4\pi at)^{n/2}}\int_{\mathrm{R}^{n}}\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4at}\right)\exp\left(-\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\phi(x)\right)\exp\left(-\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\otimes\mathscr{J}(x)\right)d\mu_{n}(y)

It follows from (7.82) that the solution to the CIVP for the quasi-linear parabolic system (7.80) is then

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi(x,t)}

\displaystyle=-\bigg(\frac{a}{b}\bigg)\log\bigg((4\pi at)^{-n/2}\int_{\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}}\exp\bigg(\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4at}\bigg)

(7.88)

\displaystyle\exp\bigg(-\bigg(\frac{b}{a}\bigg)\phi(x)\bigg)\exp\bigg(-\bigg(\frac{b}{a}\bigg)\otimes\mathscr{J}(x)\bigg)d\mu_{n}(y)\bigg)

The expectation is

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket\widehat{\psi(x,t)}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=-\bigg(\frac{a}{b}\bigg)\mathbb{E}\bigg\llbracket\log\bigg((4\pi at)^{-n/2}\int_{\mathrm{R}^{n}}\exp\bigg(\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4at}\bigg)

(7.89)

\displaystyle\exp\bigg(-\bigg(\frac{b}{a}\bigg)\phi(x)\bigg)\exp\bigg(-\bigg(\frac{b}{a}\bigg)\otimes\mathscr{J}(x)\bigg)d\mu_{n}(y)\bigg)\bigg\rrbracket

and the
p-
moments are

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{\psi(x,t)}|^{p}\rrbracket=\bigg(-\bigg(\frac{b}{a}\bigg)\bigg)^{p}\mathbb{E}\bigg\llbracket\bigg|\log\bigg({(4\pi at)^{-n/2}}

(7.90)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{R}^{n}}\exp\bigg(\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4at}\bigg)\exp\bigg(-\bigg(\frac{b}{a}\bigg)\phi(x)\bigg)\exp\bigg(-\bigg(\frac{b}{a}\bigg)\mathscr{J}(x)\bigg)d\mu_{n}(y)\bigg)\bigg|^{p}\bigg\rrbracket

It should also be possible to find solutions and moments for the Burgers equation with random initial data; for example, in modelling turbulence.

8.
Thermal equilibrium with random boundary conditions

The final scenario considered is the steady state or equilibrium heat problem
on a ball
{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)\subset{\mathbf{R}^{n}}
, discussed in Section 4.1.
However, now the boundary conditions are random or randomly perturbed so that
{\Delta}\widehat{u(x)}=0
for
x\in{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)
, with
\widehat{u(x)}=u(x)+\mathscr{J}(x)
, where
\mathscr{J}(x)
is the usual GRSF. One can then estimate the volatility or variance
\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(x)}|^{2}\rrbracket
within the ball or hypersphere, from the solution
\widehat{u(x)}
within the ball.

Theorem 8.1
.

Let
\subset\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}
with boundary

\partial{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)
. Let
u\in C^{2}({\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0))
and let

\mathscr{J}(x)
be a Gaussian random scalar field (GRSF) with
\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x)\rrbracket=0
and
\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x)\otimes\mathscr{J}(x)\rrbracket=\zeta
. The stochastic Dirichlet boundary value problem

(8.1)

\displaystyle{\Delta}\widehat{u(x)}=0,~x\in{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)

(8.2)

\displaystyle\widehat{u(x)}=\phi(x)+\mathscr{J}(x),~x\in\partial{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)

then has the solution

\displaystyle\widehat{u(x)}=\frac{R^{2}-\|x\|^{2}}{area(\partial{\mathbf{B}}_{1}(0))R}\int_{\partial{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)}\frac{\phi(y)d^{n-1}y}{\|x-y\|^{n}}

(8.3)

\displaystyle+\frac{R^{2}-\|x\|^{2}}{area(\partial{\mathbf{B}}_{1}(0))R}\int_{\partial\bm{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)}\frac{\mathscr{J}(y)d^{n-1}y}{\|x-y\|^{n}},~~x\in{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0),y\in\partial{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)

In terms of the Poisson kernel
{\mathrm{P}}(x,y)

(8.4)

\displaystyle\widehat{u(x)}=\int_{\partial{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)}{\mathrm{P}}(x,y)\phi(y)d^{n-1}y+\int_{\partial{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)}{\mathrm{P}}(x,y)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d^{n-1}y

where

(8.5)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{P}}(x,y)=\frac{R^{2}-\|x\|^{2}}{area(\partial{\mathbf{B}}_{1}(0))R}\frac{1}{\|x-y\|^{n}}\equiv\frac{1}{area(\partial{\mathbf{B}}_{1}(0))R}\frac{R^{2}-\|x\|^{2}}{\|x-y\|^{n}}\equiv\frac{1}{area(\partial{\mathbf{B}}_{1}(0))R}{\mathrm{P}}(x,y)

and where the stochastic convolution surface integral
\int_{\partial{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)}{\mathrm{P}}(x,y)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d^{n-1}y
can be shown to exist and can be defined in terms of a Riemann sum (Appendix A.)

Proof.

If
\widehat{u(x)}
solves the randomly perturbed Laplace equation (7.1) then

(8.6)

\displaystyle{\Delta}_{x}\widehat{u(x)}=\int_{\partial\bm{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)}{\Delta}_{x}{\mathrm{P}}(x,y)\psi(y)d^{n-1}y+\int_{\partial{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)}{\Delta}_{x}{\mathrm{P}}(x,y)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d^{n-1}y=0

so it is sufficient to prove that the (smooth) Poisson kernel is harmonic within the ball so that

(8.7)

\displaystyle{\Delta}_{x}{\mathrm{P}}(x,y)=\frac{1}{area(\partial{\mathbf{B}}_{1}(0))R}{\Delta}_{x}\left(\frac{R^{2}-\|x\|^{2}}{\|x-y\|^{n}}\right)\equiv\frac{1}{area(\partial{\mathbf{B}}_{1}(0))R}{\Delta}_{x}{\mathrm{P}}(x,y)=0

Set

(8.8)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{P}}(x,y)=V(x)W(x,y)=\frac{R^{2}-\|x\|^{2}}{\|x-y\|^{n}}

where
V=R^{2}-\|x\|^{2}\equiv\|y\|^{2}-\|x\|^{2}
and
W=\|x-y\|^{-n}
. It is then sufficient to show that
{\Delta}_{x}(VW)=0
. This requires the basic result from vector calculus that

(8.9)

\displaystyle{\Delta}(VW)=V{\Delta}W+W{\Delta}V+2{\nabla}V.{\nabla}U

The following basic results are also required

(8.10)

\displaystyle{\Delta}V={\nabla}.{\nabla}V\equiv div{\nabla}V

(8.11)

\displaystyle{\nabla}(f(V))=f^{\prime}(V){\nabla}V

(8.12)

\displaystyle{\nabla}.(V\vec{F})={\nabla}V.\vec{F}+V{\nabla}.\vec{F}

(8.13)

\displaystyle{\nabla}V=-2\|x\|,~~{\Delta}V=-2n

Using (2.11)

(8.14)

\displaystyle{\nabla}W=-n\|x-y\|^{-n-1}{\nabla}\|x-y\|=n\|x-y\|^{-n-1}\frac{(x-y)}{\|x-y\|}=-n\|x-y\|^{-n-2}(x-y)

The Laplacian is then
{\Delta}_{x}W
so that

\displaystyle{\Delta}_{x}W=-ndiv(\|x-y\|^{-n-2}(x-y))=-n(-n-2)\|x-y\|^{-n-3}\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{\|x-y\|}-n^{2}\|x-y\|^{-n-2}

(8.15)

\displaystyle=2n\|x-y\|^{-n-2}

Combining these using (7.9) then gives

\displaystyle{\Delta}(VW)=v{\Delta}W+W{\Delta}V+2{\nabla}V.{\nabla}U

(8.16)

\displaystyle=2n(\|y\|^{2}-\|x\|^{2})\|x-y\|^{-n-2}-2n\|x-y\|^{n}+4n\|x\|\|x-y\|^{-n-2}(x-y)

Multiplying out by
\|x-y\|^{n+2}
gives

\displaystyle\|x-y\|^{n+1}{\Delta}(VW)=2n(\|y\|^{2}-\|x\|^{2})-2n\|x-y\|^{2}+4n\|x\|(x-y)

\displaystyle=2n\|y\|^{2}-2n\|x\|^{2}-2n(\|x\|^{2}-2\|xy\|+\|y\|^{2})+4n\|x\|^{2}-4n\|xy\|

(8.17)

\displaystyle=2n\|y\|^{2}-2n\|x\|^{2}-2n\|x\|^{2}+4n\|xy\|-2n\|y\|^{2}+4n\|x\|^{2}-4n\|xy\|=0

and so
\bm{\mathrm{P}}(x,y)
is harmonic and
\widehat{u(x)}
is the solution to the deterministic problem.
∎

Corollary 8.2
.

The inhomogenous stochastic Dirichlet boundary value problem

(8.18)

\displaystyle{\Delta}\widehat{u(x)}=f(x),~x\in{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)

(8.19)

\displaystyle\widehat{u(x)}=u(x)+\mathscr{J}(x),~x\in\partial{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)

then has the solution

\displaystyle\widehat{u(x)}=\int_{{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)}g(x-y)f(y)d\mu_{n}(y)+\frac{R^{2}-\|x\|^{2}}{area(\partial{\mathbf{B}}_{1}(0))R}\int_{\partial{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)}\frac{\psi(y)d\mu_{n-1}(y)}{\|x-y\|^{n}}

(8.20)

\displaystyle+\frac{R^{2}-\|x\|^{2}}{area(\partial{\mathbf{B}}_{1}(0))R}\int_{\partial{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)}\frac{\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n-1}(y)}{\|x-y\|^{n}},~~x\in{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0),y\in\partial{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)

In terms of the Poisson kernel
{\mathrm{P}}(x,y)

(8.21)

\displaystyle\widehat{u(x)}=\int_{{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)}g(x-y)f(y)d\mu_{n}(y)+\int_{\partial{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)}{\mathrm{P}}(x,y)\psi(y)d\mu_{n-1}(y)+\int_{\partial{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)}{\mathrm{P}}(x,y)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n-1}(y)

so that

\displaystyle{\Delta}_{x}\widehat{u(x)}

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)}g(x,y)f(y)d\mu_{n}(y)+\int_{\partial{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)}{\Delta}_{x}{\mathrm{P}}(x,y)\psi(y)d\mu_{n-1}(y)

\displaystyle+\int_{\partial{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)}{\Delta}_{x}{\mathrm{P}}(x,y)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)d\mu_{n-1}(y)

(8.22)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)}{\Delta}_{x}\left(\frac{1}{\|x-y\|}\right)f(y)d\mu_{n}(y)=-\int_{{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)}\delta^{3}(x-y)f(y)d\mu_{n}(y)=f(x)

since
{\Delta}_{x}\bm{\mathrm{P}}(x,y)=0
.

Theorem 8.3
.

Let
\mathbf{B}_{R}(0)\subset\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{3}
be an Euclidean ball of radius R and centre
x=(0,0,0
with surface
\partial\mathbf{B}_{R}(0)
. The steady state of equilibrium heat problem on
\mathbf{B}_{R}(0)
with randomly perturbed Dirichlet boundary conditions is the perturbed Laplace equation is
{\Delta}\widehat{u(x)}=0,~x\in\mathbf{B}_{R}(0)
and
\widehat{\psi(x)}=\psi(x)+\mathscr{J}(x)=\psi+\mathscr{J}(x)
, where
\mathscr{J}(x)
is the regulated GRSF with
\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x)\otimes\mathscr{J}(x)\rrbracket=\zeta
.
If
\widehat{\psi(x)}
solves this problem then the volatility at
x=(0,0,\alpha)
for
\alpha\in[0,R]
is estimated as

(8.23)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket\widehat{|u(a)|^{2}}\rrbracket\leq\frac{1}{2}(\zeta+\psi^{2})R^{2}(R^{2}-\alpha^{2})^{2}\left\{\frac{1}{4\alpha R(-2\alpha R+R^{2}+\alpha^{2})}-\frac{1}{4\alpha R(2\alpha R+R^{2}+\alpha^{2})}\right\}

(8.24)

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{(\zeta+\psi^{2})R(R^{2}-\alpha^{2})^{2}}{8\alpha}\left\{\frac{1}{(-2\alpha R+R^{2}+\alpha^{2})}-\frac{1}{(2\alpha R+R^{2}+\alpha^{2})}\right\}

For a unit ball
\bm{\mathbf{B}}_{1}(0)
with R=1

(8.26)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket\widehat{|\psi(\alpha)|^{2}}\rrbracket\leq\frac{1}{2}(\zeta+\psi^{2})(1-\alpha^{2})^{2}\left\{\frac{1}{4\alpha(-2\alpha+1+\alpha^{2})}-\frac{1}{4\alpha(2\alpha+1+\alpha^{2})}\right\}

(8.27)

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{(\zeta+\psi^{2})(1-\alpha^{2})^{2}}{8\alpha}\left\{\frac{1}{(-2\alpha+1+\alpha^{2})}-\frac{1}{(2\alpha+1+\alpha^{2})}\right\}

This is a minimum at
\alpha=0
, the center of the ball, and increases as
a\rightarrow R
or
\alpha\rightarrow 1
. Hence, the volatility decays as we move away from the random boundary conditions as
\alpha\rightarrow 0
.

Proof.

From the solution (7.20), the volatility is estimated as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{\psi(x)}|^{2}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(\int_{\bm{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)}\frac{\psi(R^{2}-\|x\|^{2})}{4\pi R}\frac{d^{2}y}{\|x-y\|^{3}}\right)^{2}}_{apply~Cauchy-Schwarz}+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\left(\int_{\partial\bm{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)}\frac{(R^{2}-\|x\|^{2})}{4\pi R}\frac{\mathscr{J}(y)d^{2}y}{\|x-y\|^{3}}\right)^{2}\right\rrbracket}_{apply~Cauchy-Schwarz}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{\psi^{2}(R^{2}-\|x\|^{2})^{2}}{|4\pi R|^{2}}\int_{\partial{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)}\frac{d^{2}y}{\|x-y\|^{6}}\right)\left(\int_{\partial{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)}\psi^{2}d^{2}y\right)

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{\psi^{2}(R^{2}-\|x\|^{2})^{2}}{|4\pi R|^{2}}\int_{{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)}\frac{d^{2}y}{\|x-y\|^{6}}\right)\left(\int_{\partial\bm{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)}\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x)\otimes\mathscr{J}(x)\rrbracket d^{2}y\right)

\displaystyle=(\psi^{2}+\zeta)Area(\partial{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0))\left(\frac{(R^{2}-\|x\|^{2})^{2}}{|4\pi R|^{2}}\int_{{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)}\frac{d^{2}y}{\|x-y\|^{6}}\right)

\displaystyle\equiv(\psi^{2}+\zeta)4\pi R^{2}\left(\frac{(R^{2}-\|x\|^{2})^{2}}{|4\pi R|^{2}}\int_{{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)}\frac{d^{2}y}{\|x-y\|^{6}}\right)

(8.29)

\displaystyle=(\psi^{2}+\zeta)\left(\frac{(R^{2}-\|x\|^{2})^{2}}{4\pi}\int_{{\mathbf{B}}_{R}(0)}\frac{d^{2}y}{\|x-y\|^{6}}\right)

Now choosing
x=(0,0,\alpha)
gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{u(\alpha)}|^{2}\rrbracket\leq

\displaystyle\frac{(\zeta+\psi^{2})(R^{2}-|\alpha|^{2})^{2}}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\frac{R^{2}\sin\theta d\theta d\varphi}{(R^{2}-2\alpha R\cos\theta+\alpha^{2})^{3}}

(8.30)

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{2}(\zeta+\psi^{2})R^{2}(R^{2}-|\alpha|^{2})^{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{d\xi}{(R^{2}-2\alpha R\xi+\alpha^{2})^{3}}

Performing the integral then gives (8.23).
∎

Appendix A
Properties of Gaussian Random Scalar Fields on
\bm{R}^{n}

This appendix provides basic definitions of existence, properties, correlations, statistics, derivatives and integrals of time-independent Gaussian random scalar fields (GRSFs) on
\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}
. Classical random fields correspond naturally to structures, and properties of systems. It will be sufficient to briefly establish the following:

(1)

The existence of random fields on
\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}
.

(2)

Statistics, moments, covariances and correlations.

(3)

Unique properties of Gaussian random fields. (GRVFs.)

(4)

Sample path continuity, differentiability and the existence of the derivatives of a GRSF.

(5)

Stochastic integration.

Definition 1.1
.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}})
be a probability space. Within the probability triplet,
(\Omega,\mathcal{F})
is a
measurable space
, where
\mathcal{F}
is the
\sigma
-algebra (or Borel field) that should be interpreted as being comprised of all reasonable subsets of the state space
\Omega
. Then
\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}
is a function such that
\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow[0,1]
, so that for all
A\in\mathcal{F}
, there is an associated probability
\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}(A)
. The measure is a probability measure when
\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}(\Omega)=1
. The probability space obeys the Kolmogorov axioms such that
\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}(\Omega)=1
and
0\leq\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}(A_{i})\leq 1
for all sets
A_{i}\in\mathcal{F}
. And if
A_{i}\bigcap A_{j}=
, then
\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}(\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}A_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}(A_{i})
. Let
x^{i}\subset{\mathbf{Q}}\subset\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}
be Euclidean coordinates and let
(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}})
be a probability space. Let
\widehat{\mathscr{J}}(x;\omega)
be a random scalar function that depends on the coordinates
x\subset{\mathbf{Q}}\subset\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}
and also
\omega\in\Omega
. Given any pair
(x,\omega)
there exists maps
M:\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}\times\Omega\rightarrow\bm{\mathrm{R}}
such that
M:(\omega,x)\longrightarrow\mathscr{J}(x);\omega)
, so that

{\mathscr{J}(x,\omega)}
is a random variable or field on
{\mathbf{Q}}\subset\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}
with respect to the probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}})
. A random field is then essentially a family of random variables
\{\mathscr{J}(x;\omega)\}
defined with respect to the space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}})
and
\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}
.

The fields can also include a time variable
t\in\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{+}
so that given any triplet

(x,t,\omega)
there is a mapping
f:\bm{\mathrm{R}}\times\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}\times\Omega\rightarrow\bm{\mathrm{R}}
such that

f:(x,t,\omega)\longrightarrow\mathscr{J}(x,t;\omega)
. However, it will be sufficient to consider fields that vary randomly in space only. The expected value of the random field with respect to the space
(\Omega,\bm{\mathcal{F}},\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}})
is defined as follows

Definition 1.2
.

Given the random scalar field
\bm{\mathscr{J}(x;\omega)}
, then if
\int_{\Omega}{\mathscr{J}(x;\omega)}d\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}(\omega)<\infty
, the stochastic expectation of
\widehat{\mathscr{J}}(x;\omega)
is

(A.1)

\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x;\omega)\big\rrbracket=\int_{\Omega}\mathscr{J}(x;\omega)d\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}(\omega)

Definition 1.3
.

Let
(\Omega,\mathbb{F},\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}})
be a probability space, then an
L_{p}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}})
space or an
L_{p}
-space for
p\geq 1
is a linear normed space of random scalar fields that satisfies the conditions

(A.2)

\mathbb{E}\bigg\llbracket|\mathscr{J}(x;\omega)|^{p}\bigg\rrbracket=\int_{\Omega}|{\mathscr{J}(x;\omega)}|^{p}d\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}(\omega)<\infty

and the corresponding norm can be defined as
\|\widehat{\mathscr{J}}(x)\|=(\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\widehat{\mathscr{J}}(x;\omega)|^{p}\rrbracket)^{1/p}
with the usual Euclidean norm for
p=2
.

Definition 1.4
.

Let
(x,y)\in{\mathbf{Q}}\subset{\mathbf{R}}^{n}
and
(\omega,\eta)\in{\mathbf{Q}}
. The covariance of the field at these points is then formally

(A.3)

\mathbb{E}\bigg\llbracket{\mathscr{J}(x,\omega)}\otimes{\mathscr{J}}(y,\xi)\bigg\rrbracket=\iint_{\Omega}{\mathscr{J}(x,\omega)}\otimes{\mathscr{J}(y,\xi)}d\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}(\omega)d\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}(\xi)

The second-order correlations, moments and covariances are now

Definition 1.5
.

Let
x^{i},y^{i}\in{\mathbf{Q}}\subset\mathbf{R}^{3}
and let
\omega,\xi\in\Omega
.
The expectations of mean values of the fields
{\mathscr{J}}_{i}(x,\omega)

and
{\mathscr{J}}(y,\xi)
are

(A.4)

\displaystyle\bm{\mathcal{M}}_{1}(x)=\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket{\mathscr{J}(x)}\right\rrbracket=\int_{\Omega}{\mathscr{J}(x,\omega)}d\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}(\omega)

(A.5)

\displaystyle\bm{\mathcal{M}}_{1}(y)=\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket{\mathscr{J}(y)}\right\rrbracket=\int_{\Omega}{\mathscr{J}(y,\xi)}d\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}(\xi)

The 2nd-order moment or expectation is

(A.6)

\bm{\mathcal{M}}_{1}(x)\llbracket{\mathscr{J}(x)}\otimes{\mathscr{J}(y)}\rrbracket=\iint_{\Omega}{\mathscr{J}}(x,\omega)\otimes{\mathscr{J}}(y,\xi)d\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}(\omega)d\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}(\xi)

The covariance is then

\displaystyle\mathbb{K}\llbracket{\mathscr{J}(x)}\otimes{\mathscr{J}(y)}\rrbracket=\mathbb{E}\bigg\llbracket({\mathscr{J}(x)}-{\mathscr{J}(y)})\otimes({\mathscr{J}(x)}-{\mathscr{J}(y)})\bigg\rrbracket

(A.7)

\displaystyle=\iint_{\Omega}\bigg(\bm{\mathscr{J}}(x;\omega)-\bm{\mathscr{J}}(y,\xi)\bigg))\otimes\bigg((\bm{\mathscr{J}}(x;\omega)-\bm{\mathscr{J}}(y,\xi)\bigg)d\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}(\omega)d\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}(\xi)

so that

\displaystyle\mathbb{K}\llbracket{\mathscr{J}(x)}\otimes{\mathscr{J}(y)}\rrbracket=\mathbb{E}\bigg\llbracket\widehat{\mathscr{J}(x)}\otimes\widehat{\mathscr{J}(y)}\bigg\rrbracket-\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket{\mathscr{J}}(x)\big\rrbracket\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket{\mathscr{J}}(y)\big\rrbracket

(A.8)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bm{\mathscr{E}}\bigg\llbracket\widehat{\mathscr{J}(x)}\otimes\widehat{\mathscr{J}(y)}\bigg\rrbracket-\bm{\mathcal{M}}_{1}(x)\bm{\mathcal{M}}_{1}(y)

Definition 1.6
.

Given a set of fields
\widehat{\mathscr{J}}_{i_{1}}(x_{1}),...,\widehat{\mathscr{J}}_{i_{n}}(x_{n})
at points
(x_{1}...x_{n})\in{\mathbf{Q}}
then the
n^{th}
-order moments and cumulants are

(A.9)

\mathbb{E}\llbracket\widehat{\mathscr{J}}_{i_{1}}(x_{1})\otimes...\otimes\widehat{\mathscr{J}}_{i_{n}}(x_{n})\rrbracket

(A.10)

\mathbb{K}\llbracket\widehat{\mathscr{J}}_{j_{1}}(x_{1})\otimes...\otimes\widehat{\mathscr{J}}_{j_{n}}(x_{n})\rrbracket

where at second order

(A.11)

\mathbb{K}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)\rrbracket=\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x)\otimes\mathscr{J}(y)\rrbracket-\bm{\mathcal{M}}_{1}(x)\bm{\mathcal{M}}_{1}(y)

A very important class of random fields are the Gaussian random vector fields (GRVFS) which are characterized only by the first and second moments. The GRVFS can also be isotropic, homogenous and stationary. The details will be made more precise but the advantages of GRVFs are briefly enumerated.

(1)

GRVFS have convenient mathematical properties which generally simplify calculations;indeed, many results can only be evaluated using Gaussian fields.

(2)

A GRVF can be classified purely by its first and second moments and high-order moments and cumulants can be ignored.

(3)

Gaussian fields accurately describe many natural stochastic processes including Brownian motion.

(4)

A large superposition of non-Gaussian fields can approach a Gaussian field (Feller 1966.)

For this paper, the following definitions are sufficient for isotropic GRVFS. (More details can be found in
\bm{[24]}
)

Definition 1.7
.

Any GRVF has normal probability density functions. The following always hold:

(1)

The first moment vanishes so that

(A.12)

\bm{\mathcal{M}}_{1}(x)=\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x;\omega)\big\rrbracket=\int_{\Omega}\mathscr{J}(x;\omega)d\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}(\omega)=0

(2)

The covariance then reduces to the ’2-point’ function

\displaystyle\mathbb{K}\llbracket{\mathscr{J}(x)}\otimes{\mathscr{J}(y)}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket\widehat{\mathscr{J}(x)}\otimes\widehat{\mathscr{J}(y)}\big\rrbracket-\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket{\mathscr{J}}(x)\big\rrbracket\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket{\mathscr{J}}(y)\big\rrbracket

(A.13)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket\widehat{\mathscr{J}(x)}\otimes\widehat{\mathscr{J}(y)}\big\rrbracket=\zeta J(x,y;\ell)

where
\ell
is a correlation length such that
J(x,y;\ell)\rightarrow 0
for
\|x-y\|\gg\ell.

(3)

The GRSF is regulated at all points
x
if
J(x,x;\ell)<\infty 1
. Here,
J(x,x;\ell)=1
. For a white-in-space random field or noise

(A.14)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\big\llbracket\widehat{\mathscr{J}}(x)\otimes\widehat{\mathscr{J}}(y)\big\rrbracket=\zeta\delta^{n}(x-y)

which blows up at
x=y
. An example of a regulated 2-point correlation for a GRSF would be a colored-in-space noise of the form

(A.15)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket\widehat{\mathscr{J}}(x)\otimes\widehat{\mathscr{J}}(y)\big\rrbracket=\zeta J(x,y;\ell)=\zeta\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|}{\ell}\right)

Definition 1.8
.

The GRVF is isotropic if
J(x,y;\ell)=J(y,x;\ell)
depends only on the separation
\|x-y\|
and is stationary if
J(x+\delta x,y+\delta y)=J(x,y;\ell)
. Hence, the 2-point function or Greens function is translationally and rotationally invariant
\mathrm{R}^{n}
.

Having established the basic properties of GRSFS on
\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}
, and as a prerequisite to differentiation and integration of random vector fields, the geometric properties are briefly considered in relation to continuity of the sample paths.

Definition 1.9
.

Let
\{x_{\alpha}\subset{\mathbf{Q}}\subset{\mathbf{R}}^{n}
be a sequence of points in a domain
\mathbf{Q}
such that
x_{\alpha}\rightarrow x
as
\alpha\rightarrow\infty
or
\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow\infty}\|x_{\alpha}-x\|=0
. Let
\mathscr{J}(x_{\alpha})
and
\mathscr{J}(x)
be random vector fields at these points. (Not necessarily Gaussian.) Then:

(1)

The random field
\mathscr{J}(x)
has
continuous sample paths
with unit probability in
{\mathbf{Q}}
if for every sequence
\{x_{\alpha}\}\in{\mathbf{Q}}
with
\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow\infty}\|x_{\alpha}-x\|=0
and
\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}[\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow\infty}|\mathscr{J}(x_{\alpha})-\mathscr{J}(x)|=0;x\in{\mathbf{Q}}]=1

Continuous sample paths with unit probability, means that with a probability of one, there are no discontinuities within the entire domain
{\mathbf{Q}}
. This condition is also called sample path continuity.

(2)

The random vector field
\mathscr{J}(x)
has almost surely continuous sample paths if for every sequence
\{x_{\alpha}\}\in\mathbf{Q}
with
\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow\infty}\|x_{\alpha}-x\|=0
so that
\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}[\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow\infty}|\mathscr{J}(x_{\alpha})-\mathscr{J}(x)|=0]=1
. However, there may be
x\in{\mathbf{Q}}
for which the condition does not hold giving discontinuities within
{\mathbf{Q}}
.

(3)

The random vector field
\mathscr{J}(x)
is mean-square continuous in
{\mathbf{Q}}
if for every sequence
\llbracket x_{\alpha}\rrbracket\in{\mathbf{Q}}
with
\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow\infty}\|x_{\alpha}-x\|=0
so that

\mathbb{E}\llbracket\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow\infty}|\mathscr{J}(x_{\alpha})-\mathscr{J}(x)|^{2}\rrbracket=0

More details can be found in
\bm{[24]}
.

Definition 1.10
.

A GRSF
\mathscr{J}(x)
is almost surely continuous at
x\in{\mathbf{Q}}\in{\mathbf{R}}^{n}
if
\mathscr{J}(x+\beta)\longrightarrow\mathscr{J}(x)
as
\beta\rightarrow 0

When this holds for all
x\in{\mathbf{Q}}
then this is known as ’sample function continuity’. The following result gives the sufficient condition for continuous sample paths within a domain
{\mathbf{Q}}

Lemma 1.11
.

Let
\mathscr{J}(x)
be a RVF on
{\mathbf{Q}}\subset{\mathbf{R}}^{n}
. Then if for some
C>0
and
\lambda>0
with
\eta>\lambda

(A.16)

\mathbb{E}\llbracket\big|\mathscr{J}(x+\beta)-\mathscr{J}(x)\big|^{\lambda}\rrbracket\leq\frac{C|\zeta|2n}{|\ln|\beta||^{1+\eta}}

If
\mathscr{J}(x)
is a Gaussian random field then given some
C>0
and some
\epsilon>0

(A.17)

\mathbb{E}\llbracket|\mathscr{J}(x+\beta)-\mathscr{J}(x)|^{2}\rrbracket\leq\frac{C}{\ln|\beta|^{1+\epsilon}}

A.1.
Differentiability and existence of derivatives

Let
\mathscr{J}(x)
be a GRSF, existing for all
x\in{\mathbf{Q}}\subset\bm{\mathrm{R}}^{n}
, with covariance

(A.18)

\mathbb{E}({\mathscr{J}(x)}\otimes{\mathscr{J}(y)})=\mathbb{E}\llbracket{\mathscr{J}(x)}\otimes{\mathscr{J}(y)}\rrbracket-\bm{\mathcal{M}}_{1}(x)\bm{\mathcal{M}}_{2}(y)

where
\bm{\mathcal{M}}_{1}(x)=\mathbb{E}\llbracket{\mathscr{J}(x)}\rrbracket=0
.

Definition 1.12
.

Let
\nabla\mathscr{J}(x)
denote the gradient of a GRF. Let
\mu_{i}
be a unit vector along the
i^{th}
direction such that
\mu_{1}=(1,0,0,0...),\mu_{2}=(0,1,0,0,...)
etc. with
\|\mu_{i}\|=1
. A GRF is differentiable in the mean square sense(MSS) if

(A.19)

\nabla\mathscr{J}(x)=\lim_{|\mathcal{R}|\uparrow 0}|\frac{\mathscr{J}(x+|\mathcal{R}|\mu_{i})-\mathscr{J}(x)}{|\mathcal{R}|}

which implies that

(A.20)

\lim_{\mathcal{R}\uparrow 0}\mathbb{E}\bigg\llbracket\bigg|\frac{\mathscr{J}(x+|\mathcal{R}|\mu_{i})-\mathscr{J}(x)}{|\mathcal{R}|}-\nabla\mathscr{J}(x)|^{2}\bigg|\bigg\rrbracket=0

The Laplacian or second-order partial derivative at
x
is defined as

(A.21)

\nabla_{(x)}\nabla_{(x)}{\mathscr{J}(x)}=\lim_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{J}\uparrow 0}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{R}\mathcal{S}|}\bigg[{\mathscr{J}(x+|\mathcal{R}|\mu_{i}+|\mathcal{J}|\mu_{j})}-{\mathscr{J}(x+|\mathcal{R}|\mu_{i})}-{\mathscr{J}(x+|\mathcal{S}|\mu_{i})}+{\mathscr{J}(x)}\bigg]

An alternative definition, which is probably more useful, is given as follows

Lemma 1.13
.

A GRF is differentiable in the mean-square sense(MSS)with respect to
\mu_{i}
if

(A.22)

\lim_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S}\uparrow 0}\bigg\llbracket\bigg|\frac{\mathscr{J}(x+|\mathcal{R}|\mu_{i})-\mathscr{J}(x)}{|\mathcal{R}|}-\frac{\mathscr{J}(x+|\mathcal{S}|\mu_{i})-\mathscr{J}(x)}{|\mathcal{S}|}\bigg|^{2}\bigg\rrbracket=0

Then a GRF is differentiable if

(1)

\bm{M}_{1}(x)
is differentiable.(For GRFs
\bm{M}_{1}(x)=0
and this condition can be relaxed.)

(2)

The following covariance exists and is finite for all points
x=y
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{K}\llbracket\nabla_{(x)}{\mathscr{J}(x)}\otimes\nabla_{(y)}{\mathscr{J}(y)})\rrbracket=\nabla_{(x)}\nabla_{(y)}\mathbb{K}\llbracket({\mathscr{J}(x)}\otimes{\mathscr{J}(y)})\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\lim_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S}\uparrow 0}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{RS}|}\bigg[\mathbb{K}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x+\mathcal{R}\mu_{i})\otimes{\mathscr{J}(y+\mathcal{S}\mu_{i})}\rrbracket-f\mathbb{K}\llbracket({\mathscr{J}(x)}\otimes{\mathscr{J}(y+\mathcal{S}\mu_{i}))}\rrbracket

\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}\llbracket({\mathscr{J}(x+\mathcal{R}\mu_{i})}\otimes{\mathscr{J}(y))}+\mathbb{K}\llbracket{\mathscr{J}(x)}\otimes\widehat{\mathscr{J})}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\lim_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{R}\uparrow 0}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{RS}|}\bigg[\mathbb{E}\mathscr{J}(x+\mathcal{R}\mu_{i})\otimes\mathscr{J}(y+\mathcal{S}\mu_{i})\bigg\rrbracket-\mathbb{E}({\mathscr{J}(x)}\otimes{\mathscr{J}(y+\mathcal{S}\mu_{i})})

(A.23)

\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}\llbracket{\mathscr{J}(x+\mathcal{R}\mu_{i})}\otimes{\mathscr{J}(y))}\rrbracket+\mathbb{E}\llbracket{(\mathscr{J}(x)}\otimes{\mathscr{J}(y))}\rrbracket<\infty

Remark 1.14
.

Mean square differentiability at all
x\in{\mathbf{Q}}
implies mean square continuity at all
x\in{\mathbf{Q}}
and this is also consistent with Holder and Kolmogorov continuity conditions.

(A.24)

\displaystyle\lim_{a\uparrow 0}\mathbb{E}\bigg\llbracket{\mathscr{J}(x+a\xi_{i})}-{\mathscr{J}(x)|}^{2}\bigg|\bigg\rrbracket=\lim_{a\uparrow 0}(|\mathcal{R}|^{2})\lim_{a\uparrow 0}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{R}|^{2}}\mathbb{E}\bigg\llbracket{\mathscr{J}(x+\mathcal{R}\xi_{i})}-{\mathscr{J}(x)|}^{2}\bigg|\bigg\rrbracket=0

Note that this (and all definitions of the derivative) also requires a regulated random field or noise. Again, if the GRF is white-in-space noise then
\mathbb{E}\llbracket{\mathscr{J}(x)}\otimes{\mathscr{J}(y)}\rrbracket=\delta^{n}(x-y)
and the derivative cannot be defined.

Lemma 1.15
.

The condition for the existence of the derivative
\nabla_{i}{\mathscr{J}(x)}

is also tantamount to the differentiability of the covariance function so that

(A.25)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\llbracket\big[\nabla_{(x)}\mathscr{J}(x)\otimes\nabla_{y}{\mathscr{J}(y)}\rrbracket=\nabla_{(x)}\nabla_{(y)}\mathbb{K}\llbracket{\mathscr{J}(x)}\otimes{\mathscr{J}(y)}\rrbracket

exist for all
x=y
. Again, the GRF is differentiable only if it is not a white noise. Note that for
x=y

\displaystyle\bm{\mathcal{K}}\llbracket\big[\nabla_{(x)}\mathscr{J}(x)\otimes\nabla_{x}{\mathscr{J}(x)}\rrbracket=\nabla_{(x)}\nabla_{(x)}\bm{\mathcal{K}}\llbracket{\mathscr{J}(x)}\otimes{\mathscr{J}(x)}\rrbracket

(A.26)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{(x)}\nabla_{(y)}\zeta J(x,y;\ell)=0

Lemma 1.16
.

The expectation is
\mathbb{E}\llbracket\nabla{\mathscr{J}(x)}\rrbracket=\nabla\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x)\rrbracket=0
since

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg\llbracket\lim_{\mathcal{R}\uparrow 0}\frac{{\mathscr{J}(x+|\mathcal{R}|\mu_{i})}-{\mathscr{J}(x)}}{|\mathcal{R}|}\bigg\rrbracket

(A.27)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\mathcal{R}\uparrow 0}\frac{1}{\mathcal{R}}\mathbb{E}\bigg\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x+\mathcal{R}\mu_{i})\bigg\rrbracket-\mathbb{E}\bigg\llbracket{\mathscr{J}(x)}\bigg\rrbracket=\nabla\mathbb{E}\bigg\llbracket{\mathscr{J}(x)}=0\bigg\rrbracket

A.2.
Stochastic Integration of GRSFs

The integral of a GRSF is defined as the limit of a Riemann sum of the field over the partition of a domain.

Proposition 1.17
.

Let
{\mathbf{Q}}\subset{\mathbf{R}}^{n}
be a (closed) domain with boundary
\partial{\mathbf{Q}}
and
x=(x_{1},...,x_{n})\subset{\mathbf{Q}}
. Let
{\mathbf{Q}}=\bigcup_{q=1}^{M}{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}
be a partition of
{\mathbf{Q}}
with
{\mathbf{Q}}_{q}\bigcap{\mathbf{Q}}_{q}=\varnothing
if
p\neq q
. Let
x^{(q)}\in{\mathbf{Q}}_{q}
for all
q=1...M
. Note
x^{(q)}\equiv(x_{1}^{(q)},...x_{n}^{(q)})
. Then
x^{(1)}\in{\mathbf{Q}}_{1},x^{(2)}\in{\mathbf{Q}}_{2},...,x^{(M)}\in{\mathbf{Q}}
. Let
\zeta({\mathbf{Q}}_{q})
be the volume of the partition
{\mathbf{Q}}_{q}
so that
\zeta({\mathbf{Q}})=\sum_{q}^{M}\zeta(\bm{\mathbf{Q}})
. Similarly, if
\partial{\mathbf{Q}}
is the surface or boundary of
\partial{\mathbf{Q}}
then let

(A.28)

{\mathbf{Q}}=\bigcup_{q=1}^{M}\partial{\mathbf{Q}}_{q}

be a partition of
\partial{\mathbf{Q}}
with
\partial{\mathbf{Q}}_{q}\bigcap\partial{\mathbf{Q}}_{q}=\varnothing
if
p\neq q
. Let
x^{(q)}\in\partial{\mathbf{Q}}_{q}
for all
q=1...M
. Note
x^{(q)}\equiv(x_{1}^{(q)},...x_{n}^{(q)})
. Then
x^{(1)}\in\partial{\mathbf{Q}}_{1},x^{(2)}\in\partial{\mathbf{Q}}_{2},...,x^{(M)}\in\partial{\mathbf{Q}}
. Let
\|{\mathbf{Q}}_{q}\|\equiv\mu(\partial{\mathbf{Q}}_{q})
be the surface area of the partition
\partial{\mathbf{Q}}_{q}
so that
\mu(\partial{\mathbf{Q}})=\bigcap_{q=1}^{M}A(\partial{\mathbf{Q}}_{q}
. The total volume and area of
{\mathbf{Q}}
is

(A.29)

\displaystyle v(\bm{\mathbf{Q}})=\sum_{q=1}^{M}({\mathbf{Q}}_{q})=\sum_{q=1}^{M}v({\mathbf{Q}}_{q})

(A.30)

\displaystyle area(\partial{\mathbf{Q}})=\sum_{q=1}^{M}area(\partial{\mathbf{Q}}_{q})=\sum_{q=1}^{M}area(\partial{\mathbf{Q}}_{q})

Given the probability triplet
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}})
then a Gaussian random field on
{\mathbf{Q}}
for all
x\in\bm{\mathbf{Q}}
is
{\mathscr{J}}:\omega\times{\mathbf{Q}}\rightarrow{\mathbf{R}}
and
{\mathscr{J}(x^{q},\omega)}\in{\mathbf{Q}}_{q}
exists for all
x^{(q)}\in{\mathbf{Q}}_{q}
and
\omega\in\Omega
. The stochastic volume
integral and the stochastic surface integral are

(A.31)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\mathscr{J}{(x;\omega)}d\mu_{n}(x)=\lim_{all~v({\mathbf{Q}}_{q})\uparrow 0}\sum_{q=1}^{M}{\mathscr{J}(x^{(q)};\omega)}v({\mathbf{Q}}_{q})

(A.32)

\displaystyle\int_{\partial{\mathbf{Q}}}{\mathscr{J}(x;\omega)}d\mu_{n-1}(x)=\lim_{all~area(\partial{\mathbf{Q}}_{q})\uparrow 0}\sum_{q=1}^{M}{\mathscr{J}(x^{(q)};\omega)}area(\partial{\mathbf{Q}}_{q})

When a Gaussian random field is integrated, it is the limit of a linear combination of Gaussian random variables/fields so it is again Gaussian.

Next, the stochastic expectations or averages are defined.

Proposition 1.18
.

Since

(A.33)

\mathbb{E}\llbracket\bullet\rrbracket=\int_{\Omega}(\bullet)d\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}(\omega)

the expectation of the volume integral is as follows.

(A.34)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg\llbracket\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\mathscr{J}(x;\omega)d\mu_{n}(x)\bigg\rrbracket=\lim_{all~v(\bm{\mathbf{Q}}_{q})\uparrow 0}\mathbb{E}\bigg\llbracket\sum_{q=1}^{M}\mathscr{J}(x^{(q)};\omega)v({\mathbf{Q}}_{q})\bigg\rrbracket

or

(A.35)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg\llbracket\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}{\mathscr{J}(x;\omega)}d\mu_{n}(x)\bigg\rrbracket\equiv\int\!\!\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}{\mathscr{J}(x;\omega)}d\mu_{n}(x)d\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}(\omega)

(A.36)

\displaystyle=\lim_{M\uparrow\infty}\lim_{all~v({\mathbf{Q}}_{q})\uparrow 0}\int_{\Omega}\sum_{q=1}^{M}{\mathscr{J}(x^{(q)};\omega)}v({\mathbf{Q}}_{q})d\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}(\omega)=0

which vanishes for GRSFs since
\mathbb{E}\bigg\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x^{(q)})\bigg\rrbracket=0
. Similarly, for the stochastic surface integrals

(A.37)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}{\mathscr{J}(x;\omega)}d^{n-1}x\right\rrbracket=\lim_{all~area({\mathbf{Q}}_{q})\uparrow 0}\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\sum_{q=1}^{M}{\mathscr{J}(x^{(q)};\omega)}A(\partial{\mathbf{Q}}_{q})\right\rrbracket

or

(A.38)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\llbracket\int_{\partial{\mathbf{Q}}}{\mathscr{J}(x;\omega)}d\mu_{n-1}(x)\right\rrbracket\equiv\int_{\Omega}\int_{\partial{\mathbf{Q}}}{\mathscr{J}(x;\omega)}d^{n-1}xd\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}(\omega)

(A.39)

\displaystyle=\lim_{all~\mu(\partial{\mathbf{Q}}_{q})\uparrow 0}\int_{\Omega}\sum_{q=1}^{M}{\mathscr{J}(x^{(q)};\omega)}area(\partial{\mathbf{Q}}_{q})d\bm{\mathrm{I\!P}}(\omega)=0

Given an integral (or summation) over a random field or stochastic process, the Fubini theorem states that the expectation of the integral or sum over a random field is equivalent to the integral or sum of the expectation of the field.

Theorem 1.19
.

Let
\mathscr{J}(x)
be a random field not necessarily Gaussian, existing for all
x\in{\mathbf{Q}}
with expectation
\mathbb{E}\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x)\rrbracket
, not necessarily zero. Then

(A.40)

\mathbb{E}\bigg\llbracket\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\mathscr{J}(x)d\mu(x)\bigg\rrbracket\equiv\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket\mathscr{J}(x)\big\rrbracket d\mu_{n}(x)

For a set of N random fields
\mathscr{J}_{q}(x)

(A.41)

\mathbb{E}\bigg\llbracket\sum_{q=1}^{N}\mathscr{J}_{q}(x)\bigg\rrbracket=\sum_{q=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket\mathscr{J}_{q}(x)\big\rrbracket

It is also possible to define a ’mollifier’ or convolution integral.

Proposition 1.20
.

Let
(x,y)\in{\mathbf{Q}}
and let
k(x-y)
be a smooth function of
(x,y)
that will depend on the separation
\|x-y\|
. Given the GRSF
{\mathscr{J}(y)}
define the volume and surface integral convolutions

(A.42)

\displaystyle{\mathscr{J}(x)}=k(x-y)*{\mathscr{J}(y)}\equiv\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}k(x-y)\otimes{\mathscr{J}(y)}d\mu_{n}(y),~(x,y)\in\bm{\mathbf{Q}}

(A.43)

\displaystyle{\mathscr{J}(x)}=k(x-y)*{\mathscr{J}(y)}\equiv\int_{\bm{\mathbf{Q}}}k(x-y)\otimes{\mathscr{J}(y)}d\mu_{n-1}(y),(x,y)\in\partial{\mathbf{Q}}

For example, if
k(x-y)
is a Gaussian function then the random field
{\mathscr{J}(x)}
can be ’Gaussian smoothed’ on a scale
\ell
.

(A.44)

\displaystyle\mathscr{J}(x)=k(x-y)*{\mathscr{J}(y)}\equiv C\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{\ell^{2}}\right)\otimes{\mathscr{J}(y)}d\mu_{n}(y),~(x,y)\in{\mathbf{Q}}

(A.45)

\displaystyle\mathscr{J}(x)=k(x-y)*{\mathscr{J}(y)}\equiv C\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{\ell^{2}}\right)\otimes{\mathscr{J}(y)}d\mu_{n-1}(y),(x,y)\in\partial{\mathbf{Q}}

For the heat kernel
h(x-y,t)
the convolution give a time-dependent GRSF that solves the randomly perturbed heat equation
\square\mathscr{J}(x,t)=0
.

(A.46)

\displaystyle\mathscr{J}(x,t)=h(x-y,t)*{\mathscr{J}(y)}\equiv\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{4t}\right)\otimes{\mathscr{J}(y)}d\mu_{n}(y),~(x,y)\in{\mathbf{Q}}

The result is extended to include GRSFs
{\mathscr{J}(x)}
.

Proposition 1.21
.

Let
{\mathscr{J}(x)}
be a regulated GRSF defined for all
x\in\bm{\mathcal{D}}
such that
\mathbb{E}\llbracket{\mathscr{J}(x)}\rrbracket=0
and

(A.47)

\mathbb{E}\bigg\llbracket\big|{\mathscr{J}(x)}\big|^{\ell}\bigg\rrbracket=\frac{1}{2}[\zeta^{\ell/2}+(-1)^{\ell}\zeta^{\ell/2}]

so that
\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket\big|{\mathscr{J}(x)}\big|^{\ell}\big\rrbracket=0
for all odd p. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg\llbracket\bigg|\int_{{\mathbf{Q}}}{\mathscr{J}(x)}d\mu(x)\bigg|^{\ell}\bigg\rrbracket

\displaystyle~\leq\frac{1}{2}C[\zeta^{\ell/2}+(-1)^{\ell}\zeta^{\ell/2}]\bigg|\int_{\bm{\mathbf{Q}}}d\mu(x)\bigg|^{\ell}

(A.48)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}[\alpha^{\ell/2}+(-1)^{\ell}\alpha^{\ell/2}]\|\bm{\mathbf{Q}}\|^{\ell}

where
\|{\mathbf{Q}}\|
is the volume of
{\mathbf{Q}}
.

Proof.

Follows immediately from Fubini Thm.
∎
